* Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross- references to annexes, including project and programme details, methodology, etc.

## A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximating 1.5 pages)

The proposed project, "Public–Private-Partnerships for Ecology-Sound Agriculture and Resilient Livelihood in Northern Tanzania," aims to address the vulnerability of smallholder farmers and local communities in the NTSB by increasing their access to growing premium market segments while using their improved agricultural practices to enhance ecosystem services.

### The Northern Tanga Sub-Basin (NTSB)

The NTSB is one of Tanzania's most important agricultural regions. A fifth of the country's total maize production comes from this region. The NTSB has some of the highest poverty rates in the country and has experienced severe environmental degradation. The anticipated impacts of climate change will compound these challenges, leading to further declines in productivity and livelihoods.

### Project Objectives

The project is expected to significantly impact traditional rice production, relying on rainfed systems, with limited irrigation infrastructure and water availability. It aims to increase the area under rice cultivation by 10% over five years. The NTSB is a high-value and niche crop region for organic rice, including organic rice varieties that have been developed in the region. These crops are currently produced at small scales but have potential for further expansion.

### Project Segments

These segments include:

- **Smallholder farmers**: Limited access to finance, technologies, market information, and extension services.
- **Local value chain actors**: Lack of access to finance, technologies, and market information.
- **Market actors**: Limited access to finance, technologies, capacity development, and enabling conditions.

### Challenges

These challenges have also been highlighted by a recent assessment of COVID-19 impact on the agriculture sector in Tanzania. The pandemic has exacerbated existing constraints for smallholder farmers, including limited internet of things (IoT) for capacity development and improved market access. Increased demand for domestic food products has led to increased competition with imported goods, particularly for vegetables and fruits.

### Project Impact

The project will deliver the following three interdependent outcomes to address these challenges:

1. **Farmers' ability**: Farmers will be able to manage climate impacts and reduce risks.
2. **Adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers and their local value chain actors**, particularly vulnerable forms of agriculture, including women and youth, will increase through improved access to finance, technologies, and sustainable production and processing.
3. **Regional food security**: The project will build capacities for climate-resilient agricultural investment, cross-sectoral coordination for increased PSPPs and smallholder financing, and climate-informed investment support are strengthened.

The PECD project will deliver the following three interdependent outcomes to address these challenges:

1. **Farmers' ability**: Farmers will be able to manage climate impacts and reduce risks.
2. **Adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers and their local value chain actors**, particularly vulnerable forms of agriculture, including women and youth, will increase through improved access to finance, technologies, and sustainable production and processing.
3. **Regional food security**: The project will build capacities for climate-resilient agricultural investment, cross-sectoral coordination for increased PSPPs and smallholder financing, and climate-informed investment support are strengthened.


---
**Page 7**
---

These outcomes will achieve the project goal that of the project increases the capacity of smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors to access and use national and regional support mechanisms in the GTF to generate and apply crop-specific agrometeorological information to reduce climate risks and impacts, and adopt market-informed climate-resilient and sustainable agricultural practices. The project will also increase the capacity of smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors to be able to place this climate-vulnerable and agriculture-dependent region on a sustainable development pathway through increased access to markets and improved livelihoods. By the end of the project, it is expected that 100% of participating smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors will have developed the necessary means and capacity to take advantage of market opportunities in the agricultural sector, and adopt crop-specific climate-resilient and sustainable practices for their agricultural production and livelihoods sustainably.

The project will leverage increased private and public investments for building the climate change resilience of smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors (ca. 5% of Cambodia's population) in NT3B and benefit approximately 400,000 farmers and other local value chain actors (ca. 10% of Cambodia's population).

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## I. Background Information

### A. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### 1. Ecological Context

- The proposed GCF project, "Public-Social-Private Partnerships for Ecologically-Sound Agriculture and Resilient Livelihood in Northern Tone Sap Basin (NT3B)," aims to enhance climate change resilience of smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors in NT3B through the development of a public-private-partnership model that will promote sustainable agriculture through a value-chain approach built on effective public-social-private partnerships.

#### 2. Cambodia remains one of the few Least Developed Countries (LDCs) left in Asia (UNDESA, 2018). The country has nonetheless made steady economic development progress in 2015. Cambodia's gross national income (GNI) per capita was US$743 in 2019, up from $600 in 2018 and $500 in 2017. This growth is largely attributed to favorable macroeconomic policies and developments (i.e., increased lending, investments, and exports in the agricultural sector) and a stable political environment. The country has also been able to maintain its poverty rate at around 34% since 2009 (World Bank, 2018; Suthy and Vuthy, 2020). Among the main crops produced in Cambodia, rice is the staple and single most important crop. Rice production has increased from approximately 5 million tons in 2007 to over 6 million tons in 2019 (MARD, 2015; MAFR, 2017). The opposite trend can be observed for other crops such as cassava and maize. However, the country also faces the cost of environmental degradation, mainly through deforestation to make way for crop production.

#### 3. Despite the overall growth and expansion of the agriculture sector, smallholder farmers in rural areas have notably struggled to benefit from this progress. The agricultural sector accounts for approximately 20% of Cambodia's GDP (World Bank, 2018). However, only about 45% of the country’s labor force is directly engaged in agriculture, and over 60% of which is found in rural areas where poverty rates are significantly higher than those in urban areas. The majority of smallholder farmers in Cambodia live below the poverty line (World Bank, 2018). Smallholder farmers have been particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts as they lack access to resources and services that would allow them to adapt their livelihoods. This rural agrarian population plays an essential role in the country's economy; persistent poverty makes many farming households unable to meet basic needs such as food, shelter, education, and healthcare (MARD, 2015). Environmental degradation resulting from rapid agricultural expansion has also exacerbated the effects of these extreme weather events on smallholder farmers.

#### 4. The vulnerability of farmers and farming households in the increased climate variable poses a possible threat to the country's sustainable development as it affects not only the country’s main economic engine but also its food security, which is already under pressure due to population growth and urbanization (MARD, 2015). The COVID-19 pandemic has further exacerbated this situation. The restrictions on the movement of people and goods, has also elucidated the critical linkages between the environment and human well-being in rural areas. This has been particularly evident for smallholder farmers who are socially excluded minorities (MAFF, CARD, and FAO, 2020). This pandemic has also underscored the need to address these issues by strengthening the resilience of smallholder farmers through sustainable relationships between farmers and agricultural markets to cope with both economic and natural shocks.

#### B. Agriculture in NT3B and Underlying Vulnerabilities

- NT3B is defined as the areas north of the Tone Sap Lake, including Oddar Meanchey, Kampong Thom, Preah Vihear, and Ratanakiri Provinces (see Figure 1). The project area encompasses a total land area of 2.5 million hectares (ha) with evergreen and deciduous forests, covering hilly areas


---
**Page 8**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 5

In the northwestern foothills of the central highlands in the middle (mainly for rice production) and flooded forests and grassland areas along the Tonle Sap Lake in the south (see Annex 16 maps). The average annual rainfall in the region varies from 1,400 to 2,500 mm. The region is characterized by a high diversity of agroecological zones with rich biodiversity, vital sources of water for rice production and livelihoods in the region (Deug et al., 2019). Over 20% of the country's total area is under forest cover, which provides important ecosystem services such as carbon sequestration (Deug et al., 2019). The region has also been identified as one of the most vulnerable regions in the country to floods and droughts, which are expected to occur more frequently with increased intensity due to climate change (Rahman et al., 2018; ADB, 2016).

The production of cashew, mango, aromatic rice, and vegetables has expanded in recent decades in Cambodia (Deug et al., 2019). The NTSB is the largest producer of cashew nuts in the country. In 2015, it accounted for approximately 20% higher than the national average in Kampong Thom and Preah Vihear, and similarly, for Oddar Meanchey (Deug et al., 2019). The NTSB is also a major producer of mangoes, with an area under cultivation of aromatic rice, cashews, mango and vegetables has increased both nationally and in the NTSB between 2013 and 2019. However, while the production areas for cashew nuts and mangoes have decreased nationally, the NTSB has expanded the production areas in the same period.

Aromatic rice, cashew, mango and vegetables have emerged as alternative agricultural commodities and are now being produced under improved agricultural practices such as organic certification, the CamboDai Good Agricultural Practices (CamGAP), Geographical Indications (GI) and other quality standards. The NTSB has been a leader in this regard with 10% of its total area under organic certification (Burn et al., 2018; IECOM, 2020). For instance, although the yield for aromatic rice in Preah Vihear was 20% lower than the national average and compared to other NTSB regions, this indicates the province's prevailing trend towards higher quality production. The NTSB has also been a leader in the production of mangoes with its production accounted for 12% of the province's total rice production (Burn et al., 2018). The price premium for organic aromatic rice is estimated to be between $3 and $5 per kg.

Segments of agriculture in the NTSB present considerable sustainable expansion and diversification opportunities for smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors (Burn et al., 2018). These crops have also been identified as potential export products. However, there are several challenges that need to be addressed by smallholder farmers in the NTSB are unable to take advantage of these opportunities and practice low-value and low-quality production practices. This section provides an overview of such opportunities and challenges associated with these emerging segments of agriculture in the NTSB.

## Production of short-duration annuals rice (long grain rice segment), shifting from longer duration non-annuals rice to more productive and climate-resilient crops, is anticipated in favor for farmers in these remote hilly areas.

### Organic rice in Preak Valley is certified in the US EU markets. The organic certification is a key driver of market access.
* Topography keeps paddy fields small and fragmented with poor connectivity to markets.
* Limited mechanization
* Limited access to finance and quality supplies and inputs for farmers
* Limited uptake of AGPs, PAs and FAs to provide climate-resilient crops and practices
* Lack of irrigation infrastructure
* Lack of IRRM to ensure water quality and availability at the farm gate
* Limited integration of agrometeorological and market information services into farming practices

### Low vulnerability semi-arid and dry-forest areas:
* Several champion farmers' networks with expanding membership in these regions.
* Growing demand for food and sustainably produced vegetal products.
* Involvement of innovators (early resistor proponent for climate-resilient crops and practices).
* Limited access to finance, technology, and knowledge
* Lack of market access
* Weak regulatory conditions and enforcement capacity to denk such partnerships. These underlying socioeconomic vulnerabilities are exacerbated by the adverse impacts of climate change, making them the most vulnerable population groups in Cambodia (Ria et al., 2015a).

## III. Climate Baseline: Drought, Flooding Crop Pest and Disease

### Temperature
* Analysis of observed historical data shows annual mean temperature anomaly has increased by 0.8 degrees Celsius since 1950 with a rate of 0.03°C per year (Thounon, 2015). The rate of change is most rapid in the dry season (December, January and February) and followed by the spring season (March, April and May) and slowest in the wet season.
* FAO's analysis of bias-corrected data from the Coordinated Regional Downscaling Experiment (CORDEX), based on CMIP5 global climate projections and RCP scenarios shows notable changes in maximum and minimum temperatures for the period 2016-2045 compared to historical baseline (1986–2005) and future period (2070-2100 MME (FAO, 2022). Maximum temperature is expected to increase consistently from the historical baseline (1.3°C to 2.3°C for RCP4.5 compared to 1.8°C to 3.6°C) and future period (2.7°C to 3.9°C for RCP4.5, up to 3.3°C to 3.6°C by the far future period.
* The number of days with temperature >=+3°C is expected to increase in the medium and far future, with large increases observed over the southern regions (Ria et al., 2015b). This trend is more pronounced for RCP4.5 than RCP8.5. Higher in the southwestern and northeastern (i.e., NEST) regions of the country and will be exacerbated into the future.

* Champion farmers, who are mainly women, have extensive experience in farming and mastered the knowledge and skills, and diffused to their other farmers.

Precipitation

14 Analysis of historical precipitation observations (Figure 2) highlights significant interannual and intra-seasonal variability in precipitation during all seasonal periods throughout the year. Historical data show general decreasing trends for most seasons, with the exception of the dry season from November to March.

Figure 3: Seasonal mean precipitation over the period 1981–2014 (top left), December-February (top right), March-May (not shown), June-August (bottom left), July through August (bottom center bottom right), September (bottom right), and October-November (bottom left, red indicating regression lines, and blue indicating mean value).

15. The rainy season, which lasts from May to early October, accounts for 90% of annual precipitation in the MTSR. The dry season, from November to April, brings drier and cooler air from November to March, and then hotter air in April and early May. The maximum mean temperature in Cambodia is about 28 °C and the minimum mean temperatures in December–January are

16 Allocations from the Climate Information Platform based on CORDEX South Asia Ensemble Mean suggest an overall increase in annual mean precipitation in the medium to far future. FAO's downscaled analysis from the same model suggests a decrease in annual mean precipitation for most of the region, with an upward trend in rainfall between June and August in the northwest, and a downward trend in the northeast of the country. The projected change in annual cumulative precipitation (1980-2005) shows an increase in the medium to far future. Precipitation deviation from the historical average baseline (1980–2005) shows an increase in the near future, with significant decreases in annual cumulative precipitation in the medium and far future, with variation geographically.

17 Many agricultural producers rely on rain-fed production in the wet season, and FAO's downscaled analysis of the CORDEX South Asia Ensemble Mean suggests a decrease in the number of days with heavy precipitation over the coastal regions, to a higher degree under RCP2.6 and with changes up to +500 mm/year in the NTSS. At the same time, there is an increase in the number of days with heavy precipitation for most of the region. This will have impacts on the near and medium-term future, up to 9 days, and in the longer term by fewer days (5–6 days) compared to the baseline period. The projected change in annual cumulative precipitation shows a decrease in the medium to far future. Precipitation suggests that rainfall events will be more intense and infrequent, and the rainy season will become shorter (Figure 17).

Drought, flooding and crop pest and diseases

18 Drought in Cambodia occurs regularly and is driven by extended dry periods and the level of water resources in the terrestrial water bodies. The frequency of drought varies from province to province with many parts of the NTSS significantly affected according to the drought vulnerability index (See et al., 2015a).

## 20 The major rice-growing areas of the Mekong-Tone Sap basin are exposed to flooding every year but this is not necessarily a problem for farmers as it provides fertile soil for rice cultivation. Generally, floods in Cambodia are not entirely seasonal and can occur at any time during the year. Floods are also affected by the hydrology in the entire Mekong River Basin. In recent years, the flood levels of the Tonle Sap Lake have risen many deep-water rice crops (10–25 ha) and flooded 37% of the total cultivated area (0.5 million ha) of wet season rice has been affected by flooding. When flooding is accompanied by temporary flooding occurs, floods result in severe damage to crops such as those that are water-seeded and most upland crops, including legumes.

## 21 From 2000 to 2019, on average, 4.1% and 5.2% of the total cultivated area was affected by drought and flood in the four NTSB provinces, respectively. The percentage of damaged areas due to drought is higher than that for floods (Figure 6). It can be seen that the percentage of damaged areas was higher for flooding compared to drought, indicating that flooding poses a major challenge for production, especially for rice and cashew crops also relevant for rice and cashew production in Kampung Thom province, and also for vegetable production in Siem Reap.

## 22 Another critical challenge observed is the occurrence of pests and diseases and value chain actors, through a review of the feasibility study. As part of this project, the occurrence of pests and diseases has been identified as one of the major challenges to rice production. Changes in climatic drivers. Flooding also leads to an increase in pest and disease incidence. The most common pests are the green leafhopper, green leafhopper, grasshoppers, whitefly, mealybug, case worm, armyworm and rats. The prolonged flooding period is likely to increase significantly the occurrence of rice blast, brown spot, and cashew diseases also reported in the feasibility study. The occurrence of severe droughts and floods has led to a major challenge for production, especially for rice and cashew crops.

## 23 Droughts and pests and diseases are the most common types of shocks in the NTSP (NIS, 2019). Farming households have experienced moderate to severe food shortages due to crop loss and damage on an annual basis (NIS, 2015). Between 37 and 57 % of the households claimed that they often experienced these shocks consecutively, thus having little time to make

## Anticipated Climate Impacts on Key Crops in NTB5 and Market Opportunities

### Table: Key Crop Areas with Their Climate Exposure and Impacts in the NTB5

| Key Crop Area/Group | Climate Exposure and Impacts |
|---|---|
| **Kampong Thom** (Paddy rice, cassava) | *Production (yield):* - Flood losses. - Heat stress above 30°C: All organic rice farmers are particularly vulnerable to the delayed onset of winter and a cold dry period at the end of the rainy season. - Strong winds and storms: Damage to trees and forests. - Heavy rain: Can be very impactful for heavy rainfall during cassava's flowering season, farmers face no significant issues as the flowering period is short. *Affect Quality yield:* - Decreased yield due to high humidity at flowering, spotted nuts, kernel deformation, bacterial attack, and fungal disease. - Heavy rains cause the spread of root and rhizome diseases, pests, and diseases during flowering and fruiting. *Post-harvest Losses:* - Increased mold, fungal attacks, damage and loss during transport without proper storage and processing capacity |

### Vegetables
* Snap Bean: High seasonality of production (from November to January, early dry seasons), heavily dependent on natural resources and therefore vulnerable to climate change.
* Droughts - combined with limited or no irrigation systems can pose risks to yield failures;
* Pest/diseases - e.g. flea beetles, cutworm, leaf folder, armyworm, aphid, lady beetle, snail, butterfly, root rot, bacteria wilt;
* Floods and river flow fluctuations
* Waterlogging
* Vegetation degradation
* Reduced soil fertility due to low phosphorus content.
* Improper timing for harvesting resulting in over or under maturity; and
* Nutrient leaching.

### Post-harvest Losses
* High temperatures due to lack of cold storage infrastructure, capacity of products, rapid drying.


## At the same time, the increased public awareness of healthy eating and food safety in the country and the growing international tourism market drive consumer demand for higher-quality agricultural products (Nguyen, 2019). Premium prices are paid by consumers for these products. This has led to a shift from low-value crops such as rice to high-value crops in the NTSB, offering opportunities for smallholder farmers, who are particularly vulnerable to the impact of climate change due to their limited access to resources and technologies. The transition towards higher-value crops is challenging for them: the adoption of climate-resilient and higher-value practices and technologies to increase their adaptive capacity requires a significant investment.

Furthermore, with the observed increase in drought conditions and unpredictable wet seasons, many rice farmers in the NTSB have shifted from long-duration varieties to shorter duration fragment varieties to reduce their exposure to the risk of yield losses. This shift has led to an increase in the production of premium quality rice for domestic consumption and international market demand for fragrant rice and associated market opportunities. This example demonstrates the importance of identifying climate risks and opportunities, as well as the need for a transition strategy that supports them. This makes it essential for the project to identify climate risks and shifts in crop suitability over the short, medium, and long term, and to develop an appropriate transition strategy with smallholder farmers, extension officers, researchers, and financial enablers to expedite the transition. The transition must also be sustainable and equitable.


## In addition to the climate exposure and impacts on these crops, the sector is responsible for 97.5 % of greenhouse gas emissions (27 t CO2e/ha). According to the Biennial Update Report (BUR) (2020a), the sector has been identified as a priority area in the country’s NDCs. The main sources of these emissions are: livestock expansion, crop residue and biomass burning, fertilizer use, animal waste, and wetting and drying rivers. However, the agricultural sector is also one of the most vulnerable sectors to climate change impacts (e.g., increased CO2, CH4, and NOx at the Tier 1 methodology level from the 2006 Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) guidelines). Therefore, it is currently not possible to provide accurate emissions baselines for CO2, CH4, and N2O (t/ha).

## B. V. Baseline Information: Complementarity and Coherence (See Annex 2 for further details)

### 31. Justifying the specific approach under this proposed PEARL project in the NTSB, commercialization, mechanization, interlinkification, and infrastructural improvement efforts are also crucial; especially in the lowland areas where climate change adaptation is already a major challenge. In addition to these efforts, there is an array of agricultural enhancement and climate change adaptation options are necessary to increase the adaptive capacity of farmers.

### 32. From this perspective, the efforts under the PEARL project must coordinate with other regional and national initiatives supporting agricultural adaptations from various angles. Table 3 shows recently launched and planned voluntary carbon projects in the region that aim at increasing the resilience of smallholder farming systems to land management and food security. Their geographical and strategic linkages will make these initiatives highly complementary to the PEARL project. The table also lists other relevant national and regional climate change initiatives currently under development (i.e., ASPIRE-AT and EU-funded deforestation-free agriculture projects), the PEARL project must coordinate with them in order to avoid duplication of efforts, maximize synergies, and operational linkages. Such linkages will ensure the project's sustainability, replicability, and scale-up potential to other regions and countries. The table also lists other relevant national and regional climate change initiatives and others (see Annex 2 for a detailed map of baseline investments) to ensure its additionality and complementarity in relation with these initiatives.

### 33. Across these initiatives, the importance of rice production as the country's main staple will continue. For rice and other crops, increasing their production capacity while addressing their climate vulnerability is critical. The need for better management practices, improved infrastructure, and access to markets, including cold chain storage and processing facilities are all fundamental enablers that these initiatives work together to provide. Having a diverse portfolio of complementary projects in the region will ensure that the PEARL project complements and ensures that a wide range of adaptive options is available to the sector to meet its specific needs and priorities.

### Project Activities

### Outcomes

- **Outcome 1**: Improved agricultural productivity and livelihoods of smallholder farmers through the adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices.
- **Outcome 2**: Enhanced market access for smallholder farmers, leading to increased income and improved livelihoods.
- **Outcome 3**: Strengthened institutional capacity in the value chain actors to support smallholder farmers.

### Enablers

- **Enabler 1**: Access to climate information services.
- **Enabler 2**: Availability of appropriate technologies for climate-smart agriculture.
- **Enabler 3**: Financial incentives and enabling environment for smallholder farmers.

## Goal Statement (VI)

35. If the project increases the capacity of smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors and institutional support mechanisms in the NTBB to generate and apply crop-specific agrometeorological information to reduce climate risk, then smallholder farmers will be able to adopt appropriate climate-smart agricultural practices and technologies through improved PSPPS. THEN Cambodia will be able to place this climate-vulnerable and poverty-stricken region on a path of sustainable development by increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change impacts, improving their livelihoods, and enabling them to access markets for their produce.

This effort will include developing innovative partnerships between government agencies, civil society organizations (CSOs), private sector actors (e.g., youth, women, and other socially excluded minorities) and business and financial literacy and providing tools to make better decisions in the face of climate change. ## VIII. Barriers

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 20

## Barriers to Smallholder Farmers' Climate Resilience and High-Value Agriculture in the NTBSP

### Barrier 1: Lack of reliable and crop-specific agrometeorological forecasting and related advisory services to support climate-resilient and high-value agricultural practices.

The lack of access to reliable and crop-specific agrometeorological forecasting and related advisory services has hampered progress towards swiftly adapting their farming practices to the changes in the seasonality of rainfall, temperature, and other climatic variables. This barrier is particularly acute for smallholder farmers (FAO, 2012). With international support, Cambodia has developed a network of hydrometeorological stations to provide guidance on climate change adaptation strategies. However, there remains a significant gap in the provision and dissemination of agrometeorological forecasting and advisory services, including the last-mile delivery through extension services.

### Barrier 2: Limited awareness of climate risks and climate-resilient and high-value options promoted through extension services, social media platforms, and mobile apps

The limited awareness of climate risks and climate-resilient and high-value options, promoted through extension services, social media platforms, and mobile apps, is another barrier attributed to the weak institutional and cross-sectoral coordination. Smallholder farmers, particularly women and youth, who are often socially excluded from adopting alternative production techniques and technologies that are climate-resilient, sustainable, and high-value, have continued to rely on traditional farming practices such as rainfed agriculture, subsistence cropping, increased use of chemical fertilizers and pesticides, and poor soil management. These practices have continued to be promoted by extension services, which often lack the necessary resources and knowledge to target smallholder farmers or meet their required needs.

### Barrier 3: Limited capacity and financial and technological access for smallholder farmers and other small-scale agricultural producers to adopt climate-resilient and high-value agricultural practices

Limited market knowledge about commercial gains among smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors, along with the adoption of new technologies and techniques poses a related challenge (MATTI, 2013; Kula, Turner and Suri, 2015). Smallholder farmers are often excluded from accessing these markets for these actors, as they lack the necessary resources and knowledge to target such markets or meet the required production standards. This is particularly true in the case of small-scale agricultural producers who are often considered too risky by banks and other financial institutions.

### Barrier 4: Limited contract capacity and market-based knowledge of agricultural extension providers

Limited contract capacity and market-based knowledge of agricultural extension providers to support the necessary enhanced change and shift in agricultural practices.

## Barriers to Effective PS&D and Leveraging Public and Private Investment for Climate-resilient, High-value and Inclusive Agriculture

### Barrier 5: Weak IFM/IFN to Ensure Good Agroclimatic Conditions and Connectivity and Ecological Functions

#### 5.1 Definition of Forest and forest degradation due to rapid agricultural expansion for monoculture plantations through large scale agro-industrial Economic Land Concessions (ELCs) and destruction of land titles under Social Land Concessions (SLCs), particularly along waterways (Forest Trends, 2015; Sasaki et al., 2016; RCG, 2017; MFD, 2020). The sub-optimal environmental conditions for agroforestry and tree-based systems in the region are exacerbated by soil degradation due to depleted soil nutrients and the supply and quality of water and increased natural disaster risks from soil erosion and sedimentation. These factors combined with climate change impacts such as droughts, floods, and heatwaves tend to compound these disaster risks to cause more localized flooding and landslides to reduce agricultural yields and product quality. The lack of agroforestry systems also results in the loss of biodiversity and ecosystem services that support the development of livelihood options, and WRM in critical catchment areas, among farmers and members of CPAs and CBOs. This barrier is further exacerbated by the conversion of natural forests to plantations for palm oil, rubber, cashew or mango orchards with intercropping to enhance agroecological functions and benefits (Bematchi et al., 2018; Bongers & van Noordwijk, 2015).

### Barrier 6: Limited regulatory and institutional capacities for establishing effective PS&Ds and leveraging public and private investment for climate-resilient, high-value and sustainable agriculture

#### 6.1 The lack of favorable regulatory conditions and institutional arrangements is another barrier to ensure enabling conditions for the development of climate-resilient, high-value and inclusive agriculture. The regulatory compliance costs borne by individual farmers, cooperatives, associations, unions and other local value chain actors are often prohibitive in terms of time, money, and resources. These vary across countries in the region, and procedures and requirements need to be streamlined to make more accessible (FCD, 2015; Sasaki et al., 2016). The regulatory compliance costs borne by individual farmers, cooperatives, associations, unions and other local value chain actors are often prohibitive in terms of time, money, and resources. These vary across countries in the region, and procedures and requirements need to be streamlined to make more accessible (FCD, 2015; Sasaki et al., 2016). #### IX.Risks and Assumptions

There are six risks and several assumptions, as seen in Figure 6. Section F provides a complete description of the risk categories and associated measures. The risks involved in the project are categorized as technical and operational, reputational, financial, and other. The technical and operational risks include: 1) Rapid changes in market trends; 2) Unfavorable weather conditions; 3) Inadequate institutional arrangements for PS&Ds and WRM; 4) Procurement delays in operationalizing the FARM arrangements. As described in Section F., a range of adaptive measures are proposed to mitigate these risks.

#### IX.Risks and Assumptions

The regulatory risk concerns the potentially harmful social and environmental impacts identified under the Social and Environmental Management Framework (SEMF) (see Annex B) and Gender Action Plan (Annex B). Specific mitigation measures are described in these annexes and briefly summarized in Section G.

## Key Assumptions and Mitigation Measures

67 There are two risk associated with financial irregularities, and this risk will be addressed by implementing the necessary checks and balances for financial regulation to address this issue (see Section F). Another risk relates to the consistency of the project’s assumptions. This risk will be mitigated through a thorough review of the assumptions that take stock of lessons learned and best practices from the current pandemic and apply relevant measures to minimize the risk.

68 Key assumptions include effective coordination between relevant ministries, sub-national governments, and private actors, continued and increased public investment for systemic, institutional, and individual capacity development to support the project’s activities. The project will also assume that there is sufficient demand for the products of the private sector. NGOs, farmers, and other local value chain actors. This partly rests on the assumption that the current market prices are sustainable in the long term and that the project will be able to sell its products at competitive prices. This product will continue. Also, the project beneficiaries’ willingness to adopt new and alternative technologies and practices is assumed. The project assumes that there is sufficient demand for the products of the private sector. A better understanding of risks and benefits, ensured through extensive hands-on support programs and awareness-raising activities, will help mitigate this risk. These assumptions are also discussed as part of the risk identification and mitigation measures in Section F below.

69 The project will focus its investment exclusively on generating adaptation results.

70 Meanwhile, the project will produce a co-benefit in climate change mitigation through improved soil and residue management, postharvest quality control, and processing to reduce food waste and critical catchment forest protection and restoration. This co-benefit is expected to be quantified as an emission reduction of 125 tCO2e per hectare from the 2006 IPCC guidelines, except for CO2 emissions and removals from REDD+ (at the Tier 2 level). Given the low carbon intensity of the project’s activities, this co-benefit is expected to be quantified as an emission reduction at the Tier 1 methodology level (e.g., if 8 trees planted, ha. of forest restored, ha. of rice field under enhanced soil management practices, ha. of land under improved livestock management). The project will also produce a co-benefit in terms of chemical fertilizer use and postharvest loss. The project will coordinate with the National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS) to ensure that the project’s activities are aligned with the NFMS and other national forest monitoring systems, as well as with the ongoing FAGGNET initiative “Capacity-building Initiative for Transparency (CBIT)”175, which is currently working on developing a methodology for quantifying co-benefits in the context of REDD+ at the Tier 2 level. The project will also work closely with the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry to ensure that its activities are aligned with the agriculture sector. Furthermore, the project will establish an operational link with a new FAGGNET project “Scaling Up Forest Carbon Management in Indonesia” (FSCM)176, which is currently working on developing methodologies for sustainable forest management efforts and animal waste management, including activities related to emissions mitigation and adaptation. The alignment of these two projects’ co-benefits and emission reduction initiatives will ensure that this co-benefit in climate change mitigation makes a tangible and cost-effective contribution.

The United Sales Generalizes through the Simulation Program has been also collaborating with the CBIT project to support the General Directorate for Agroforestry (DGA) in developing methodologies for quantifying co-benefits in the context of REDD+ at the Tier 2 level. The collaboration is aimed at improving the quality data for several priority crops and brings in the Royal University of Agriculture to increase national capacity for emission data collection.

#### B.3 Project/programme description (max 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

### General Approach

The PEARL project builds on the Royal Government of Cambodia (RGC)'s vision for the NTB's climate-adapted agriculture that is high-value, and sustainable based on the logic described above to provide a range of options for smallholder farmers. The project will focus on the development of a climate-resilient value chain in the NTB region.

The PEARL project will enhance the climate resilience of smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors, including women, by focusing on the following three pillars: climate resilience, high-value agriculture, and enabling policy and institutional environment. The approach is based on the concept that food safety performance and sustainable value chain development to trigger transformational change.

The following outcomes, outputs and activities are designed to build on mutually dependent relationships between the three fundamental pillars - climate resilience, high-value agriculture, and an enabling policy and institutional environment. The approach will be implemented through a focus on women's empowerment in vegetable production under this project.

## XI. Narrative Descriptions of Expected Outcomes, Outputs and Activities (See Annex 2 for further details)

### EXPECTED OUTCOME ONE: FARMERS' CAPACITIES ARE ENHANCED TO MANAGE CLIMATE IMPACTS AND RELATED RISKS

75 This outcome will provide farmers and local communities with access to balanced and crop-specific agrometeorological advisory services, including climate risk information, early warning systems of climate risks among farmers and other local value chain actors to enhance their adaptive capacity. The framework will identify gaps in needs and vulnerabilities concerning their agricultural production, processing, storage, marketing, and consumption. Agrometeorological information will foster complementary service delivery by public and private sector partners, including the use of mobile apps, and incorporate climate risk information into crop calendars, crop production, and harvest preparation, pest management, and post-harvest handling. The framework systematically strengthens the capacity of agricultural value chains to access agrometeorological advisory services from both public and private sectors and develop effective public and private partnerships to ensure the effective dissemination of agrometeorological advisory information through increased and extended outreach capacities.

76 Climate services involve the production, translation, transfer, and use of climate knowledge and information for different stakeholders. The framework will provide a platform for the development and implementation of innovative (bi)technologies for data collection, processing, and dissemination (e.g., use of mobile apps). Key attributes for effective climate service delivery include: (i) the ability to generate actionable climate services; (ii) the capacity to disseminate actionable products; and limited engagement with other actors involved in agricultural value chains (private and public).

77 The framework will proceed as follows from FAO's Global Outlook on Climate Services in Agriculture (FAO, 2021), highlighting the importance of building a strong foundation for climate services delivery at both national and local context. It is important to highlight that gaps at any stage of the framework will jeopardize efforts to develop services and ensure their effective use by farmers and other value chain actors. The framework will therefore be strengthened to ensure that the last mile barrier is overcome, and that information can lead to strategic agricultural decision-making.

78 Cambodia has undertaken various initiatives to increase agrometeorological service capacity through multiple projects over the last decade. These efforts include building weather and agriculture infrastructure and improving agrometeorological services in collaboration with the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), Asian Development Bank (ADB), the United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction (UNDRR), World Meteorological Organization (WMO), Global Climate Observing System (GCOS), Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO), Global Facility for Disaster Reduction and Recovery (GFDRR), Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN), Sub-Committee on Meteorology, Climatology and Hydrology (SCMCH), Government of Cambodia (GoC), Japan International Cooperation Agency (JICA) and Japan Meteorological Agency (JMA).

79 However, as shown in Table 6 below, it is evident that in Cambodia, current efforts and investments are focused on the early stages of the framework, i.e., the generation and production of information products with far fewer investments are focused toward the later stages of the framework, or the "last mile", which focuses on the tailoring, dissemination and uptake of climate services for farmers.

## Activity 1.2.2: Mobilize SCPs for the production and dissemination of agrometeorological advisory services and data sharing networks and architecture, targeting cassava, maize, rice, and vegetables through a variety of mechanisms.

### Description:
Crop-specific SCPs will allow farmers to grow, harvest, store the target crops and other local value chain actors to anticipate yield losses due to climate change. The SCPs will provide information on crop suitability for different locations and varieties in response to climate change. The SCPs will ensure coordination among public and private extension services and provide highly tailored information through a variety of mechanisms including mobile phones, radio, SMS, and the internet. The SCPs will be developed as part of the TAWG project and the University of Queensland and WMO project, to provide highly tailored information. The SCPs will consider strategies for scaling up the SCPs.

### Subactivity 1.2.2.1: Mobilize the TAWG with additional stakeholders in developing the SCPs for the production and dissemination of crop-specific agrometeorological advisory information.
- Activities:
    - Develop a framework for SCP development based on humanized data management and sharing agreements under the SCP with defined dedicated information dissemination and outreach methods (e.g., web portal hosted by MAFF, mobile apps (i.e. Tapa App launched in 2018), SMS, radio, etc.)
    - Mobilize additional stakeholders to develop the SCPs for cassava, maize, rice, and vegetables through the Department of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (DAFF), Provincial Department of Water Resources and Meteorology (PDWRM), Provincial Department of Environment (PDOE), National Agricultural Research Institute (NARI), University of Queensland, World Meteorological Organization (WMO), CGIAR, and private extension agencies.
    - Mobilize additional stakeholders to develop the SCPs for cassava, maize, rice, and vegetables through the Department of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (DAFF), Provincial Department of Water Resources and Meteorology (PDWRM), Provincial Department of Environment (PDOE), National Agricultural Research Institute (NARI), University of Queensland, World Meteorological Organization (WMO), CGIAR, and private extension agencies, providing including NGOs on the SCPs.

### Subactivity 1.2.2.3: Mobilize additional stakeholders in developing and implementing SCPs for cassava, maize, rice, and vegetables in farm management and value addition activities to support decision-making and reduce smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors' vulnerabilities to climate change.
- Activities:
    - Increased awareness of available services and their application among smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors will effectively reduce the climate vulnerability in agricultural production and processing. Increased service demand and user feedback will also create opportunities for scaling up SCPs through public-private partnerships (PPPs).
    - Subactivity 1.2.3.1: Prepare and roll out training (TDT) through NGOs to increase awareness of advisory services and promote the application of agrometeorological advisory services among smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors.
    - Subactivity 1.2.3.2: Mobilize additional stakeholders in developing and implementing SCPs for cassava, maize, rice, and vegetables through linking members of targeted ACA, FPA, PQA, CPA, and agricultural unions with their respective TDT/SCP representative.

## Output 2.1: Premium market access opportunities for cashew, mango, organic rice, and vegetable producers and processors increased through climate-resilient sustainable agriculture certification programs

### 56. The project will build on the experiences of IRAM (2014), IFC/CCM (2015) and other ongoing certification programs and climate-resilience value chains identified and examined the economic, social, and ecological opportunities and challenges associated with these initiatives. In addition to the lessons learned from these projects, the project will engage key stakeholder consultations (Annex 7), the project targets four crops: 1) Rice in Vang Vihear; 2) Mango in Oudomxay Province; 3) Cashew in Kampong Thom and parts of Preah Vihear; and 4) Organic rice and vegetables in Kampong Thom, promoting climate-resilient and sustainable agriculture through certification programs in the NTSB. As stated earlier, these initiatives will be implemented as part of a broader strategy for expanding organic rice production capacity.

### 57. The agricultural certification programs that were examined in these studies include Geographical Indications (GI), Organic Certification, Cambodian Good Agricultural Practices (CAGP), Hazelnut Analysis Critical Control Point (HACCP), and the International Rice Federation (IRF) Sustainable Rice Platform (SRP). The organic rice certifications employed under this project will be made at the project inception based on a crop-specific strategy developed in consultation with key stakeholders.

### 58. Rice production in Preah Vihear remains relatively traditional, which has enabled the development of a significant organic rice sector mainly through the PMUCG, which supports 20 ACs more than 1,000 farmers. A sizable production base is now established and supported by the PMUCG, which has been certified under the Organic Agriculture Certification System (OAC) and EU and US/NOP certified in 2017. The PMUCG delivered more than 10,000 tons of organic rice to two buyers in 2018 and is expected to deliver over 35,000 tons by 2020. Project support will further strengthen the PMUCG in expanding this organic rice production capacity.

### 59. Contract farming has proven itself to be an effective tool for farmers and producers, giving the traditional smallholder farmer access to a stable market through larger farm enterprises like AMU Rice/Rice, where an intermediary like the PMUCG is involved. The project will support the development of contract farming in Preah Vihear, which guarantees their access to a premium price market while the intermediary like the PMUCG increases their collective bargaining power and provides technical assistance for quality control and marketing. Project support will also help establish and support the establishment and capacity strengthening of crop-specific unions to promote this win-win scenario through contract farming.

### 60. Among several certification options, GI offers a unique opportunity for organic rice production in Preah Vihear, where the potential for expanding its organic rice production capacity is somewhat limited, to increase their premium market access opportunities. The project will support the development of a GI for organic rice in Preah Vihear through efforts and investment, based on the lessons learned from other GI development in the country. The PEARL project document provides an overview of the potential for developing a GI for organic rice in Preah Vihear. The project will ensure that this expansion in production capacity does not lead to further agricultural expansion to cause deforestation or environmental degradation and will support the establishment and expansion of number of farmers producing organic rice based on market incentives and capacity support.

### 61. Cashew in Kampong Thom and some parts of Preah Vihear: Due to the rising regional demand for cashew nuts, there is a growing interest among farmers in cashew production in Kampong Thom. The leading producer of cashew in the country, the total cashew production area in the province was 54,089 ha (of which 21,376 ha were under cultivation) and the number of farmers engaged in cashew production was more than 20,000 in 2019 (NIS, 2019).

### 62. Most Kampong Thom and Preah Vihear farmers cultivate M27 (B53) in Kampong Thom). The "local variety" (local name: "Kampong Thom") is the most popular cashew variety locally replaced by M27 (a lesser degree, 1908, 9416, and M27) because it gives a higher yield, between 1 to 2 metric tons of raw cashews per hectare. The local variety has a high yield but is more susceptible to diseases and insect pests. The incidence of pest and disease is mainly due to prolonged droughts and increased temperatures. The cultivation of this variety thus often goes hand in hand with an

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 29

## Introduction of production methods and increased use of chemical inputs, suggesting the need for increased efforts in IPM.

### Value-addition processes and market access

53 Despite the recent market expansion, cashew production in Cambodia is not geared toward value addition through processing or export. Cashew producers are mostly smallholders. Also, cocoa farmers are more common. Most cashew farmers often sell their harvests to collectors on a first-come-first-served basis. Most collectors/ processors do not have any contracts with the product quality. This practice keeps the quality and price of RCN low.

### Mango production in Oddar Meanchey

54 Mango production in Oddar Meanchey has been expanding rapidly due to high prices of mangoes with increasing demand from China, Thailand, Vietnam, and others. Mango production in Oddar Meanchey is split between commercial and smallholder farmers. The majority of the mangoes are sold fresh on the domestic market; however, there is a need for improved value addition through processing or export.

55 In 2017, there were more than 7,000 hectares of mango orchards in the province, with an estimated production capacity to reach 3.6 million tons by 2020 according to DOAMF. The production capacity was estimated to be three times that of the next five years (Blum et al., 2018).

56 The most prevalent variety of mango under cultivation in the province is Kaey Limet, which is in high demand domestically and internationally. This variety is also widely used for cross pollination with other varieties.

57 Most mango producers in Oddar Meanchey are not currently organized into legally recognized cooperatives, although the prevalence of cooperatives is growing. Smallholder mango farmers in the province typically sell their produce to middlemen or traders who then sell it on to processors or exporters. The majority of mangoes cross into Thailand are often processed and sold as Thai products.

58 GAP certification of mango in Oddar Meanchey has the potential to reduce production costs by cutting down the expenses associated with chemical inputs, such as pesticides and fertilizers. This can be achieved through reducing exposure to chemicals. This is consistent with the overall GAP certification trend in the country. Quality assurance and GAP certification are currently not widely practiced among mango producers compared to other provinces.

59 Oddar Meanchey is among the driest and most drought-prone areas of Cambodia. Although heat stress can have a negative impact on yield, it also has positive effects by reducing the effects of extreme heat and drought, making them comparatively climate-resilient compared to other crops. These geographical and climatic conditions allow for the production of high-quality mangoes that are in demand both domestically and internationally.

60 Planting mango and cashew trees on fields previously used for annual crops such as cassava can have an emissions removal impact. The Peafly project will also prevent the expansion of these orchards into existing forests, thereby preserving biodiversity.

### Leafy vegetable production in Siem Reap

61 Leafy vegetable production in Siem Reap is vital for farmers, particularly smallholders and female farmers. Due to the lack of infrastructure, most leafy vegetables are sold fresh on a daily basis at local markets. The main early season crops farmed are Chinese kale and lettuce; others include green garlic, cabbage, spinach (NIS, 2019).

#### Activities, Descriptions and Sub-Activities

**Activity 2.1.5: Develop and operationalize climate-resilient value chain development and activities for premium-value crops**

* **Description:** Include gender-responsive value chain development roadmaps, linking key value chain actors, will establish strategic partnerships with SMEs and other actors to develop a business plan for the transformation of selected climate-resilient sustainable agri-foods. Each roadmap will assess and identify appropriate certification programs as essential vehicles for accessing premium-value markets. The project will also build direct partnerships with SMEs and other actors to develop the necessary tools and resources to ensure that the chosen certifications consider climate risks and impacts adequately.

**Sub-Activity 2.1.5.1: Develop and operationalize value chain development roadmaps for climate-resilient, inclusive and gender-responsive premium-value crop development**

* **Description:** Include gender-responsive value chain development roadmaps, linking key value chain actors, will establish strategic partnerships with SMEs and other actors to develop a business plan for the transformation of selected climate-resilient sustainable agri-foods. Each roadmap will assess and identify appropriate certification programs as essential vehicles for accessing premium-value markets. The project will also build direct partnerships with SMEs and other actors to develop the necessary tools and resources to ensure that the chosen certifications consider climate risks and impacts adequately.

The project will engage with these certification programs for the development of market-based instruments to promote higher-value agricultural products through a value chain approach.

### Output 2.2: Access to technologies for climate-resilient agriculture and value chain development improved among smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors, particularly women farmers and value chain actors.
- The loss of agricultural infrastructure and value-added capacity in the region results in a substantial loss of competitiveness for farmers and other value chain partners, including producers and traders. Hence limiting local employment and business development opportunities. For instance, because of their lack of proper postharvest storage and processing capacities, smallholder farmers are unable to store or process their produce effectively, leading to spoilage and losses in the markets.
- These practices keep their agricultural production low quality and low value, thus limiting their long-term competitiveness and profitability.

#### Subactivity 2.2.1: Improve access of smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors to services and information creates a lack of awareness and understanding of market trends and opportunities. Limited knowledge about the latest technologies and innovations in agriculture can also hinder the adoption of new practices, leading to inefficiencies and reduced productivity.

- One such objective is the observed shift towards increased use of climate change induced plant pest and disease which has affected smallholder farmers' access to markets. Therefore, removing production and processing technological and infrastructure capability and capacity building for smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors in transitioning towards climate-resilient and sustainable agriculture practices.


---
**Page 35**
---

10 To do so, the PEARL project will establish a climate adaptation asset acquisition mechanism, the Farmer-led Agroforestry Mechanism (FARM), to assist forest associations, agribusinesses and other small-scale local value chain actors who would otherwise not have access to the necessary finance to adopt climate-resilient and high-value agricultural practices. The FARM will provide loans for the purchase of assets such as solar water pumps, drip irrigation systems, solar-powered cold storage facilities, pack houses, smart high-efficiency irrigation systems, and energy-efficient value-added processing equipment. Financial improvements by farmer associations and other small-scale local value chain actors are also expected to benefit the project through improving the quality and quantity of supply capacity and services and public sector spending in related enabling conditions.

10 The FARM will support up to 124 legally registered agricultural unions, AICs, FAs, and PGIs, including CPAs and CGI. These groups will be supported by a range of climate-resilient assets that are designed for smallholder farmers and climate-resilient business plans developed under output 2.1 to transition to climate-resilient and high-value agricultural practices. The FARM will provide loans for the purchase of assets such as solar water pumps, drip irrigation systems using gender-responsive, inclusive and equitable processes and will include operation and maintenance plans and support for the development of business plans that are designed to improve financial performance through the use of solar-powered cold storage facilities, pack house, smart high efficiency irrigation systems, and energy-efficient value-added processing equipment. Financial improvements by farmer associations and other small-scale local value chain actors are also expected to benefit the project through improving the quality and quantity of supply capacity and services and public sector spending in related enabling conditions.

The selection criteria for beneficiary groups include 1) Financial management capacity (e.g., membership-based savings and credit associations, cooperatives, etc.), 2) Technical capacity (e.g., literacy, equality (e.g., governance arrangements), and 3) Viability and impact potential of their business plans.

10 The FARM builds on best practices and lessons learned through the past and ongoing initiatives in the sector (see Annex 2 for a list of baseline investment projects). One of such initiatives is the Tonde Sap Poverty Reduction and Smallholder Development Project (TS3D®), implemented by the ADB and PAG. The project supports ID Poor farmers in establishing membership-based savings schemes through commercial banks to provide individual members with access to credit, insurance, and other financial services. In a line of credit arrangement, the bank provides loans for the purchase of livestock and agricultural inputs. TS3D® has shown that the establishment of ownership of livelihood improvement actions among the beneficiaries, while the social support network has helped in the implementation of these small-scale projects. The Tonde Sap experience has informed the design of the FARM.

A more detailed description of the TS3D’s experience is provided in Annex 2.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V3.0 | Page 13

## Box 1: Reporting Farm Experience of Tons Sap Poverty Reduction and Smallholder Development (TS2D)

### Target Group
- Poor farmers in 7 provinces, including Kangyang Thon and Phash Vihear.
- Beneficiaries are smallholder farmers who represent two-thirds of the beneficiaries use funds for chicken/rooster raising (quality) instead of other quick income generation opportunities.

### Target Area
- Livelihood Improvement Groups (LIGs)
    - Each LIG comprises 20 to 35 LID members.
    - First phase (2015–2019): 196 communes and 27 districts supported, with 1,241 Livelihood Improvement Groups (LIGs) established.

### Initial Capital Investment
- Total investment: $8.2 million (US$).
- Funds were supplied, and a total of US$10 million was released for the first phase operational.

### Intermediary for Money Control
- LIGs receive funds through NCID via dedicated accounts and manage fund disbursements and repayments from LIGs.
    - An individual member of an LIG is eligible to borrow up to $240 at a time.
    - An applicant must develop a business plan, including repayment plan.

### LIG Activities
- LIGs provide training in financial management, savings, and assistance.
- TS2D provides capacity development support to LIGs and their members and assists in establishing individual bank accounts to receive funds. 

### Requirements for Borrowing
- Registration durations are determined based on income cycle (e.g., four, six months, nine months for handicrafts).
- 50% of the interest collected paid back to LIGs to establish group savings accounts.
- Repayment is done in installments over a period of time.

### PFP efforts
- Staff members supporting farmers through business planning and bookkeeping within each LIG created social cohesion among members, which has been very successful.
- Use of ICT was crucial for successful implementation. All transactions are done through mobile apps - bank transfers are often not really necessary.
- LIGs have also been able to save more than the loan amount available from the NCID would have been much more significant.

### Activities under Output 2.2 (See Section E.1)
The FARM will be implemented in three stages as a graduation program (see Figure B). Beneficiaries will group themselves into LIGs and progress through each stage of this process to reach the next stage of the program.
- The first stage begins under Output 2.1 to develop crop-specific, climate-resilient production and processing technologies and practices that are adapted for local conditions in collaboration with the National Commune Development and Investment Plans (CDIPs) and CIPs to contribute to climate resilience building at the local level.
- The second stage will focus on capacity development of LIGs through training and technical support services to the beneficiary groups during this stage. Each business plan will include growth strategies using a variety of technologies, including climate-resilient production practices and processing techniques that are adapted for local conditions.
- The third stage is the final stage where each LIG will be able to access credit from financial institutions and includes access to private financial capital and insurance services. The project will set up a business plan review committee at the commune level (see Table 8) and district level (see Table B).

# Farmer-led Agriculture Resilience Mechanism (FARM)

A graduated climate adaptation asset acquisition mechanism for ACLs, FAs, PGs, CIFRs, CFs and unions (registered businesses) that promotes savings for future investment for climate-resilient and high-value agriculture.

## Stage 1: Business Plan

### Development and approval of project proposal
- Project description (Project Title)
- Description of the proposed asset(s) (e.g., ACL, FAL, FA, PG, CIFR, CF)
- Description of the proposed business plan (Stage 1)
- Description of the proposed development services (Stage 1)
- Description of the proposed project implementation (Stage 1)
- Approved by the AE

### Parallel technical support throughout Stage 1
- Development and approval of project proposal
- Project description (Project Title)
- Description of the proposed asset(s) (e.g., ACL, FAL, FA, PG, CIFR, CF)
- Description of the proposed business plan (Stage 1)
- Description of the proposed development services (Stage 1)
- Description of the proposed project implementation (Stage 1)

## Stage 2: Fund Initial Asset Acquisition

### Procurement of relevant production inputs
- Procurement of relevant production inputs (e.g., seeds, fertilizers, pesticides) to be used in the asset(s) and/or for the development services.
- Procurement of relevant infrastructure assets (e.g., ACL, FAL, FA, PG, CIFR, CF)
- Procurement of relevant equipment and technology
- Procurement of relevant project implementation services

### Parallel technical support throughout Stage 2
- Procurement of relevant production inputs
- Procurement of relevant infrastructure assets (e.g., ACL, FAL, FA, PG, CIFR, CF)
- Procurement of relevant equipment and technology
- Procurement of relevant project implementation services

## Stage 3: Saving and Finance for Future Assets

### Parallel technical support throughout Stage 3
- Procurement of relevant production inputs
- Procurement of relevant infrastructure assets (e.g., ACL, FAL, FA, PG, CIFR, CF)
- Procurement of relevant equipment and technology
- Procurement of relevant project implementation services

112 In the second stage, following the compilation and acceptance of the business plans, the project, in consultation with each beneficiary group, will identify a technology(ies) or infrastructure asset(s) that is/are essential for operating the proposed asset(s). The beneficiary group will then determine the level of financial contribution towards the proposed asset(s) and select vendors and procure the chosen technology(ies)/infrastructure assets for the project. This contribution can take the form of voluntary contributions from the beneficiaries (e.g., 5 to 20% of the total value) or non-financial contributions (e.g., labor for site preparation). The beneficiary group may provide a non-financial contribution towards the purchase of the asset(s), which will be used as seed-funds in the beneficiary group’s trust fund account. This contribution can also take the form of voluntary contributions from the beneficiaries and/or non-financial contributions (e.g., labor for site preparation) towards completion. The level of minimum funds to facilitate the initial financial contribution will be negotiated taking into account the total value of the asset(s), the number of beneficiaries, and the type of technology(ies)/infrastructure assets.

Each Business plan, assessed by Review Committee:

* Present business graduation strategy and action plan, including milestones (in 3 phases)

* Technical and financial monitoring plans and reporting procedures
* In-kind uplift upfront financial contribution commitment and/or in-kind uplift upfront non-financial contribution commitment
* Terms and conditions of asset transfer and fund disbursement
* Asset maintenance and operation plans, including financial sustainability
* Regular financial contribution level and schedule based on the business plan (e.g., annual, biannual), the annual business activities of the beneficiary group and the project's progress towards achieving its objectives
* Capacity, human cost, etc.
* Project proposal (e.g. a gender-balanced approach to project design, implementation, monitoring procedures, a dispute resolution mechanism, stakeholder engagement, reporting responsibilities, and post-project arrangements)

**GC instruments for asset acquisition and transfer**

* Grant funds manual (developed at inception)
* Financial management guidelines
* Technical standards and procedures
* In-kind uplift upfront in-kind (e.g., labor) contribution commitment and/or in-kind upfront non-financial contribution commitment
* Asset transfer agreement, including terms and conditions to prevent any third party from acquiring the asset(s), or not on the level business group's terms and schedule
* Customized documentation/standardized criteria per beneficiary group
* Banished business plan progress reporting by each beneficiary group at least once every 6 months (e.g., board meeting minutes and tank statements)

**Terms and arrangements of Trust Fund account**

* List of project's recommended microfinance banks and/or commercial banks
* Trust account per beneficiary group (CGR or CGF)
* Financial management guidelines
* Technical standards and procedures
* In-kind uplift upfront in-kind (e.g., labor) contribution commitment and/or in-kind upfront non-financial contribution commitment
* Asset transfer agreement, including terms and conditions to prevent any third party from acquiring the asset(s), or not on the level business group's terms and schedule
* Customized documentation/standardized criteria per beneficiary group
* Banished business plan progress reporting by each beneficiary group at least once every 6 months (e.g., board meeting minutes and tank statements)

113 Each beneficiary group will develop and cover the cost of maintenance and operation and insurance through their membership fees or revenue generated through the implementation of their business plan. As part of its asset-based approach, the project will provide a financial contribution to each beneficiary group based on the proportion of the assets (i.e., a proportion of the revenues generated through their business plan implementation) to increase the beneficiaries' adaptive capacity and reduce vulnerability. The financial contribution will be provided at the discretion level based on crop-specific harvest cycles (e.g., biannual, annual), the business activities of the beneficiary group and the project's progress towards achieving its objectives.

114 At least 50% of each beneficiary group's total financial contribution to the project will be used by each beneficiary group to be able to make a regular contribution of approximately US$30 annually or based on an estimated annual revenue generated from their business plan implementation. The remaining amount will be used for repayment level with the MFIs in Cambodia and the project financial impact of the project demonstrated in Annex 3.

115 At least 75% of each beneficiary group's total financial contribution to the project will be used by each beneficiary group to make the annual contribution. The project expects to contribute to a yearly household income increase of US$200 per year for each beneficiary group. This is based on the assumption that the business plan implementation at operation level or financial contribution level would make the mechanism unviable for the beneficiary groups as it would further reduce their adaptive capacity and reduce vulnerability.

116 In the third stage, the project will assist all beneficiary groups in establishing a trust fund account with a local commercial microfinance bank (e.g., ACLEDA, AMK - see Table 5). The account will be established using a commercial

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal 3.0.3 | Page 14

banking products and standard services. Most commercial microfinance banks in Cambodia offer a trust account service which charges US $5 for an annual service fee for an account holding up to US $5,000 and requires a board of trustees or directors to be established by the bank. The GCF will provide funding to establish a trust fund account with appropriate financial institution to establish the trust fund account. A minimum requirement for selecting a financial institution is that it has a sound governance structure (e.g., independent audit committee, code of conduct) and liquidity risk management (2017) as well as the new Banking and Financial Institutions’ Code of Conduct (2022). The GCF will provide funding to establish a trust fund account with an appropriate financial institution. A board of trustees, comprising of beneficiary group members (gender balanced) will be established to oversee fund development and management. The board will also be responsible for the oversight of the project team and will assist the board in developing post-project governance arrangements.
