# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 17

## Description:
The project will support the climate adaptation asset acquisition mechanism. FARM will be established to support the operationalization of the action plans/businesses plans developed by a total of 104 ACL, FAG, PFA, CPAs, and CFCs, and agricultural unions under Activity 3.1.1. This will be achieved through the following sub-activities:

### Sub-activity 2.1.1: Train ACLs, FAGs, PCAs, CPAs, and unions of cooperatives annually to develop financial and business literacy and entrepreneurial skills; with particular focus on youth, women, and other socially excluded minorities for preparing and updating business plans.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2: Procure an initial lot of agricultural assets based on the approved business plans to operationalize FARM (average purchase price per asset is US$500). The procurement will be done through a competitive bidding process, however, as aggregate demand or a group of cooperatives/associations may access a larger amount by pooling funds.

### Activity 2.2.1: Establish the FARM mechanism to provide financial and business support services for ACLs, FAGs, PCAs, CPAs, and unions of cooperatives annually through clearly defined governance arrangements and ongoing business development support (e.g., FARM grants manual, Board of trustees meetings, etc.).

### Activity 2.2.2: Assess the feasibility of developing additional risk financing options for cassava, mango, and vegetable producers, particularly in vulnerable areas.

## Description:
A feasibility study will be conducted to assess risk financing opportunities for cassava, mango, and vegetable producers to lessen their financial risks due to climate-induced crop loss and damage. Concurrently, the lack of insurance products for supporting agricultural activities is a major challenge that hinders farmers’ access to credit facilities and other financial services. This project aims to ensure business continuity in assisting individual farmers. The article builds on the existing agricultural (index-based) insurance products for rice and dairy farming.

### Sub-activity 2.2.1: Train POGATs, IPAGs, and IFDC staff; district administration officers, commune and village extension agents, and NGOs through TOT programs on the costs of benefits of index-based and other insurance products to raise awareness.

### Activity 2.3.1: Develop a database containing information about cassava, mango, vegetable producers, and their respective geographical information for cassava, mango, and vegetable production and explore the possibility of developing and piloting prototype index-based insurance products in these areas.

## Output 2.3:
Awareness of available financial support services and products in Cassava systems continuously among smallholder farmers and other stakeholders.

### Description:
A comprehensive list of various financial institutions as well as types of financial tools and risk financing options will be made available for ACLs, FAGs, PCAs, CPAs, unions, and their individual members to ensure the beneficiaries have the information necessary to make informed decisions about accessing these services.

### Sub-activity 2.3.1: Compile and regularly update a menu of financial support and insurance products and services available for ACLs, FAGs, PCAs, CPAs, and agricultural unions and other individual members in NTPs at per FPTSS cumulation and demonstration activities under Output 2.1.

### Output 2.3:
Awareness and knowledge of climate-resilient and sustainable, high-value agriculture increased among farmers and other local value chain actors, particularly women farmers and value chain actors.

## 110 To counter the main climate hazards affecting the NTBP, including flooding, drought, pest and diseases, and crop damage, a number of climate-smart agricultural practices are being promoted. These practices include the use of organic fertilizers, which generally support climate-resilient practices such as the utilization of organic pesticides that can also be combined with other inputs. The promotion phase should be supported by early warning systems to monitor the impact of flooding and pest death.

## 120 These stakeholder inputs were analyzed together with the existing best practices and lessons learned documented in Annexes 3, 4, and 5. This analysis was used as a basis for developing the climate adaptation action plan under Output 2. The analysis considered climate risks and impacts at several critical stages of the value chain cycle for the target crops.

## Table 12.1:
Table 9 shows the crop-specific adaptive measures, which are further described in Annexes 2 and the climate rationale study (FAC, 2022). Under this output, the PEARL project will promote these measures in conjunction with the development of a climate adaptation action plan under Output 2.1. The implementation phase should be supported by the full operationalization of these measures demonstrated under this output will be fully operationalized as part of business plans under Output 2.1 and financed by the FARM under Output 2.2.

## Activities Under Output 2.3 (See Section E.1)

### Activity 2.3.1: Develop a clearinghouse, consolidating existing knowledge systems for harmonized knowledge management and systematic collection of lessons learned and best practices from all past and ongoing initiatives by promoting climate-resilient, high-value, and sustainable agriculture practices and technologies.

#### Description:
A consolidated and improved clearinghouse will systematically collect and disseminate relevant lessons learned and best practices from various sources to ensure institutional knowledge management for supporting systems progress. The centralized system will be accessible through a variety of mediums, including social media platforms and mobile apps (e.g., Tumpe App, Agri-Chamkar and Ecostakeholder) for on-demand information access among smallholder farmers, other local value chain actors and buyers, particularly processors, exporters, and consumers.

#### Sub-activity 2.3.1.1: Consolidate and improve existing knowledge systems to establish a semi-automatic clearinghouse system to collect, disseminate, and share lessons learned and best practices from all past and ongoing initiatives by promoting climate-resilient, high-value product value-production and processing practices and technologies.

#### Sub-activity 2.3.1.2: Promote the use of social media platforms (e.g., YouTube channels) and mobile apps (e.g., Tumpe App, Agri-Chamkar and Ecostakeholder) for on-demand information access among smallholder farmers, other local value chain actors and buyers, particularly processors, exporters, and consumers.

#### Sub-activity 2.3.1.3: Conduct annual training seminars for trainers, holders, restorers, and traders/producers to increase their capacity in climate-resilient high-value product production and processing practices and technologies, including cold storage and sourcing

**Page 44**

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

#### Activity 2.2.1: Strengthening and vertically harmonized and targeted extension services, linking the provincial, district, commune, and village levels and public and private extension providers, to promote the adoption of climate-resilient, inclusive, gender-responsive, and high-value practices and technologies relevant for the implementation of restoration and action plans developed under Activity 2.1.1 and financed under Activity 2.2.1.

**Description:** For successful implementation of the provincial-level value chain roadmap and cross-pilot action-plan processes prepared by FAO, it is essential to strengthen and vertically harmonize and target extension services at all levels. This will be achieved through coordinated extension services to promote highly relevant production and processing practices and technologies that are climate-resilient, inclusive, gender-responsive, and high-value for the implementation of restoration and action plans developed under Activity 2.1.1 and financed under Activity 2.2.1.

The following sub-activities will be implemented:

- **Sub-activity 2.2.1.1** Design inclusive and gender-responsive training curricula for four target groups: (i) public extension officers at the provincial, district, commune, and village levels; (ii) private sector actors including agribusinesses, cooperatives, and other value chain actors; and iii) female farmers and value chain actors, to promote the adoption of climate-resilient and high-value practices relevant for the implementation of restoration and action plans developed under Activity 2.1.1.

- **Sub-activity 2.2.1.2** Conduct technical training of public extension officers for identifying, demonstrating, and promoting climate-resilient and high-value practices relevant to the implementation of restoration and action plans developed under Activity 2.1.1.

- **Sub-activity 2.2.1.3** Conduct annual training of private extension services, including NGOs to manage climate-resilient and high-value practices relevant for the implementation of restoration and action plans developed under Activity 2.1.1.

- **Sub-activity 2.2.1.4** Conduct biannual TOT training of representative farmers and other local value chain actors, particularly women farmers and value chain actors in CPAs on demonstrating and promoting climate-resilient and high-value practices and technologies relevant for their action plans implementation (flexible time arrangements to meet women specific needs).

- **Sub-activity 2.2.1.5** Establish farmer field schools (FFS) at the commune level, particularly targeting women farmers and value chain actors in CPAs on demonstrating and promoting climate-resilient and high-value practices and technologies relevant for their action plans implementation.

- **Sub-activity 2.2.1.6** Establish model farmer and processor demonstration sites to promote the adoption of climate-resilient, inclusive, gender-responsive, and high-value practices and technologies relevant for the implementation of restoration and action plans developed under Activity 2.1.1.

#### Output 2.4: Improved agro-ecological conditions and connectivity.

A 123 study on forest landscape restoration opportunities in the NTZB (Bernacki et al., 2018) provides the case for investing in catchment restoration to restore and enhance ecosystem services and downstream agriculture. The approach is consistent with Cambodia’s National Forest Policy (NFP), which aims at increasing the area of natural forests, particularly non-timber forest products (NTFPs), and improve local livelihoods. The approach is also consistent with Cambodia’s National Strategic Plan for Agriculture (NSPA) 2016-2035, which aims to increase agricultural productivity through the maintenance and restoration of ecosystem services and connectivity through ensuring agroecology in the mosaic landscape.

Building on these recommendations, the PEARL project focuses its activities under this output to support CPAs and CFSs in restoring and protecting 7,600 ha of critical catchment forests and other ecologically sensitive riparian zones. It is expected that the restoration of these areas will increase the availability of water resources for agriculture and CFS’s business plans developed under Output 2.1. The FARM under Output 2.2. and demonstration of alternative livelihoods under Output 3.1 are also expected to benefit from this activity, as they will have access to increased knowledge and skills in climate-resilient practices. From this perspective, IWM and agroecology activities will promote connectivity between the mosaic landscape and the river basin through the restoration of critical catchments that increase local public services while also increasing income generation opportunities, agroecology of native and introduced species, and water resources for agriculture.

The above study’s key guidelines and area estimates were adopted to analyze and identify potential CPAs and CFSs to engage in the restoration work under the PEARL project. During project formulation, a study was conducted to identify critical catchments and other ecologically sensitive riparian zones within the project area. The study recommends 14 CPAs and CFSs (Table 10). A large group of these communities is located within the catchment areas of the four major river basins in the PEARL project, which have a significant bearing on downstream rice-growing regions in Preah Vihear and Kampong Thom provinces.

The project will scale up the efforts of the National Integrated Pest Management (IPM) Program, supported by FAO and the International Rice Research Institute, developed over the past decade.

### EXPECTED OUTCOME THREE: REGULATORY AND INSTITUTIONAL FRAMEWORKS AND CAPACITIES FOR CLIMATE-RESILIENT AGRICULTURAL CERTIFICATION, CROSS-SECTORAL COORDINATION FOR INTEGRATED FINANCING AND SMALLHOLDER FINANCING, AND CLIMATE-INFORMED INVESTMENT SUPPORT ARE STRENGTHENED

#### Output 3.1: Regulatory and institutional arrangements and capacity relevant to developing certification-based value chains strengthened to provide enabling conditions for adopting climate-resilient, high-value and sustainable agricultural practices

127 This component will support the delivery of Components 1 and 2 by ensuring enabling conditions through a coordinated approach that integrates regulatory frameworks, institutional arrangements, and capacities. The overall integration of efforts and best practices across relevant sectors, stakeholder groups, and levels of government will be critical to achieving this outcome.

Output 3.1: Regulatory and institutional arrangements and capacity relevant to developing certification-based value chains strengthened to provide enabling conditions for adopting climate-resilient, high-value and sustainable agricultural practices

128 Institutional support and coordination efforts and mechanisms for supporting the market-based transition of smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors to climate-resilient and high-value agriculture are currently not in place. The project will ensure that the institutional arrangements and capacities identified as critical by the project are effective and climate resilient to achieve its goal. A systematic approach to promoting inclusive and participatory governance, including stakeholder engagement mechanisms such as public consultations or a grievance redress mechanism is necessary to complement FARM under Output 2.2 to accelerate their transition. The PEARL project will also ensure that the institutional arrangements are in place for the ARDB to demonstrate its leadership in promoting, adapting, and operationalizing the lending scorecard system.

129 Under this output, the PEARL project will support the capacity development of the ARDB as it lead any sectoral coordination efforts. The ARDB is expected to be a key actor in the institutional arrangements and system for the agricultural financial sector and is also expected to be an integral part of the project's exit strategy, as the ARDB with the project's support may develop into a leading institution in the country that can provide technical assistance to other institutions seeking to transition to climate-resilient agriculture. The project’s capacity development support for the ARDB will include establishing and strengthening its institutional arrangements and capacities to ensure it is able to lead any sectoral coordination efforts, as well as providing technical assistance to the ARDB to demonstrate its leadership in promoting, adapting, and operationalizing the lending scorecard system.

130 The joint leadership of MAFF and MOE with technical support from FAO Cambodia. Concerning agricultural finance, the ARDB® will be a coordinating role. The target beneficiaries of this output include the public institutions and private sector actors involved in the development and implementation of climate-resilient agriculture programs, integration of the private sector into local development planning, and standardization of the smallholder lending scorecard system. The project will support the ARDB to develop an institutional arrangement for stakeholder engagement with consultations with prioritized private sector entities based on a mapping exercise to identify and score their potential relevance in supporting climate-resilient agriculture programs. This will be done through the development of resource mobilization strategies. Potential private sector partners are identified based on their leadership in relevant areas such as credit, insurance, and other financial services. The ARDB® will work with MAFF, MOE, ARDB, and the Secretariats of NCDO and NCCD.

Activities under Output 3.1 (See Figure E.8.)

## Activity 3.2.1: Establish a gender-responsive landscape-level agroecology monitoring system (LAMS) to develop in public-private partnerships for climate-resilient, high-value and sustainable agriculture.

### Description:
This approach will promote and demonstrate harmonized practices across the agricultural finance sector to mainstream climate and food security considerations into financial products and services. It will also facilitate the development of a gender-responsive landscape-level agroecology monitoring system (LAMS) that will provide an interactive web platform for public and private investors in agriculture to inform investment decisions.

### Activities Under Output 3.2.1 (See Section E.6.)

#### Sub-activity 3.2.1.1: Establish a gender-responsive landscape-level agroecology monitoring system (LAMS) with an interactive web platform.
* **Description:** LAMS will offer a one-stop data platform for public and private investors in agriculture to inform investment decisions. LAMS will guide investors on the best investments, based on climate change projections, soil health, water availability, biodiversity, and social and economic impacts over time.

#### Sub-activity 3.2.1.2: Facilitate data sharing and harmonization agreements between MFPT of HLG and new/renewed hosting institutions.
* **Description:** This sub-activity will facilitate data sharing and harmonization agreements between MFPT of HLG and new/renewed hosting institutions.

#### Sub-activity 3.2.1.3: Design a gender-responsive LAMS operating framework with a well-defined role for an SDP for operating LAMS, including:
* **Description:** This sub-activity will design a gender-responsive LAMS operating framework with a well-defined role for an SDP for operating LAMS.

#### Sub-activity 3.2.1.4: Train system analysts and administrators on the SDP to operate LAMS.
* **Description:** This sub-activity will train system analysts and administrators on the SDP to operate LAMS, including data collection and analysis of climate-resilient crops in AquaCrop and through AEZ methodologies based on ground data to aid the identification and validation of potential agroecological conditions under LAMS.

### Output 3.2: Gender-responsive landscape-level agroecology monitoring system (LAMS) developed to crowd-in public-private partnerships for climate-resilient, high-value and sustainable agriculture.

## Alignment with National Policies and Strategies

### Climate Change and Agriculture under Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC)

132 Out of the 95 priority adaptation actions identified in the updated NDC, 7 focus on agriculture (bid.). Among such actions are: increasing the resilience of crop systems and agricultural livelihoods to climate change impacts; promoting the adoption of climate-resilient and higher-value production of cash-crop, mango, orange, rice, and leafy vegetables among smallholder farmers; and supporting the development of a national framework for climate change adaptation at USD 305 million, much of which is expected to come through international support.

133 These climate change adaptation actions aim to increase the resilience of crop systems and agricultural livelihoods under climate change impacts. The NDC also identifies public sector investments in research and development, as well as management practices for increased agroecological forms and crop suitability. The NDC calls for increased public participation in the design and implementation of these actions, with a focus on the transition towards a climate-resilient and sustainable development pathway. The NDC also stresses the need for strengthening institutional capacity to support the implementation of adaptation actions through planning, analysis, management, monitoring, and reporting, ensuring an enabling regulatory environment for PPPs, and establishing effective PPPs.

135 The NDC contributes directly to the Cambodia Climate Change Strategic Plan (CCSCP) (2014), which builds on the NSDP and Rectangular Strategy IV.

### National Adaptation Plan (NAP) Process

136 Cambodia's NAP process builds on the National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) (2009) and identifies a list of adaptation actions for climate change and human resources as the primary challenges and sets out medium- and long-term adaptation goals in critical affected sectors, including agriculture and water resources and human health. The NDC also contributes to the development of the NAP process by identifying key sectoral adaptation elements (e.g., developing implementation strategies, etc.) and setting up reporting and monitoring framework to place the NDC at the center of policy agenda (LISDA, 2017). Together with the NDC, the NAP also contributes to NSDP and CCSCP.

### Cambodia's GCF Country Programme

137 Building on its NAPA, NAP process, CCFP, and sectoral climate change action plans, Cambodia's GCF programme prioritizes agriculture and water resources, infrastructure, forestry, health and coastal development as its priority investment areas for climate change adaptation. Agriculture and forestry are also identified key sectors for the implementation of the NDC. The GCF programme is currently in the process of developing a National Strategy for Establishing effective PPPs to leverage private investment for supporting adaptation actions as an essential strategy under the Country Programme. Cambodia currently has one Direct Access Entity (DAE), the National Committee for


---
**Page 49**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 46

## Sub-National Development Secretariat (NDSS): Increasing the number of DAEs in Cambodia is another priority. In this context, the ARD is currently considered by the NDA for DAE nomination.

### Climate Change Action Plan for Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries Sector

138 Directly contributing to the CCGBP, the Climate Change Action Plan for Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries Sector aims at increasing the capacity of local communities in terms of climate-resilient agricultural practices and human resources in the sector to devise new and innovative technologies and measures. It also aims to increase farmers' awareness of climate- change impacts on their livelihoods and to provide them with the necessary knowledge, skills, tools, inputs, and measures to minimize their crop and livestock damage and loss. The COPAF-APP identifies strategic objectives for its implementation in the sector, including: (i) increasing the capacity of local communities to implement adaptation actions to reduce their greenhouse gas emissions and increase their adaptive capacities to cope with increased temperatures and more frequent extreme weather events; (ii) promoting climate-smart agriculture practices such as the promotion of sustainable farming systems and postharvest technologies, development of suitable crop variety and an improved livestock management system; (iii) increasing access to finance for smallholder farmers through smart technologies and techniques, capacity building of agricultural cooperatives and SMEs in climate-smart agriculture.

139 While the COPAF-APP sets out a comprehensive plan of action that sets ambitious targets, less than 50% of the US$246 million required for the full implementation of the COPAF-APP was secured at the start of its implementation. The remaining funds will be mobilized through the World Bank's Climate Change Adaptation Fund (CCAF), government annual budget allocation, loans and grants from the World Bank, Asian Development Bank (ADB) and other development partners. In addition to the funding gap, the lack of coordination between donors and bilateral agencies also provide additional support; much more is still needed to close the current financial shortfall.

### Cambodian Sustainable Development Goals (CSDGs) Framework (2016-2030)

140 Guided by the NDSP and Rectangular Strategy IV, the CSDGs Framework (2016a) sets out the country-specific targets and indicators to track and monitor its progress towards its 18 goals, adapted from the Global Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The 18 goals are grouped into four thematic areas: Goal 1 (end poverty); Goal 2 (zero hunger); Goal 3 (gender equality); Goal 4 (climate action); and Goal 15 (life on land). Each goal is further divided into sub-goals. For example, under Goal 1 (end poverty), the sub-goal for per capita gain will be doubled; and public expenditure on climate actions will also be increased by one percentage point by 2030.

### FAO's Country Programme Framework 2019-2023

141 Building on the RSCD’s national development, sectoral and climate priorities, as briefly described above, and the United Nations Development Assistance Framework (UNDAF) 2019 - 2023, combined with FAO’s corporate strategic framework, the Country Programme Framework (CPF) sets out a comprehensive strategy for the period 2019-2023. The CPF is aligned to the RSCD’s national development priorities and sectoral strategies, as well as the UNDAF’s thematic areas and cross-cutting issues. It also provides a framework for FAO’s engagement in climate action and sets out the key results expected from its work on agriculture and food systems for poverty reduction and food and nutrition security.

142 Each CPF goal is further divided into sub-goals, which are grouped under five strategic objectives: (i) improving agricultural productivity; (ii) increasing access to markets and value chains; (iii) increasing capacity to monitor and report climate action; (iv) reducing vulnerability; and improved resilience to climate change and shocks at national, community and household level.

143 The PEALR project contributes to the delivery of these outcomes, for example: by 1) promoting organic agriculture, GAP, good agricultural practices, inclusive, and gender-responsive practices, guided by relevant best practices and lessons-learned; under Outcome 1.2) monitoring and protecting critical catchments and agroecological functions and connectivity through participatory approaches; (iii) increasing the capacity of farmers to manage their resources more effectively; (iv) supporting farmers and other value chain actors through improved access to highly tailored climate risk information, adaptive innovations and services.

#### Accredited Entity (AE)

143 FAO will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for this project. As such, FAO will be responsible for the overall management of the project, including: (i) all aspects of project appraisal; (ii) administrative, financial and technical oversight of the project implementation; (iii) ensuring that the project is implemented in a manner to achieve expected results and achieve objectives; (iv) ensuring the quality of project monitoring, as well as the timeliness and quality of reporting on project progress; and (v) ensuring compliance with all applicable laws and regulations in accordance with the detailed provisions outlined in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) between FAO and GCF.

144 FAO’s role as AE will be attributed to the relevant offices and divisions in FAO Headquarters located in Rome, Italy and FAO Country Offices (COs) in Cambodia and the Pacific (FAO-RAP) located in Bangkok, Thailand and FAO Representation in Cambodia (FAO-Cambodia).

145 In order to fulfill its AE functions, FAO will set up a dedicated Project Task Force (PTF) on line with GCF project requirements. The PTF will be led by the Country Director (CD), supported by the Lead Technical Officer (LTO), Funding Liaison Officer (FLO), HQ Technical Officer and other officers, as appropriate.

146 The PTF will remain independent from the Executing Entity functions also performed by FAO for Project execution selection and implementation. This separation of responsibilities is necessary to ensure that the independent segregation of responsibilities within FAO will ensure that the Organization can independently and effectively perform its AE function in line with GCF General principles and indicative list of eligible costs covered under GCF fees and project management costs.

### Project co-financing

147 The Royal Government of Cambodia acting through MAFF and MoIc will provide co-financing in the form of in-kind contribution to the project: (i) through MAFF USD 3.6 million; and (ii) through MoIc USD 2.58 million. The co-financer contributions are subject to the terms and conditions set out in the Project Agreement between the Royal Government of Cambodia and GCF as AMA, Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) between GCF and FAO and the Project Agreement between the Host Country and GCF. The project will be implemented through the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (MAFF), and the Ministry of Industry, Commerce and Tourism (MoIc). The co-financing amount is subject to the terms and conditions set out in the Project Agreement between the Royal Government of Cambodia acting through MAFF and MoIc and GCF.

148 FAO will provide USD 0.67 million co-financing in the form of in-kind contribution.

### Other project partners

150 The project will be engaged in the project when ever it ensures complementarity with their activities and/or delivery of goods, works and services. These include: MoWRAM, MoIC, ARIDC and NGOs (potential NGO partners includes CARE Cambodia, Concern Worldwide, Oxfam, Save the Children, World Vision, etc.). The selection will be based on competitive bidding process.The procured parties will be selected during project implementation in accordance with relevant FAO Manual Sections.

### Execution

150 The project will be executed by The Royal Government of Cambodia acting through MAFF and MoIc and FAO in to deliver the project activities (see Table 11).

151 FAO will act as co-EE and will ensure strong country-driven execution of project activities and execute the in-kind contribution. FAO will also be responsible for the selection of selected activities funded by GCF proceeds based on its comparative advantages.

152 A Project Execution Team (PET) will be set up in FAO Cambodia, comprising relevant FAO country personnel (including FAO Representative of Programs), Administrative and operational staff and will be responsible for FAO EE function.

153 The Royal Government of Cambodia acting through MAFF and MoIc will executing project activities funded by GCF proceeds. FAO will enter into Operational Partnership Agreements with MAFF and MoIc.

Project Execution

15A A dedicated Project Management Unit (PMU) will be established and hosted by MAFF in Cambodia. The PMU shall be responsible for the entire duration of the project. The PMU will be established at the General Directorate of Agriculture at the MAFF (GDMAFF) in Phnom Penh, Cambodia. The PMU will coordinate directly with EEs and project stakeholders to ensure that the project is executed according to plan and to monitor the execution of day-to-day activities and technical quality of the project outputs, effective stakeholder engagement.

15B The PMU shall be led by a National Project Director (NPD) and managed by a project-specific National Project Coordinator (NPC). The NPC will be responsible for overall project management and coordination with project stakeholders. The PMU will also include a finance officer, communications officer, and other technical staff as required.

15C Within the PMU, project-recruited staff and staff seconded by the government will collectively comprise a project delivery team that will ensure sound and effective project implementation. The specific roles of critical members of this project delivery team are described in Table 12.

Financial management and procurement under this project will be guided by relevant FAO rules and regulations as relevant procurements are subject to the Memorandum of Agreement (MOA) between FAO and GCF. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 53

## B.S. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

166 Cambodia is an LDC with many development priorities and investment needs. One of these priorities is accelerating the transformation to a more sustainable agricultural sector. The country's economy and its rural population heavily depend on this sector, and rural agrarian communities are highly vulnerable to the observed and anticipated negative impacts of climate change. In 2019, approximately 75% of Cambodia’s GDP came from agriculture, and 84% of the country’s agricultural output comes from this region, and about 20% of the country's population, mainly engaged in agriculture, resides here.

However, Cambodia has limited financial and technical capacity to address this urgent need on its own adequately. The RSC is also aware that both public and private finance is necessary to harness the benefits of value-chain approaches for sustainable agricultural development. To achieve these goals, the RSC recognizes the need for public sector efforts to establish the necessary enabling conditions to leverage and mobilize private sector investments in a timely manner.

The GCF project will support the Government of Cambodia (GoC) in implementing its national priority project under Cambodia’s GCF Country Programme (2023a). This project is aligned with the GoC’s National Strategic Plan for Agriculture 2019-2024 and the GoC’s Agricultural Sector Strategy. The project will be implemented through a multi-sector approach, particularly agricultural suppliers, traders/exporters, retailers, and local financial institutions, to work in partnership with the private sector. In addition to supporting the development of the value chain for agricultural products, this effort, the RSC also intends to expand the value chain concept to include consumers as a critical group of actors to promote sustainable consumption practices and secure increased access to safe, quality, and sustainably produced agricultural products in the country.

169 The financial support from the GCF will directly contribute to the country's current effort toward realizing the full-scale implementation of the CCPAP in agriculture, while the project’s value-chain approach keeps investment risk low.


---
**Page 57**
---

The activities proposed in the project would not be viable at a lower level of concessionality, as revenue-generating activities are not included in the project. The project will partner with private-sector actors who are SMEs with limited access to high-value premium price markets and will support them through the establishment of public-private partnerships (PPPs) with smallholder farmers and other small-scale local value chain actors. The project’s intervention is crucial to increase, the marketability of their products and services in order to improve their income levels. To achieve this objective, these actors need to meet specific certification standards and their technological access through the FARM (Outcome 2). The private sector actors will be supported by a combination of public and private resources, where public funds (for proceeds and SMEs) are needed and complementary. The successful adaptation approach based on premium price markets is expected to scale up and expand its efforts to develop climate-resilient and green investment projects in the post-project environment through PSPPS by leveraging further investment and crowding-in of others in the post-project environment.

170 GCF grant financing will be directed towards capacity development and the establishment of enabling regulatory frameworks, including the design of public-private partnerships (PPPs) with smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors to make technologies more accessible to smallholder farmers, ACS, FAL, PGGs, and agricultural unions. The areas of intervention will include: a) promoting the adoption of climate-resilient and sustainable agricultural practices; b) reducing social and environmental externalities through producing and securing market access for climate-resilient, quality, safe, and organic products; c) increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change.

### B. Ext strategy (max. 500 words, approximately | page)

171 The project’s potential for sustainability and replicability is built on PSPPS through the integrated landscape and value-chain approaches. PSPPS are especially suited for the agriculture sector, where public needs from both the private and public sectors can be met by combining public and private resources to achieve a common regulatory framework. Having such shared social values and visions will ensure fair and equitable distribution of benefits and risks among all stakeholders involved in the project. The project’s approach is based on the idea that smallholder farmers, ACS, FAL, PGGs, and agricultural unions can be supported by public funds to reduce their social and environmental externalities through producing and securing market access for climate-resilient, quality, safe, and organic products.

172 Through the PEARL project, smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors currently without full access to high-value premium price markets will have developed pertinent knowledge and skills to meet various market requirements. The project has also provided them with a range of financial services, including microfinance loans that have received capacity development support to prepare, update and operationalize highly tailored business plans and marketing strategies for their products and services in the post-project environment. These actors are now better positioned to access high-value agriculture and sustainable livelihoods visions. The project will have facilitated the development of premium price markets through PSPPS, which will provide smallholder farmers with a range of financial services, including insurance and enabling regulatory and institutional conditions. As indicated earlier, the majority of value chain actors in the project are already engaged in climate-resilient agricultural practices and are now better positioned to engage in value chain development and expansion that focuses on inclusion, climate resilience, and sustainability as key strategic momentum for further unlocking private investment for climate-resilient agriculture in this post-project phase.

## D1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 181 Based on stakeholder consultations during the project design phase, combined with the projections of climate change impacts and adaptation strategies, the Pearl River Delta region is identified as a priority area for the project in 24 districts in the northern sections of the NTSS (as the maps in Annex 16) with relatively well-preserved agricultural ecosystems. The majority of these areas are characterized by low-lying topography, which makes them highly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events and rising sea levels.

### 182 In these sections of the NTSS, many smallholder farmers in these areas produce cash crops, mango, orange, rice, and other crops. These farmers are also involved in aquaculture activities. The project will focus on improving their access to climate-resilient agriculture.

### 183 As shown in Table 13, among the most recent agricultural census (NIS), approximately 300,000 households were identified as smallholder farming as their primary livelihood activity in these 24 target districts. About 85% of these farmers are involved in rice production, while the remaining 15% produce perennial and other crops, including vegetables, fruits, and aquaculture.

### 184 Additionally, based on the areas identified for the IWM activities under Output 2.4, derived from the recent agricultural census (NIS), approximately 300,000 households were identified as smallholder farming in these districts (50,000) as direct beneficiaries of improved agroecological conditions while noting the overall benefit of enhanced ecosystem services and biodiversity conservation. Based on the above-identified beneficiary groups and outreach figures, the project development team consulted with the past and ongoing agricultural support projects of FAO and other development partners in the country to identify the project’s potential outreach capacity from the operational and financial standpoints.

### Project Impact on Beneficiaries

The project also targets roughly 35% of the NTSB's population (1,000,000) who are primarily dependent on agriculture as their primary livelihood. These beneficiaries form a beneficiary group that is not directly targeted by the project and this group is thus indirectly impacted through the project’s activities. Beneficiaries will benefit from the project by having general access to improved agricultural practices and technologies through peer-to-peer learning, demonstration of alternative practices and technologies, and increased market access.

In addition, private sector actors, including agriculture suppliers, collectors, processors, traders, and other value chain actors, will also be indirectly impacted. The project will provide training for these actors in providing agricultural extension services, developing market opportunities, and increasing their capacity to supply the NTSB with high-value crops. Beneficiaries will benefit from increased supply and sourcing capacities for climate-resilient and sustainable agricultural products through improved access to markets by both farmers and actors. However, at the inception of this project, the project cannot at this point estimate to target beneficiary groups.

### Delivering these benefits

The project's direct and indirect beneficiaries contribute to the three GCF Result Areas: AR4.1: Climate-resilient and sustainable agriculture; AR4.2: Ecosystems and ecosystem services, as described further in Section 3.3.1.

### Project Impact on Beneficiaries

The project will focus on creating enabling conditions through market-based mechanisms based on PSSPs to support a smooth and timely transition to climate-adapted, high-value, and sustainable agriculture. This will facilitate increased access of smallholder farmers to markets for their climate-resilient, high-value crops, leading to greater resilience and sustainability. The increased market access and opportunities created under Outcome 2 through market-based mechanisms based on PSSPs will lead to the development of value chains that are more resilient, sustainable, and higher-value agricultural practices. Regulatory and institutional enabling conditions under Outcome 3 ensure that these market-based mechanisms can be scaled up and replicated in other regions. These incentives and enabling conditions provide the project's beneficiary farmers and other local value chain actors practical benefits.

The project will catalyze impact beyond its one-off investment by supporting its beneficiaries in improving access to climate information, business, market and extension knowledge, finance, and technologies. The accelerated adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies will lead to increased yields for crops such as maize, mango, organic rice, and vegetable value chains to access premium price markets to increase their farm income and improve food security.

The project will also support the development of a market-based mechanism based on PSSPs in the northern regions of the NTSB. This is considered highly relevant to the northern sections of the NTSB where climate change features are generally more pronounced, leading to increased yields for crops such as maize, mango, organic rice, and vegetable value chains will also benefit the private sector actors, leading the development of sustainable value chains based on the target crops by increasing their supply capacity and business expansion opportunities.

The project will mitigate transformational change in the NTSB through the following six mechanisms. The market-driven nature of these mechanisms make them highly scalable and replicable within the NTSB and beyond.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 60

## Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project is expected to build stronger and closer relationships between climate resilience, sustainable agriculture, and food security in the NTSB region of Ontario, Canada. It will provide a framework for sustainable development in Cambodia. Such efforts will produce several additional benefits, contributing directly to UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).

### Project beneficiaries

The project's beneficiaries will be provided with the services and capacities needed to sustainably increase their food production capacity and income generation opportunities along the targeted value chains. The catchment area of the NTSB region is characterized by a high degree of climate variability, which has been exacerbated by climate change.

### Needs of recipients (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Migration

Labor migration by men to urban areas and abroad is widespread in the northern sections of the NTBS. The number of female-headed households has increased significantly since 2014, particularly in the NTSB, with Oador Meanchey and Fresh Wheat among the country's poorest provinces (RSCG, 2014; 2016). Many female-headed households have shifted to vegetable production due to cope with labor shortages.

#### Climate-related activities

The climate change adaptation strategy, described in its NBS, CCSPS, and sectoral CCPAPs, has also been well demonstrated through mostly public spending. In 2014, over US$ 200 million were allocated for climate-related activities (2015–2016), with the largest share going to agriculture. Despite the increasing interest in climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts in agriculture-related sectors to tackle growing commitments and interests from the public and private sectors.

#### Climate-related spending

Despite these increasing interests and commitments, the climate-related spending of the MAFU accumulatively accounted for only 1% of its total budget (2015–2016). The sources of finance for the CCPAP include the Secretariat Working Group on Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (SGAFF), the National Adaptation Programmes of Action (NAPA), and the Climate Change Trust Fund. Various climate change funds mobilized through the UN and bilateral agencies also provide additional support, much more than what is required to meet the country's commitments under the Paris Agreement. The NAP includes development and framework building (i.e., NAP Process, REDD+, general early warning) at the national level. Despite the increasing interest in climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts in agriculture-related sectors, it is mainly focused on agricultural productivity and poverty alleviation or on specific climate adaptation on a pilot basis without adequate coordination and mechanisms for scaling up best practices (see Annex 2 for more details).

### D.5 Country ownership (max 300 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Brief Description of FAO, MAPP and MoE

**2) FAO is a specialized agency of the United Nations with the mandate to improve food security, nutrition, and agricultural productivity and reduce rural poverty; it is leading a global initiative with highly technical expertise and experience in climate change adaptation. The project will be implemented under the overall guidance of FAO in meeting the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development through the transformation to more efficient, inclusive, resilient, and sustainable agriculture and food systems.**

**3) FAO has supported Cambodia since 1979 in agricultural production, value chain development, market access, climate change adaptation and mitigation, sustainable management of natural resources (forestry and fisheries), environmental protection, and disaster risk reduction. FAO is currently the lead agency for the MAPP in the country and has extensive experience designing and implementing projects in the NTSB. The overarching goal of this project is to contribute to the achievement of Cambodia’s national development goals through sustainable management and use of the country's natural resources. FAO has had a strong partnership and strategically aligned with the MoE, which has been responsible for the implementation of the National Agricultural Policy, food security and nutrition, sustainable management of natural resources, and climate resilience.**

**4) The two Co-EEs of the project are the MAPP and MoE. The MAPP was established in 1996 with the mandate to provide technical assistance to the MoE for the implementation of the National Agricultural Policy (NAP). In 1996 the MoE assumed the mandate to coordinate the environmental affairs in Cambodia. The MoE is Cambodia’s focal institution for the Rio Conventions and for COP25.**

#### Country Ownership

**The Pearl project is identified as the number one priority adaptation project under Cambodia's GCF Country Programme (CP). This project will be implemented by the MAPP, which has been working with the MoE in Cambodia's NDA and other vital stakeholders recognize the urgent adaptation challenges faced by the country’s agricultural sector. The Pearl project is a key component of the NDA and the NDC. In this respect, establishing effective FPIC to unlock private finance for adaptation projects is an essential element of the Programme.**

**The NDA looks to the Pearl project to lay the necessary foundation for strategically directing public spending to fill the existing capacity gaps in enabling conditions to increase the number of bankable projects for private investment and to provide a strong basis for scaling up climate-smart agricultural practices, including climate-resilient agriculture adaptation. The Pearl project will strengthen the technical and operational capacity of the ARBS as a place of last resort for farmers who are unable to access other sources of credit. The ARBS is expected to continue advancing the aims of agricultural finance to accelerate the transition to climate-resilient and low-carbon agriculture in the NTSB region, which offers significant opportunities for private sector investment.**

**In this context, the Pearl project has been prepared under the overall leadership of the NDA and will be fully active engagement by relevant national and local government and NGO partners, including the MAPP, MoE, MiWRM, and other key stakeholders in the NTSB region. The Pearl project is also expected to provide a strong platform for representing SMEs, traders/exporters, microfinance institutions, insurance providers, input suppliers, and retailers have**

## Sustainability

There are limited policies and institutions conducive for women in value chain actors, particularly women farmers and women agribusinesses. There is a need to provide the necessary financial resources for transitioning to climate-resilient agricultural practices, including inputs production and processing. Also, coordinated efforts towards addressing investment gaps and agricultural markets are needed at the national level as direct public spending on agriculture is limited.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND VISIONARY PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 58

## Core Indicator 1: Design to which GCF will contribute to strengthening policy and regulatory frameworks for the promotion of climate-resilient development and high-value agriculture driven market systems.

### Current policy, regulations, and institutional framework for the promotion of climate-resilient development and high-value agricultural production processes (e.g., Cambo. Act 2019, NAPCC Act 2020, and NACOPA Act 2020) and a systematic approach for a comprehensive assessment of existing policies, regulations, institutional frameworks, and other enabling conditions through the integration of transformational approaches to climate-resilient high-value agriculture.

### Limited institutional support structures for multiplier interventions in value chain actors, particularly women. Women are underrepresented in agricultural value chains and have limited access to resources and opportunities for transitioning to climate-resilient high-value agriculture production and processing.

## Core Indicator 2: Design to which GCF will contribute to effective knowledge sharing of processes and use of best practices, and the adoption of standards and benchmarks.

### Relevant lessons learned and best practices for climate-resilient and high-value agriculture are available and accessible through a robust information management system. Best practices are regularly updated by multiplier interventions in value chains using lessons from various sources including farmers’ associations, research institutions, and other stakeholders. Best practices are regularly applied by multiplier interventions and other actors to increase the adoption of climate-resilient and high-value agricultural technologies.

### Public and private financial institutions establish a dedicated loan program with an information management system for tracking loans and monitoring their impact on climate-resilient and high-value agriculture value chains, and other value chain actors through increased access to finance, increased FPOs, and improved financial access (see Outcome 3).

The project will establish a clear framework and develop information management systems to disseminate capacities to increase the adoption of climate-resilient and high-value agricultural technologies under Outcome 2.

## Objective 2: Strengthen market infrastructure for cashew, mango, organic rice, and other smallholder producers and processors through capacity building, resident and high-value action business plans (linking to Sub-components 2.1 and 2.2) and technical capacity building opportunities based on certification programs.

### Baseline
- Number of ACFs, FAs, PIsA, OPAs, OAIs, OPIsA, and agricultural unions in the project area.
- Development of updated action business plans and gender-responsive progress reports.

### Target
- 50 ACFs, FAs, PIsA, OPAs, OAIs, OPIsA, and agricultural unions in the project area with active action business plans, and at least half of them are fully operational.
- Effective coordination and collaboration among ACFs, FAs, PIsA, OPAs, OAIs, OPIsA, and other national governments, NGOs and private sector actors.

### Notes
- Strong interest and support from the government.
- Targeted beneficiary groups are interested in participating in the project with active participation of members (especially women and youth).
- Despite the growing private market opportunities for cashew and mango adaptation potential, these opportunities are not fully exploited by smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors in the project area. This is due to lack of strategy planning and quality control issues related to organic production.
- The average membership size of ACFs, FAs, PIsA, OPAs, OAIs, OPIsA, and agricultural unions is 10.

### Baseline
- Percentage increase in the number of cashew contracts and direct purchase agreements signed with local farmers and local retailers, foodservice restaurants, processors, and other actors.
- At least a 15% increase in contract demand for cashew and direct purchase agreements over the inception baseline.

### Target
- More than a 20% increase in contract demand for cashew and direct purchase agreements over the inception baseline.

### Notes
- Strong interest and support from the government.
- Cashew will be included in the baseline assessment.
- Direct farming and direct purchase agreements are practical tools to improve market access. In most markets, such agreements are, however, primarily developed between processors and vegetable value chain actors and can also be established among organic rice producers.

# Project/programme co-benefit indicators

## Agricultural production and greenhouse gas emissions reduction through agricultural management practices (e.g., improved soil health, reduced tillage, enhanced soil organic carbon sequestration)

### Baseline
- According to Cambodia’s National Forest Management Plan (NFMP), the total area of forest under protection is 105,327 hectares.
- The total area of protected forests is 68,930 hectares of natural forest and 36,397 hectares of planted forest.

### Mid-term
- 2,500 hectares of natural forest and 4,000 hectares of planted forest under protection are established by 2025.
- At least 100 farmers adopt improved soil management practices (e.g., reduced tillage, enhanced soil organic carbon sequestration) on at least 30% of their land.

### Target
- 7,000 hectares of natural forest and 8,000 hectares of planted forest under protection are established by 2030.
- At least 15,000 hectares of natural forest and 9,000 hectares of planted forest under protection are established by 2040.

### Assumptions/Notes
- Effective coordination and collaboration exist between relevant ministries, sub-national governments, NGOs, and private sector actors.
- Funding is available (including donor funding) for the project.
- Data availability (especially women and other socially excluded communities) is limited.
- Baseline will be updated after the baseline assessment.

## Gender considerations are fully internalized into climate-resilient agricultural development practices

### Baseline
- Women farmers and value chain actors are visited by at least 50% of project staff during completion surveys.
- At least 80% of women farmers have access to information, technologies, and services that provide opportunities to increase their income or change behavior in their farming activities. More women agri-businesses are established to unlock opportunities for women and other value chain actors.

### Mid-term
- At least 50 ACA, FAPs, OPIAs, CPAIs, and CFAIs are implemented.
- Project progress is monitored through qualitative and quantitative assessments.

### Target
- At least 82 ACA, FAPs, OPIAs, CPAIs, and CFAIs are implemented.
- Effective coordination and collaboration exist between relevant ministries, sub-national governments, NGOs, and private sector actors.
- Targeted beneficiaries are interested and available.
- Baseline will be updated after the baseline assessment.

## Activity 1.1.1: Increase the spatial scale of national meteorological and agrimeteorological data collection and processing capacity for climate services, including improved agrometeorological forecasts and advisories tailored for target value chain crops.

### Sub-activities
* **Sub-activity 1.1.1.1:** Mobile the TFRS-AIR led by MAPF and MWR&M, with additional experts from other relevant organizations, to conduct training workshops on climate services, including weather and climate information, agrometeorological forecasts and advisories, and crop suitability maps and data gaps for specific crops, as well as identify strategic locations for adding new agrometeorological stations. The team will also provide technical assistance in the installation of hardware and software at key priority stations and advise on the selection of appropriate agrometeorological stations to provide climate services.
* **Sub-activity 1.1.1.2:** Deepen and roll out an annual training program for project staff, including meteorologists and agrimeteorologists, as well as crop advisors and station administrators to improve their capacities to manage and interpret weather and climate information, agrometeorological forecasts and advisories, and data storage and processing facilities.
* **Sub-activity 1.1.1.3:** Develop a career development plan for outstanding staff members from the training and advisory services sector with graduate degrees/certificates in hydrometeorology.

### Deliverables
* A report by the expert working group with recommendations on capacity upgrades and personnel training needs and programs.
* Upgrade and installation completion reports, 12 existing stations upgraded with additional sensors, eight (8) new agrometeorological stations installed.
* An annual training program: seven (7) annual training workshops with project staff, including meteorologists and agrimeteorologists, as well as crop advisors and station administrators to improve their capacities to manage and interpret weather and climate information, agrometeorological forecasts and advisories, and data storage and processing facilities. Two staff members will be trained.
* A career development plan for outstanding staff members from the training and advisory services sector with graduate degrees/certificates in hydrometeorology.

* **Sub-activity 1.1.2.2:** Develop SOPs for the production of climate services, including weather and climate information, agrometeorological forecasts and advisories tailored for target value chain crops and trees, and vegetables by a variety of mediums.

## Management of climate change risks and opportunities, including gender-responsive, and actions to support the implementation of the roadmap and action plans developed under Activity 2.1.2. # RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F1 Risk factors and mitigation measures (pages 3-5)

### Selected Risk Factor 1: Possible low demand due to changes in market trends

#### Category: Technical and operational
##### Probability: Medium
##### Impact: High

**Description:** 223 Both domestic and international agricultural commodity markets and trends evolve at a relatively fast speed. At the same time, the trend towards quality and safe food and agricultural products will likely remain as the countries transition into more industrialized economies. The project is expected to be affected by these changes in market trends. As part of this project preparation, the market opportunities could shift towards other crops or the demand relative to the production and value-added activities of the project may reduce thus reducing its impact.

#### Mitigation Measure(s): 224 The project has identified and assessed several agricultural certification programs already operational in the country. These programs are designed to help farmers adapt their practices to meet the changing demands associated with climate-resilient agriculture. Specific certification programs will only be selected at the inception stage. This is because the demand for these products may change over time, as well as the availability of markets and the formulation and ingestion stages. Therefore, the selection of specific market instruments will be made after updating information on the market trends and the project’s value chain. The project will also monitor the market trends in real-time. Similarly, the choice of specific target beneficiary groups, in addition to the potential champion group already identified, will be determined at inception stage based on the demand for these products. This is part of the overall strategy to address this risk and ensure that the project identifies the most suitable and timely match between the production activities and market demands. The project will also continue to monitor the market trends through coordination with the EEA and private sector collaborators, the project will keep a close watch on these shifts throughout its implementation.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Limited intersectoral coordination and institutional arrangements

#### Category: Technical and operational
##### Probability: Low
##### Impact: Medium

**Description:** 225 Weak coordination and cooperation between actors at the national and sub-national levels and between public and private actors pose a risk operationally and technically to undermine the project’s effort to address climate change. The project will adopt an intersectoral approach that is consistent with the country’s overall approach in contingent upon effective coordination and collaboration between actors along the target value chains.

#### Mitigation Measure(s): 226 While having the SPC with members of government, farmers’ groups, NGOs, and private sector at the project oversight level ensures effective coordination and collaboration between these actors, the project will include specific mechanisms to ensure that this is maintained throughout its implementation. These include:

- **Intersectoral capacity building (e.g., training and awareness-raising activities) at the national and sub-national levels**
- **Data-sharing agreements (e.g., Activities 1.1.2 and 3.2 and MoUs) (e.g., Activity 3.1.2) to establish formal coordination mechanisms between actors**

#### Selected Risk Factor 3: Limited stakeholder engagement

#### Category: Technical and operational
##### Probability: Low
##### Impact: High

**Description:** 227 The likelihood of limited engagement by the beneficiary groups, including ACs, FAs, PGS, CPAs, FAOs and users, and the private actors, in the project activities is limited given the extensive consultations with the potential beneficiary groups during the preparation phase. However, there are still risks that these groups may not be able to show skills, shifts in market trends, opportunities, and baseline capacities and conditions could affect their engagement level.

#### Mitigation Measure(s):

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 86

## Selected Risk Factor: 4. Procurement delays under FARM operationalization (Output 2.2)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and financial | High | High |

### Description
The project will establish a dedicated team of national and international procurement experts to execute this task. The team will closely monitor the progress of this task and apply adaptive measures to remove any operational glitches while at the same time applying FAO's standards and international best practices to ensure the quality and speed of this activity.

## Selected Risk Factor: 5. Social and environmental risks

### Description
The project has been designed with a focus on gender equality, as well as other social and environmental risks that could negatively impact the livelihoods of the project’s beneficiaries and the surrounding environment they depend on for their livelihoods. The gender action plan (GAP) will be developed in close collaboration with FARM (i.e., a climate adaptation asset acquisition mechanism), as its primary vehicle of transformation, the project is expected to have a positive effect on specific groups of people and individuals, especially women and other socially excluded minorities.

## Selected Risk Factor: 6. COVID-19 Pandemic

### Description
The full scale and severity of the COVID-19 pandemic are yet not known. However, what is clear that this global pandemic has had both immediate to mid-term effects on livelihoods and food security and has deeply affected the project’s operational environment. The pandemic has led to significant restrictions in movement of people and goods. These restrictions have caused significant delays and posed many logistical challenges for the project. In addition, some countries have imposed travel restrictions which could have a considerable bearing on the implementation of the project both operationally and technically.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
FAO, as the AC, will take stock of the immediate operational impacts from this current pandemic in the country, regionally, and globally to establish enhanced protocols and procedures for operating under similar restrictions to those currently in place. FAO will also work closely with the NCDs and co-CEOs where they have comparable protocols and procedures in place. FAO will use the NCD as its primary platform for coordination and collaboration for this purpose.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### 236 FAQ confirms that:
- The project activities will not be conducted in any jurisdiction which is subject to or affected by United Nations Security Council sanctions.
- No individual or entity that is listed on any UN Security Council sanctions list, including the UN Consolidated Sanctions List, will have any direct or indirect interest in the project or its activities as owner, counterparty, implementer, or beneficiary.

### 237 FAD will follow the findings and recommendations of the HACT micro assessment on co-EE (i.e., M&E and MAPP) and the results of the risk assessment. The project will ensure that all goods and services and equipment procured under this project are used only for the purposes intended and are not diverted or misused for unauthorized, illegal, or unethical purposes. This includes ensuring compliance with the GCF’s procurement policies and procedures, mechanisms, corporate policies on fraud and other corrupt practices, FAO Vendor Sanctions Policy (Admin Circular 04/15), and the UN Common Procurement Guidelines (CPG) at: <https://www.fao.org/procurement/en/> or <http://tinyurl.fao.org/departments/investigations> including those of the Office of the Inspector General (OIG) will be in place to ensure this.

### 238 The following summary highlights a few notable risks that may have potential diverse environmental and social impacts on the project:

- **High risk linked to the undertaking of project activities within Community Protected Areas (CPAs) (less than 10,000 ha) and Community Forest Areas (CFAs) located within National Parks and Wildlife Sanctuaries:**
  - Modification of land use for agricultural purposes, including conversion of natural vegetation into crop or forest enrichment planting as well as the establishment of planted forests – based on support to the provision of improved livelihoods through agriculture and/or forestry.
- **All risks linked to the undertaking of project activities within areas linked to agriculture and land management across the target landscapes through adoption of improved practices and provision of improved livelihoods for smallholder farmers:**
  - These may include environmental and social risks that have been fully hedged against as-usual levels even where linked to a risk area.
- **240 Further information on the risk areas identified and assessed as well as controls put in place is also provided in the due diligence section of the Environmental and Social Sustainability Strategy (ESS) while also linking back to the IFC Performance Standards, which the GCF provisionally adopted. The project’s risk management approach will also be based on the ESS and the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP). While the ESFME outlines the overall risk mitigation framework for the project, the ESMP provides a more detailed description at the operational level once specific beneficiary groups, locations, and standards are identified at the project inception stage.**

## FAQ ESS 6: Involuntary Displacement and Resettlement
No

The project will not allow any involuntary resettlement or displacement.

### A2C
- **A2C 1:** Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement
    - Although no specific impacts are foresee in proposed actions, an ESMP will be prepared once specific project interaction sites have been identified. This will ensure that all groups who will be displaced from their land will be adequately compensated.
    - The project will not result in the direct employment of A2C. Employment opportunities are likely to be generated through improved migration options for employment. All target groups are already established (A1C, PQA, and PCQA). An ESMP will be prepared once specific project interaction sites have been identified.

## FAQ ESS 7: Decent Work
No

The project will not result in the direct employment of A2C. Employment opportunities are likely to be generated through improved migration options for employment. All target groups are already established (A1C, PQA, and PCQA). An ESMP will be prepared once specific project interaction sites have been identified.

### A2C
- **A2C 2:** Labor and working conditions
    - The project will work within value chain landscapes that include subsector products and their other associated activities. These value chains are already established (A1C, PQA, PCQA). This will ensure that the existing community-based family units will be protected.
- **A2C 3:** Health, safety, and security
    - The project will work within value chain landscapes that include subsector products and their other associated activities. These value chains are already established (A1C, PQA, PCQA). This will ensure that the existing community-based family units will be protected.

## FAQ ESS 8: Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment
No

The project will support gender equality and women and has been designed to take into account the specific needs of women. The project will not result in any adverse impacts on women, but it is important that what action can be taken to address these. A gender assessment and Gender Action Plan have been developed for the project (Annex 6). It provides specific actions to address and an overall budget.

### A2C
- **A2C 4:** Assessment and management of gender equality and women's empowerment
    - The project will support gender equality and women and has been designed to take into account the specific needs of women. The project will not result in any adverse impacts on women, but it is important that what action can be taken to address these. A gender assessment and Gender Action Plan have been developed for the project (Annex 6). It provides specific actions to address and an overall budget.

## ESS 1: Indigenous peoples
No

There are over 40,000 people across 15 groups identified as living within the target projects based on their self-identification. The project will not result in any adverse impacts on indigenous peoples if it is implemented as proposed. However, a baseline Indigenous Peoples (IP) Plan has been prepared to ensure that IP rights are respected and protected during implementation of the project. If specific IPs for target areas being required during project inception should they include IP groups.

### A2C
- **A2C 5:** Indigenous peoples (IP)
    - There are over 40,000 people across 15 groups identified as living within the target projects based on their self-identification. The project will not result in any adverse impacts on indigenous peoples if it is implemented as proposed. However, a baseline Indigenous Peoples (IP) Plan has been prepared to ensure that IP rights are respected and protected during implementation of the project. If specific IPs for target areas being required during project inception should they include IP groups.

No

The project will not result in any adverse impacts on cultural heritage if it is implemented as proposed. However, a baseline Indigenous Peoples (IP) Plan has been prepared to ensure that IP rights are respected and protected during implementation of the project.

## 241 Based on this screening process, the ESMPs specific risks-based ESMP at the activity level will be prepared to establish specific management plans. Monitoring and reporting on the overall plan and specific sub-plans will be done through the project’s monitoring framework. The project will also have a grievance mechanism in place for the project’s Grievance and Redress mechanism that will integrate closely with those existing in Cambodia, including the GCF SEED+ system (see Annex 6 above).

## 243 Gender Analysis
This gender analysis is based on a combination of FAO and GCF global best practice and guidance frameworks as well as extensive stakeholder consultation that occurred during project formulation, including specific ESMPs for A2C.

### G.2: Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)
An analysis of the gender-related aspects of the project was also undertaken during the project formulation phase. The analysis identified a number of key issues that are relevant to women in various forms in legal documents, including the Constitution, specific policies and strategies such as the Five-Year Plan (2013-2017), the National Gender Equality Strategy 2014-2018, the National Climate Change Strategic Plan for Gender and Climate Change (2013-2023), the Land Law and the Marriage and Family Law (2001) and the Forest Law (2001) as well as guidance on community forestry and community protected areas.
