# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 90

### GENDER ACTION PLAN

244 While these frameworks have supported significant progress towards gender equality, there remains a number of projects that need to address gender and diversity issues in their target landscapes and value chains. The assessment has looked at these through the lens of areas linked to project outputs:

- **i.** gender gap in access to market opportunity, technology, climate info, risk info, and credit;
- **ii.** gender gap in decision-making power (which includes CPO, CIF, AC, FA, and value chain);
- **iii.** gender gap in capacity development and participation.

245 This assessment has used the above criteria to identify key actions within the gender action plan.

### Gender Action Plan

246 A detailed GAP has been prepared for the project (Annex 6). The GAP is structured on supporting a fully gender-sensitive and gender-positive approach through the project's activity areas. It is fully consistent with the GCF’s Gender Strategy, which sets out the principles of gender equality in the context of women’s agricultural cooperatives and associations. The GAP’s broader approach is based around strengthening the capacity of women’s agricultural cooperatives and associations to achieve their own development goals as well as working with the specific needs and conditions identified with the target value chains and beneficiary groups, taking into account the GCF’s gender strategy principles.

247 The GAP has been developed in close consultation with key stakeholders (including women) on expected outcomes along stakeholders, specifically Outcome 3. To ensure a more effective and gender-positive approach to project activities, the following actions have been identified as critical for success:

- **i.** Practical tools within the GAP include: ensuring new tools, systems, and support mechanisms are developed through a participatory process with women’s agricultural cooperatives and associations; ensuring that all project activities and within the project's own operating structures (trainings, FARM arrangements, membership of decision-making bodies, etc.) are gender-sensitive and promote inclusion (e.g., by ensuring equal representation in making bodies, regulation, and so) that these might become more gender-sensitive and promote better inclusion.

### Financial management and procurement

( max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

247 As indicated above, FACC as the AE for this proposed project, will assume fiduciary and procurement responsibilities in accordance with the GCF’s Operational Manual (OM) and Procurement Procedures Manual (PPM). The OM and PPM are subject to the relevant FAO rules and regulations, and to the terms of FAO's Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) with the GCF. FACC will ensure timely disbursement of the GCF funds and accounting upon production of financial reports on the use of funds.

248 This responsibility will also encompass financial management and procurement performed by the GCL/CME in accordance with the terms of the Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) between the GCL/CME and FACC under the Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfer (HACT), providing a common framework for UN agencies cash transfer operations. The HACT is based on the FAO’s Risk Mitigation and Assurance Plan (RMAP). Following the report's specific findings and recommendations, FACC will set clear parameters for each e-EoT to perform its financial management and procurement functions in accordance with the terms of the MoU under the HACT assessment mitigation measures as well as FAO’s RMAP Risk Mitigation and Assurance Plan (RMAP) will be used and updated on a regular basis. The GCL/CME will also have access to FACC’s dedicated resources, including a dedicated Operations Coordinator (see Table 11, Section B.4) for each e-EoT to support them and ensure financial compliance.

249 Further to approval of the project by the GCF, FACC will disseminate its Operations Manual and Procurement Procedures Manual among the members of the PSC, e-EoTs, core team of area experts, and other relevant stakeholders. The dissemination will be done in a manner that ensures full understanding of all aspects related to the institutional capacities of the PSC, PSC-eEoT, and GCL/CME in strategic planning, technical control, financial management, and procurement.

250 Direct procurement by FAO will be done in accordance with its Manual Section on “Procurement of Goods, Works, and Services.” To sub-contract the delivery of specific activities through IPs (see Section B.4) using Letters of

6.4 Accreditation of the proposal

- No confidential information: "The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF; no information is being provided in confidence."
- With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may only be disclosed to the extent necessary for the purposes of the GCF. In such cases, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, all including annexes:
  - A redacted copy for discussion with the GCF Secretariat and the PMU, accompanied by a cover letter with an explanatory note regarding the said version and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accreditation agreement.
  - Redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by FAO regarding Public-Social Private Partnerships for Ecologically-Sound Agriculture and Resilient Livelihood in Northern Teak Sap Basin (PEARL)

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the project titled "Public-Social-Private Partnership for Ecologically-Sound Agriculture and Resilient Livelihood in Northern Teak Sap Basin (PEARL)" in the Kingdom of Cambodia, as outlined in the funding proposal submitted by FAO on 21 February 2022.

The undersigned is the Secretary of State of the Ministry of Environment (MoE), the National Designated Authority of the Kingdom of Cambodia.

Pursuant to GCF decision 8.0/0.1, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby confirm our no-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal:

(a) The government of the Kingdom of Cambodia has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal;

(b) Taking into account that all aspects of the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, strategies and plans of the Kingdom of Cambodia,

(c) In view of the absence of any adverse social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objections to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP199

## Proposal name: Public-Social-Private Partnerships for Ecologically Sound Agriculture and Resilient Livelihood in Northern Tonle Sap Basin (PEARL)

### Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)
### Country/ies: Cambodia
### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remark:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
|-----------|-------------------|
| An innovative approach to agricultural asset acquisition mechanism that can give empowerment and financial resources, while incentivizing them to take collective actions in business implementation. | |

3. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions outlined on the proposed term sheet and addendum B titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations."

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

> *The Northern Tonle Sap Basin (NTSB) is one of Cambodia's most important agricultural regions, covering an area of approximately 30,000 km². The region has experienced a mean annual temperature increase by 0.8°C since 1950, while precipitation is showing a general decreasing trend with large inter-annual variability, particularly in the rainy season. The region is identified as one of the most vulnerable to climate change impacts, including increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts and floods, which are expected to occur more frequently with increasing intensity due to climate change. These anticipated changes will have a significant impact on agricultural production systems, particularly rice and vegetables.*

*The Public-Social-Private Partnerships for Ecologically Sound Agriculture and Resilient Livelihood in the Northern Tonle Sap Basin (PEARL) project aims to tackle these identified impacts of climate change. The project proposes to transform the current agricultural production and processing system into a more resilient one, with an emphasis on sustainable land management practices.*

*The project will achieve this objective through three interlinked outcomes: namely (1) climate forecast-based decision support systems for improved livelihoods in the NTSB; (2) enhanced market access and value chain development to increase income opportunities for smallholder farmers and other actors involved in local value chains, reducing poverty levels; and (3) capacity building of 450,000 smallholder farmers and other actors involved in local value chains, reducing poverty levels.*


---
**Page 105**
---

The number of food insecure farmers by 60 per cent compared with the baseline, and returning 7,600 hectares of tropical rainforests with improved ecosystems and ecosystem services.

5. The total project financing is USD 185,431, with a request for GCF grants of USD 36,231,981. Co-financing will be provided by Cambodia’s Ministry of Agriculture Forestry and Fisheries (MoAFF) in kind contribution of USD 70,000 as in-kind contribution by the Ministry of Environment (MoE) and USD 670,000 as in-kind contribution by the Royal University of Agriculture (RUA). The project is submitted under environmental and social safeguards Category II.

### Component-by-component analysis

**Outcome 1:** Farmers’ capacities are enhanced to manage climate impacts and reduce risks (total cost: USD 3.4 million; GCF cost: USD 3 million).

* The first outcome will deliver an advanced system of agrometeorological advisory services for the most vulnerable crops in sensitive areas. As adopted in the feasibility study, lack of reliable crop-specific agrometeorological forecasting and advisory services is a major constraint to farmers’ ability to adapt their agricultural practices to climate change impacts as well as to the NTSR. As previous projects have worked on installing hydro-meteorological stations in the country, the proposed approach to reach the “last mile” would be timely interventions to expand and tailor these efforts for climate adaptation.

* The outcome will bring along advanced Internet of Things technologies for data collection, processing, analysis, and dissemination of agrometeorological advisory services. With this outcome includes annual training for extension officers from various related government departments, it would be critical to provide targeted training support to farmers in the use and interpretation of these new technologies as well as climate change adaptation strategies and land planning. The outcome proposes to undertake this training through Farmer Field Schools and peer-to-peer learning among farmers. This approach has been successfully used by the MoAFF, as all have built experience of taking the same approach elsewhere in Cambodia, and the farmers on the ground are very familiar with it.

**Outcome 2:** Adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors, particularly vulnerable women farmers, is increased through market incentives that promote climate-resilient, high-value agricultural commodities and processing (total cost: USD 33.5 million; GCF cost: USD 10.8 million).

* Outcome 2 comprises four outputs: (1) increased premium market access through agricultural value chain development; (2) demonstration of climate-resilient, high-value technologies; (3) demonstration and promotion of climate-resilient, high-value and sustainable agroforestry systems at the farm level; and (4) demonstration and promotion of climate-resilient, high-value and sustainable agroforestry systems at the landscape level. The outcome will target in total 124 agricultural cooperatives, farmers associations, and other local value chain actors involved across the project life cycle.

* The key result of this output is to facilitate access for smallholder farmers to premium markets through a combination of market incentives and improved infrastructure and institutional support at farm and landscape levels. This outcome supports farmers to obtain certification for organic production and processing, as well as to improve their access to value-added products and services.

* Another key result under this outcome is providing agricultural infrastructure to farmers through a climate adaptation asset acquisition mechanism, namely Farmer-led Agricultural Infrastructure Development (FLAID). Funds from GCF grants will be used to support

1 For example: CamGAP, PUL GL SBF, ID2008H and RACOP.


---
**Page 106**
---

two areas: work capacity building for agricultural cooperatives, farmers associations and producer groups, to develop their financial literacy, entrepreneurial skills and business planning; the second area is to support the establishment of trust funds with local financial institutions.

10. Extensive discussions have been held between the AE and the Secretariat on the mechanics of the FARM model, focusing on bringing in innovation through financial sustainability. The proposed arrangement would be a revolving fund structure, including a revolving type of fund was discussed and designed with a repayment arrangement in exchange for asset creation. This revolving fund would be structured as a trust fund to support additional beneficiaries. That design had to be modified to fit the project financial structures and to accommodate the concept of a revolving fund; beneficiaries will set up their own trust funds using available local microfinance institutions, paying some fees and run the trust funds for continuous implementation of their business plans. Although monitoring and evaluation is an important part of this arrangement, since the trust funds that will be set up, this arrangement is deemed to be the most feasible option under the AE’s accreditation.

12. Overall, the outcome is critical to smallholder farmers to advance from their baseline to climate-resilient agriculture with value addition with greater opportunities to access premium markets. The project will also contribute to the development of a market-based commodity not deemed to be bankable by the private sector. NTSH is characterized by its high poverty rate and vulnerability to natural disasters, which are exacerbated by poor infrastructure and lack of proper agricultural production and processing infrastructure. With technical assistance support being provided through the project, it is expected that the proposed project’s activities will strengthen ARDH in widening its agricultural lending portfolio and increasing the take on the project by the end of the project (i.e., lending for procuring agricultural inputs).

Outcome 3: Regulatory and institutional frameworks and capacities for climate-resilient agriculture. The project will establish a clearing house to enable faster innovation and knowledge-sharing, which will be a cornerstone of the exit strategy and will drive the process of scaling up into other areas of the country through sharing lessons learned. The outcome will introduce new regulatory and institutional frameworks and capacity building in communication channels.

13. The project will establish a clearing house to enable faster innovation and knowledge-sharing, which will be a cornerstone of the exit strategy and will drive the process of scaling up into other areas of the country through sharing lessons learned. The outcome will introduce new regulatory and institutional frameworks and capacity building in communication channels.

14. Knowledge products and experience gained during the project will help to foster future innovation. An innovative financial structure will be developed to continue the work after the project ends, so that the strategy can adapt and cater for future challenges while scaling up sustainability.

Project management (total cost: USD 2.0 million; GCF cost: USD 1.7 million).

15. The GCF portion of the project’s management cost is less than 5 per cent of the total requested GCF funding and in compliance with the GCF policy on fees.

## 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

**Scope:** N/A


10. The project proposes three innovative outcomes that, when implemented jointly, will offer a strong long-lasting opportunity to help transforming the local food systems by targeting the current challenges of climate change and poverty in the region. These innovations pave the way for the target region to become a diverse and economically sustainable local food system with clear benefits for all stakeholders involved.

11. The targeted value chains offer a unique opportunity to further involve communities at the centre of their production and marketing while strengthening food security, job creation and sustainable livelihoods.


## 3.3 Sustainable development potential

**Scope:** N/A


10. The project will deliver important solutions to boost income streams through the proposed FARM model that will help small-scale farmers increase and foster innovative approaches.

These will help to generate jobs, stimulate local growth and boost markets and development of new and improved commodities, and processing and storage; as well as generate numerous benefits for the environment.


15. The project will support the design and implementation of innovative farming systems that will reduce soil erosion, improve water use efficiency, decrease the pressure on forests, and reduce water consumption and usage for rice production.


---
**Page 108**

3.4 Needs of the recipient

The project aims at supporting vulnerable community groups in northwest Cambodia, where livelihoods depend on production and sustainability of food systems.

Through these innovative outcomes and proposed activities the project will be able to lift the communities out of the poverty challenges they currently face and support them to develop more diversified and sustainable livelihoods with improved resilience to climate change impacts, and to boost their local food systems to provide opportunities to continue diversifying and reducing risks of food insecurity by establishing food systems and farming practices with innovative and strong community values.

3.5 Country ownership

In the northwest is an important region for food production in Cambodia but also one of the most vulnerable regions and the region with the country's highest poverty levels. The nationally determined contribution, as well as national strategies and plans in the agriculture sector, highlight the need to increase agricultural productivity and resilience to provide stronger, more resilient solutions for the region to transform itself into a resilient and strong food system.

ARIB will play a crucial role in implementing the project and in guaranteeing access to finance to community groups during the project and in the long term by acting as the pathway, knowledge hub and platform to ensure that farmers can successfully transition their food systems.

Country ownership is fully embedded into all aspects of the project and the three outcomes, and is one of the cornerstones of successful implementation of the project.


3.6 Efficiency and cost-effectiveness

The project will work with the farming communities in one of the key food-producing areas of Cambodia. The project offers a unique opportunity to build and reinforce the local food systems to become more diverse through local micro investments along the supply chains, and by creating new opportunities for value addition and income generation from post-harvest processing and storage as well strong involvement of the local and international private sector.


IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

Project Brief: The project aims to enhance the climate change resilience of smallholder farmers in northwest Cambodia through the SAG (Sustainable Agroforestry) model by increasing their access to the present market segments while using their improved market access to incentivize their transition to climate-resilient practices, mainly through effective public-private partnerships. This will be achieved by improving soil quality and crop yield through better residue management, enhanced agroecological conditions and ecosystem services, as well as by strengthening quality control and forest protection and restoration activities in the catchment areas.

Environmental and social risk category: The AII has assigned the project an environmental and social risk category of 1 (low), which reflects the low level of risk of the project's activities. The Secretariat agrees with this categorization given that actual ground


---
**Page 109**
---

interventions are small scale and spread out and are intended to be ecologically sound and sustainable. The risk category is also within the AE's environmental and social risk accreditation level. The environmental and social risks for activities involving restoration works and agricultural land use change are assessed through a combination of tools such as the Community Protected Areas (CPA) and Community Forest Areas (CFA) sites, and the application of proven best practices in agroforestry systems and sustainable agricultural activities. The project will also provide training to project beneficiaries involved in the implementation of these activities on land rehabilitation, agroforestry systems, and sustainable agricultural activities. Moreover, the project will promote energy-efficient facilities such as solar-powered irrigation pumps, photovoltaic panels, and other renewable energy sources.

### 15. Safeguard Implementation

The project has developed an Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF). The ESMF is the appropriate instrument for this project as site-specific information activities/valuer chain investment plans under Farmer-led Agricultural Rural Development (FLARD) projects are already in place. The ESMF will be implemented and developed through participatory processes with the beneficiaries during project implementation. The ESMF will be prepared once the activity and site-specific risks and impacts are identified.

### 16. Compliance with the GCF Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards

The project is expected to comply with all relevant aspects of the GCF's ESS standards.

#### ESS1 (Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts)

The ESMF provides a general assessment of the E&S risks and potential impacts of the project. The ESMF will also provide an overview of the various site-specific interventions which will be identified and developed with the beneficiaries during project implementation. The ESMF will describe the baseline conditions about the baseline conditions of the physical environment (i.e., climate, geology, soil), biodiversity, fauna, land use, water resources, and social conditions such as population density, ethnic groups. The ESMF also describes the process for assessing and managing the E&S impacts and risks related to the project activities. This will be done in three main steps: (1) activity identification; (2) E&S screening; and (3) preparation, implementation, and monitoring of the environmental and social management plan (ESMP).

#### ESS2 (Project Workforce Conditions)

The project is expected that the project will not involve direct hiring of workers. Nevertheless, depending on the place and types of work in the farm and forest areas, workers may be exposed to various occupational health and safety (OHS) hazards. For instance, workers in watershed restoration and protected areas may be exposed to hazards associated with working in remote locations such as snake bites, insect stings, contact with venomous animals, parasites, and vector-borne diseases. Workers in construction activities may also be exposed to a variety of occupational health and safety risks including exposure to noise, dust, and other airborne particles. Workers involved in agricultural activities are likely to be exposed to various other hazards such as repetitive motion, sharp tools, high temperature, among others. The project will develop an OHS management plan that will include the results of the E&S screening and assessment and the formulation of relevant management measures will have to be developed for each identified risk. The project will also provide training on how to support activities to extensions and extension staff supporting the implementation of activities that will involve workers, including the use of appropriate tools and equipment. The project will likewise provide training to project beneficiaries involved in the implementation of these activities on land rehabilitation, agroforestry systems, and sustainable agricultural activities.

#### ESS3 (Resource Efficiency and Pollution Control)

The project is not expected to use large amounts of chemicals or other hazardous substances. The project will promote good management practices, pollution control and invest in energy-efficient facilities (e.g., solar-powered irrigation pumps).


---
**Page 110**
---

powered water (facilities). The project is expected to promote climate-smart irrigation systems (e.g., drip irrigation and ponds) and develop schedules to optimize water resources. The sustainable land management practices that will be promoted by the project are also envisaged to prevent soil erosion, improve soil fertility, and enhance carbon sequestration. The risk related to pesticides use is also low given that the project will promote integrated pest management (IPM), which reduces the need for chemical pesticides and promotes agroecological approaches. The project will also develop guidelines and provide training to farmers to reduce their pre-existing use of pesticides based on international and local best practices. Good agricultural practices (GAP) and good manufacturing practices (GMP) are already part of agricultural certification programs such as the Organic Certification, the Cambodia Good Agriculture Practice (CGAP), the Global G.A.P., the International Organization for Standardization (ISO) 22000 on Food Safety Management System, and Hazard Analysis Critical Control Point (HACCP) principles.

### ES4 (Community Health, Safety and Security)

The project will involve members of the communities working and living near or within the CRAs and CFA. Hence, communities could be exposed to various risks such as accidents, injuries, and health hazards related to the restoration works in these areas, the construction of small infrastructure and enterprise facilities, and the use of pesticides. The ESMS will include a risk assessment for all activities, including construction-related hazards at construction sites, pesticide poisoning (in instances where people are exposed to pesticides), and other risks such as those associated with COVID-19. While the ESMS outlines the overall risk mitigation framework, more detailed assessments and preparation of the ESMS will be done at the activity level once the specific beneficiary groups and locations have been identified. The project will ensure that all activities are implemented with appropriate mitigation measures, as needed.

### ES5 (Land Acquisition and Resettlement)

The ESMP rules out any involuntary resettlement or displacement impacts from the project since it will not support activities that result in a negative change to existing legitimate tenure rights* and *will not involve the acquisition of land for the project. The project will also avoid *voluntary* displacement*, or*, economic and occupational displacement*, as provided in the project exclusion criteria. The project will ensure that all communities living within CRAs and CFA that have clear and identified production areas and work in CRAs and CFA that have adequate documentation of their tenure rights are not displaced by the project. Specific interventions and sites have been identified to ensure that no groups will be displaced from their land or livelihoods as a result of the project implementation. The project adheres to the principles of the Voluntary Guidelines on the Responsible Governance of Tenure of Land, Fisheries and Forests in the Context of National Food Security (VGGT) to clarify ownership and tenure rights.

### ES6 (Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources)

The project will also include the following activities: (a) activities that result in increases in areas under cultivation within protected areas; (b) activities that support the direct supply of agricultural products from CRAs and CFA to local markets or communities; and (c) activities that use non-locally adapted species. In addition, a Biodiversity Management Framework (BMF) will be developed for each CRA and CFA. The BMFs for CRAs and CFAs will be carefully selected in terms of management capacity, clear delineation of boundaries, and approved by the relevant authorities. The BMFs will include: (1) expansion of production areas in the CPA/CFA; (2) site-specific effects of agricultural activities; and (3) monitoring and evaluation of biodiversity conservation measures to be implemented in the project, including subjecting each intervention to E&S screening using FDA's Screening Checklist. Based on the result of the screening, an ESMP will be developed and will be monitored throughout the implementation phase


---
**Page 111**
---

### e2. Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESSE 7 Indigenous Peoples

At this stage specific activities have not been determined, so the AE has included an indigenous peoples and social inclusion planning framework (IPSIFP) within the environment and social management framework (ESMF). The IPSIFP is a tool to identify and address the indigenous peoples that are present in the project target provinces, analysis of the legal framework, and an identification of potential risks. These risks will be addressed through the development of a mitigation proposal on risks from (1) exacerbating inequalities, (2) exclusion and (3) pressure on Indigenous Peoples' lands. Information is included in the IPSIFP to guide the process of screening and preparation for project activities that may have an impact on indigenous peoples. The IPSIFP also contains information on meaningful consultations and free, prior and informed consent (FPIC) during project implementation.

### e3. ESS8 (Cultural Heritage)

The ESMF addresses potential project impacts on cultural heritage resources. It is important to ensure that the traditional knowledge are respected and benefits accruing from their use is shared. However, given the diversity of cultural heritage resources in the project area, it is not possible for the ESMF under the project should ensure that information about the location and status of both tangible and intangible cultural heritage resources is available. The ESMF also provides guidance on how potentially being adversely affected by project activities. Management measures including avoidance, minimization, compensation, mitigation, and enhancement are provided in the ESMF as well as the involvement of experts and authorities apart from communities in formulating any cultural heritage conservation plans if necessary should also be considered.

### e4. Social and Gender Mainstreaming

The SMIF has identified potential gender-based violence (GBV) and SEAR risks linked to the overall nature of the project, including the creation of new jobs, increased access to markets for smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors; it may also create power imbalances across project personnel and between project personnel and the target beneficiaries specifically when project beneficiaries are women. The SMIF provides guidance on how these risks can be addressed through contract farming agreements are created. The SMIF presents the current risks and mitigation measures for GBV and SEAR, which are linked to the overall nature of the project; it requires that specific SEAR risks be screened and addressed at sub-activity level guided by AE's new framework. The SMIF also provides guidance on how to ensure the appropriate reference to SEAR while the ESMF presents key mitigation measures on SEAR including to ensure the SEAR risks are identified, assessed, and managed in a manner that addresses gender vulnerability and provide training to participants on SEAR risks; enhance awareness on gender roles; and improve access for SEAR and GBV related grievances in the grievance redress mechanism. The SMIF also provides guidance on how to ensure that appropriate referral pathways to assure survivor-centred and gender-responsive support on SEAR incorporated in the project implementation.

### f1. Implementation Arrangements

The ESMF discussed the implementation arrangements for the project which involve the establishment of a Project Steering Committee (PSC) comprised of representatives from the AE, the PMU, and the GEF Secretariat. The PSC's main responsibility will be to provide strategic guidance and support coordination among the AE, PMU, and GEF Secretariat in order to ensure the implementation of the project. A PMU will also be established and will be responsible for, among others ensuring the implementation and monitoring of day-to-day activities, implementing the IPSIFP and ESMF, and providing technical assistance to communities and beneficiaries. A National Safeguard Specialist to lead the project's effort in mitigating negative

**Page 112**
---

### Gender policy

#### 4.2 Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Adopted Gender Policy.

The gender assessment describes the gender issues at the country level and indicates the existence of gender-based discrimination, supports national policies and plans to promote gender equality, and designates Cambodia’s commitment to gender equality and women's empowerment. The Government of Cambodia is committed to gender equality and women's empowerment as a key element in its development strategy. This is reflected through initiatives such as the ratification of the convention on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the establishment of the National Action Plan for Gender Equality 2014–2023, which aims to reduce gender vulnerability and risks from climate change and disaster risk management in all sectors. The plan also addresses women’s rights, minorities and other vulnerable groups. The second Gender and Climate Change Action Plan 2019-2023, and a master plan for gender and climate change (2018–2030) are being used as a roadmap to ensure the effective implementation of the Multi-Year Work Programme (MoWP). MoWP takes the role of promoting gender equality and women's engagement in all sectors. The AE will also work with relevant institutions at national level, and is expected to advance the commitments expressed in the national strategic plan. MoWP’s Gender and Climate Change Action Plan 2019-2023 includes a list of relevant institutions at all levels on gender mainstreaming with reference to climate change.

### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

The theory of change (ToC) clearly defines the project goal. The ToC’s past statements and the revised assumptions will be used to monitor progress more clearly articulate how the proposal's climate-resilient value chain approach and premium price market approach will address adaptation needs. The assumptions capture the most important factors underlying the achievement of the project goals. These assumptions are linked with the barriers that have been articulated and linked with the relevant set of activities to which each of these barriers respond.

The logic framework has been designed with relevant details, as per the GCF Integrated Results Management Framework (IRMF). The selected GCF results area and IRMF indicators will enable the project to monitor progress towards achieving its goals. The project will report against Goal 2 Indirect and direct beneficiaries reached; Supplementary indicator: 1.1. Climate-resilient livelihoods created; Supplementary indicator: 2.2. Beneficiaries (female/male) with improved food security, nutrition, and income from climate-resilient agriculture; Supplementary indicator: 3.1. Climate resilient freshwater and coastal marine areas brought under restoration and/or improved ecosystems.

At the GCF outcome level, the AIs has strengthened the means of verification for the IRMF indicators. The logic framework will be updated to include a baseline value where possible primary and secondary data sources, and quantitative and qualitative surrogates.

At the output level, the logicframe has been streamlined to focus only on output-level indicators that are directly linked with the project’s goals.

A few baseline figures at the outcome and output levels will be updated after a planned baseline study during the inception phase. Across the results level, the AIs will need to ensure that the baseline data it will specify for the baseline will match the suite of analysis of the mid-term and final targets.


---
**Page 117**
---

70\. The model Plan in Annex 11 sets out the data collection techniques for each indicator as well as the indicative budget for each. The AE provided the required clarifications related to the total costs for independent evaluative data collection costs, which have been consequently reflected in the project budget line in Annex 4 Project Budget in line with the requirements of the Evaluation Policy.

74\. The Evaluation will deliver an interim evaluation and a final evaluative assessment consistent with the requirements of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Accountability Dialogue Policy. The separate provision for evaluative data collection activities in the project budget and the increased evaluation budget as part of the AE fees provide the assurance that the AE will deliver interim and final evaluations that meet the requirements of the IRM and the Evaluation Policy.

### 4\.6 Legal assessment

71\. The accreditation master agreement was signed with the AE, and it became effective on 4 October 2018.

73\. The AE has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and that it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

75\. The proposed project will be implemented in Cambodia, a country in which GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, disbursements from the GCF are protected against litigation or expatriation in this country, which risks need to be further assessed. Disbursements from the GCF will be made only after they have been sent to the national designated authority of Cambodia by courier and received by the national designated authority on 30 August 2017. Negotiations on the draft privileges and immunities agreement have not yet commenced.

76\. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit both have expressed their concern that disbursements from the GCF may be subject to legal actions and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made only after they have been sent to the national designated authority of Cambodia for receipt and expatriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

### 4\.7 List of proposed conditions (including legal)

76\. In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

(a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat on or before the date of Board approval; and

(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP199

## Proposal name: Public-Social-Private partnerships for Ecologically-Sound Agriculture and Resilient Livelihood in Northern Tonle Sap Basin (PEARL)

### Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

### Country/[region]: Cambodia
### Project/programme size: Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: Low

#### 1.1.1 Cambodia is located in South East Asia, on the south-western part of the Indo-China peninsula and borders Vietnam to the west, Thailand to the southwest, Laos to the north and the Gulf of Thailand to the east.

#### 1.1.2 The country's economy has been predominantly an agrarian society, owing to its ecological endowment. The country is served by the Mekong River, which supplies freshwater, while the South East Asian monsoon brings in additional rainfall. Cambodia has a considerable temporal as well as spatial distribution of rainfall within Cambodia; the availability of freshwater resources is therefore critical.

#### 1.1.3 In recent times, Cambodia made great strides towards economic growth. Since 2004, the country's gross national income (GNI) tripled to reach US$589 in 2018. Published literature suggests that this increase was due to a combination of factors including increased foreign aid, public and private investments in various sectors, and favourable macroeconomic policies.

#### 1.1.4 Agriculture has been the mainstay of Cambodia's economy for decades. The sector experienced significant growth during 2004-2012, mostly due to rapid expansion of area under cultivation. The expansion was mainly driven by rice production, which is why the growth rate in crop sub-sector declined with respect to that during 2004–2012.

#### 1.1.5 Starting 2008-2009, Cambodia has focused on greater value addition in crops. The country's agricultural sector has been dominated by rice production for decades; the product being fragrant, lower yielding but capable of bringing home higher income for the farmers. However, the market for aromatic rice is limited to a few countries and the demand is growing. Aromatic rice is grown in orchards to catch international markets. Mangoes are also being grown for export as well as for local markets, looking for organically produced special variety of mangoes with a premium price tag.

#### 1.1.6 The farmers have been facing challenges due to lack of quality seeds and premium quality organically grown vegetables, again the farmers moved away from rice based farming to respond to market demand. The farmers are now focusing on organic farming practices.

#### 1.1.7 The premium-priced aromatic rice found a readily available export market in China, the smallholder farmers found it relatively easy to cope with post-harvest of rice, even establishing value chain for the same. The farmers were also able to get better prices from the market. In terms of total production, they accepted the apparent decline in yield involving aromatic rice, while the transformation promised them greater economic returns due to achieving premium quality seeds and organic farming practices.


---
**Page 119**
---

price. However, farmers could not readily access the growing international market for cashew, due to lack of know-how, adoption of technology, inadequate investment and lack of proper value chain. Most of the cashew grown in Cambodia is sold at respective farm gates in bulk to traders who then sell it on to processors or exporters.

### 5.
There is no denying the fact that the GNI of the country has reached USD1380 in 2018, but the country still belongs to the Least Developed Country (LDC) group. In 2009, the rate of poverty was 47% and the rural population was 65%. The urban-rural disparity is reflected by a disparity between rural and urban poverty rates: the poverty rate in urban areas is at 12.6 per cent while it is as high as 83.5 per cent in rural areas. This high level of rural poverty and the disparity in rural versus urban poverty can be attributed to high rates of engagement in crop agriculture, which often results in low productivity and income generation.

### 6.
In Cambodia, rice is the staple food for about 90% of Cambodians living in rural areas; there are other important crops: cassava, maize, soybean, mung bean and other crops. Cambodia is famous for its high quality rice, which is exported to many countries around the world.

### 7.
Cambodia is also vulnerable to climate change. There are changes in the climatic system. The average surface temperature has risen by 0.5°C between 1950 and present time. The most rapid rise of temperature occurred during the last decade, with an increase of about 0.2°C per year.

### 8.
It is claimed that the micro-structure of rainfall pattern in Cambodia has been changed, which is exhibiting an early departure of the season resulting in extension of the dry period, short rainy season (June to August) and relatively heavy intensity during late parts of the rainy season (July–September) and relatively lesser intensity during early parts of the rainy season. The funding proposal submitted by the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (MAFF) has not been able to substantiate these claims. Despite the fact that the country has an official hydro-meteorological data repository, it is unable to provide reliable information on rainfall patterns in Cambodia (i.e., rainfall). The funding proposal reportedly could not have access to such data and it resorted to citation of other studies which are based on limited data. As a result, no conclusive statement about availability of data, no significant trend temporal analysis regarding climatological phenomena could be presented. Available literature also does not provide evidence of climate change's subtle changes in climatology.

### 9.
According to inter-governmental panel on climate change (IPCC), the recent rainfall variability is attributed to a number of factors including El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) which may be found in an analysis involving evapo-transpiration. Although a shorter epoch of 20 years has been wrongly cited as a link to elinian linkage with climate, the funding proposal presented by MAFF has not provided any evidence that ENSO is linked to Cambodia's seasonal rainfall (SFR). To exhibit the presence of drought in Cambodia, high intensity rainfall during peak rainy season and low intensity rainfall during dry season are required. The funding proposal does not provide such evidence.

### 10.
Apart from rainfall-related hazards, a rise in surface temperature is already broad. Although there has been no significant increase in mean annual temperature due to climate warming has been found to increase the number of occurrences of reaching temperatures thresholds for crop growth stages. Temperatures above certain optimum threshold may cause problems in terms of yield reduction and quality deterioration, delay growth, withering, etc. Eventually, crop yield is found to suffer due to such incidences.

### 11.
The projection of future climate with respect to baseline of 1980-2005 is derived from Coupled Model Inter-comparison Project's fifth phase experiment (CMIP5) outputs reserved for


---
**Page 120**
---

near future timeline of 2011-2040 using IPCC-promoted scenarios of Representative Concentration Pathways 4.5 (RCP4.5). The downsampling was done by Coordinated Regional Downscaling Experiment (CORDEX). Six CMIP models were run on a course domain (i.e., using 85-degree resolution) to generate the climate projections for this region.

10. The modeling outputs exhibit much prominent rise in temperature, with more frequent exceedance of critical thresholds for heat stress and evaporation. This will likely be accompanied by increased concentration of greenhouse gases in the atmosphere. The annual precipitation is projected to increase slightly (by 2-3%), however such a slight increase will not be able to dent rising evapo-transpiration, resulting in increased moisture stress.

11. In terms of post-harvest crop agriculture, the model outputs indicate the following risks associated with climate change in the near future: (a) the heat stress due to ambient temperature rise and more frequent exceedance of critical thresholds for heat stress will be negligible in near future will only be negligible; (b) increased reference evapo-transpiration might induce water stress for crops, especially for wet-season aromatic rice, only negligibly at the plant establishment stage. However, this stress is likely to increase during the reproductive and harvest might require supplementary irrigation, as the available rainfall late in the season might not be appreciable.

12. The project aims at increasing crop resilience and simultaneously addressing the issue of post-harvest marketing by establishing value chains so that the smallholders, including women-headed households, can have greater export earnings. By choice, the project targets four northern Tonde Bay Sagin (TBS) regions: (1) the north-western region of Ogbia Mezobokom, which is known as a high-value vegetable producing area; (2) the north-eastern region of Ogbia Mezobokom, which is also a high-value vegetable producing area; (3) the Tonde Bay Sagin region north of the Tende Say lake, which is known as a high value vegetable producing area; and (4) the Kananga region just south of Pvirah. These regions are the major center for producing cashews. The relative production of cashew nuts in these four regions is significantly higher than that of other parts of the rest of the country. However, the level of poverty in these target regions is found to be generally higher than those in other parts of the country.

13. In addition, the project will consider the vulnerability of the smallholders and accounts of climate related vulnerabilities of these target regions to justify their inclusion, however this is not a pre-requisite for inclusion as it is considered that there is sufficient justification of considering a climate change related project in these target regions.


14. From the weak targeting of project locations, the approach of the project is found to be away from the “sensitivity” to climate change induced hazards” to build adaptive capacity mostly through awareness and knowledge creation. The project will not focus on developing new products so that the producers get a fair share from premium price-of specific products. By examining various options in the proposed project, it was found that the primary target of the project is to enable the smallholders to produce more and for greater export earnings. As reported in paragraph 2 above. In addition, the project will not focus on developing new varieties or technologies that follow a certain pattern. However, the project will not connect agricultural research in a bid to develop varieties that are adjusted to shifts in seasonal patterns so that smallholders can switch to alternate

### Varieties as well when necessary. It appears rather obvious that, if the current production system intensifying the target crop types get affected due to increased exposure and sensitivity to changes in climate system, the intended advisory, awareness, training, capacity-building and strengthening of marketing skills will be not of much use towards reducing vulnerability.

The project has three following target outcomes:

- Outcome 1: The quality and coverage of agrometeorological advisory services tailored to target value chains improved among smallholder farmers and local; value chain actors, particularly women farmers and value chain actors.
- Outcome 2: The productivity and profitability of smallholder farmers and value chain actors is increased through market incentives;
- Outcome 3: Regulatory and institutional frameworks and capacities are strengthened.

### Activity 1.1.1: Mobilize Technical Working Group (TWG-AW).

- Upgrade existing hardware; and
- Conduct annual training programme.

### Activity 1.1.2: Develop Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) for the production and dissemination of agrometeorological advisory services and data sharing:

- Mobilize Technical Working Group (TWG-AW);
- Establish central data base;
- Conduct annual training;

### Activity 1.1.3: Awareness raising regarding benefit in farm management and value chain activities:
- Prepare and roll-out of training of trainers (TOT); and
- Support peer to peer knowledge sharing and training.

The interview with the Director of DMA, Mr. Gervais Vilainque, as described above, singly or collectively, to effectively reduce either exposure or sensitivity to climate change induced hazards and impacts on smallholder farmers and value chain actors are regarded by the proponents as the "key responses" to directly reduce climate change induced vulnerabilities and risks; the impacts of the project will be negligible low.

**Page 122**

### The Independent TAP went on to enquire whether insurance of agronomic-climatological advisors could be effectively delivered from a virtual context, given the state of climate modeling and capacities within the country.

The AE was reminded about the logical steps towards achieving this objective: (a) development of an appropriate model for the project including model parameterization and fine tuning; (b) generation of computer-aided forecasts, perhaps by using decision support systems; (c) validation of the forecasts (suggested by hazard type and seasonality); (d) formulation of advisories and messages based on the validated forecasts; (e) dissemination of these advisories through a national contextual application; (f) developing regulatory aspects including institutional protocols for advisory dissemination (in order to avoid redundancy and establishing authority so that farmers would be able to distinguish between different sources of information).

The AE was asked whether the six years of implementation of the project would be adequate to perform all these steps. The AE expressed concern about the time required to identify vulnerabilities.

The AE expressed that given their association with other projects, it would be possible for them to provide some support in terms of capacity building and dissemination. However, even if the activities under Output 1 were critically important to deliver direct vulnerability assessments and advisories, they would not be able to complete the project and the realization of the positive impacts would not be possible within the stipulated time-frame.

The project identifies 135,000 smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors as the direct beneficiaries. The indirect beneficiaries are expected to reach over one million people who would receive agro-meteorological advisory services under Outcome 1 above. The total direct beneficiaries of the project (Output 2) is estimated at about 675,000 people.

This project is primarily a capacity-building focused project; its indirect beneficiaries are expected to reach over 1 million people, which is about 8 per cent of the population of Cambodia. It is expected that 90 per cent of these will be smallholder farmers.

In the target regions, it is reported that 65 per cent of the farmers were involved in rice production and 35 per cent in other crops (Outcome 1). In the last five years, however, recently some farmers have switched to organic aromatic rice farming, again their agronomic requirements are by and large not met by current climate. In the larger agricultural scene, cashew, mango and rubber plantations are also being affected by climate change.

Therefore, vulnerability assessments need to be done for these crops as well. The extent of vulnerability to these crops in current extent of climate change is still low. Therefore, direct vulnerability assessment will not be possible under Outcome 1 (2023-2024). However, there is a financial opportunity under Outcome 2 (activity 2.2.1) and employ either irrigation or create some other form of water storage to mitigate the effects of climate change.

The project has identified a number of areas where it can provide support in terms of agronomic-climatological advisors (Outcome 1), awareness raising (throughout the project), and watershed management (Outcome 3). The project will also be able to contribute towards development of regulatory and institutional framework (Outcome 3) which will have indirect benefits, cumulative over time. Much of these benefits will depend on selection of right information package, appropriate dissemination channels and level of participation of particular participants, especially in the planned watershed management.

The project is estimated at a total cost of USD 108 M, USD 95M, 231 where GCF is expected to contribute USD 36.231,981 only.

Although the project is targeting smallholders in an LDC, due to weak linkage with climate change, the derived results might not be achievable in view of climate change-related hazards and risks. The Independent TAP’s opinion that the impact potential of this project is low.


### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: Low to medium

The project is expected to intervene in areas where there is adoption of domestic premium rice, vegetables, high value mangoes and cashews. The climate advisors, awareness raising messages, manuals for inputting training of extension workers involving NGOs and public communication materials are already in place. These are being used by the farmers even without the additional financing of GCF. The right institutions are already part of the project. Clearly, the project is not about providing inputs or other resources to build such tools towards building adaptive capacities; the project clearly refrains from any expansion of area under production. However, in addition to the above-mentioned activities, regarding environmental nuances which may be extremely sensitive to location-specific realities, could be quite risky. Moreover, if a larger group of smallholder farmers are persuaded to grow the same product, it is likely that they will face competition with other producers and also face their own tree-competition, as supply will tend to interfere with market price. Therefore, scaling up merit in this case.

24. There is a theory of change. However, as explained earlier, the focus of the project is deliberately placed on capacity-building of smallholders, if not for safeguarding their production systems, then at least for ensuring that they are aware and informed about their smallholder's possessory very little know-how. The proponents have given high priority on Outcome 2.1.3 which states that the project will provide farmers with meteorological advisories as a mechanism to empower farmers with knowledge regarding an impending climate-change induced hazard. This is in line with the theory of change, which assumes that the information provided by the project so that farmers can make the best use of advisories and safeguard their production systems. The assumption is that if the farmers are aware about the hazards and preferred products will be climate safe, following the issuing of an advisory. The independent TAF is of opinion that, if the product process remains as vulnerable, no matter how the post-harvest handling is improved, it would not be possible to safeguard the production systems.

25. Responding to advisories is effective when the lead-time is sufficient to tweak agricultural practices and/or plant varieties. The project has a very limited capacity of alternate variety that are simultaneously profitable. Farm level hydrological realities can change upon issuance of advisories, only if catchment-scale governance on watershed management is in place. This is not the case with respect to land use rights and water rights. Hazards such as floods in the Mekong river and its associated hazards on cropping cannot be addressed by providing farmers with meteorological or hydrological advisories. There is no concrete intervention proposed under the project that might go beyond awareness raising, which is a very limited capacity of the project. The smallholder farmers address a hazardous situation on their own. Without having any indication how these challenges are being addressed, it would be difficult to assume that providing farmers with varieties which might be equally acceptable as premium products, sharing advisories will be equally useful to reducing vulnerability of the farmers.

26. Since there is no concrete intervention in terms of capacity of almost all the stakeholders, there is plenty of potential for knowledge and learning. The training for trainers, the training of extension workers, awareness raising messages, manuals for inputting training of extension involving central and regional level decision-makers—all will be useful in raising overall awareness levels. Majority of the financing for the project is going for conducting these events. Perhaps it would make more sense to focus on capacity building of advisory groups only.

The funders have not been very clear regarding adequacy of lead-time or the role of advisories. The micro-scale models which are used globally for the purpose, can be precise if lead time is less than five days. However, in case of climate change induced hazards such as floods and droughts on crops. The forecast accuracy diminishes drastically as the lead time is extended. The probability of occurrence of a hazardous event is not certain; it is only an indication. If the advisory is issued too late, there are risks associated with failure of occurrence of a predicted agro-meteorological event. The farmers might not even trust the advisories provided by the advisory service providers. The issue regarding lead time is critical to ensure meteorological advisories.

### Sustainable development potential

**Scale: Medium**

The project has elements which will contribute to sustainable developments, especially those aspects related to climate change adaptation and capacity-building needs are strong (3/5). The following direct linkages to contribute to various Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) are worth highlighting:

- SDG 1 (End poverty by increasing household income): By increasing income primarily by participating in capacity-building for post-harvest practices and value chain management, especially in areas such as Oddar Meanchey where the rate of poverty is higher than national average).
- SDG 5 (Achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls): By leveraging women farmers' participation in capacity-building activities.
- SDG 13 (Take enhanced management action to combat climate change) by providing agro-meteorological advisories, by raising awareness on climate resilient agriculture (CRA), by developing a framework for integrated water resource management, and by creating financial support for the development of micro-insurance schemes. The project also aims at the management of drought and/or ensuring access to credit to protect high value products from rotting in storage.
- SDG 15 (Protect, restore and promote sustainable use of terrestrial ecosystems): By setting up a framework involving investments in averting climate risks of agricultural production, which may or may not lead to an action contributing climate change.

**Note:** In addition to the direct linkages mentioned above, there can be other indirect modalities of contributing to SDGs. For example, contribution to SDG 6 (ensure availability and sustainable management of water and sanitation) through the development of a landscape-level Agro-Ecology Monitoring System (LAMS) with an interactive web-based data repository (activity 3.2), which is expected to contribute to achieve integrated water management in the downstream areas of the project. The framework will also be developed under SDG 15, as a framework for sustainable land management and soil conservation. The implementation of the framework is not guaranteed and no subsequent financing is earmarked in the budgetary allocation of the project, which is a limiting factor in this pursuit. Contribution to achieving SDG


---
**Page 126**
---

## Social co-benefits

The project is also about promoting farmers, especially women farmers and traders, in numerous rural communities. Activities will contribute to the cohesion of the social fabric. Particular reference may be made to the formation of farmer groups and activities under the former community-based management (CBM) projects. In particular, it will support women such as women producers in value chain delivery under the proposed FARM LiB is likely to boost social inclusion.

### Economic co-benefits

The project is all about optimizing economic benefits from strengthening value chains involving primary agricultural products. If climate change do not create new hazards, then the economic benefits are expected to be positive. However, in the near term, a no-hazard scenario means much increased cash flow into the farms. It also means that farmers will have more money to invest in their farms and in other activities such as value exchange - a much coveted objective for a Government leading an LDC. Since the project documents are based on a no-hazard scenario, it is important to note that this is hypothetical, and the projection results indicate increased variability and change in climate systems, a no-hazard scene does not appear to be plausible. Therefore, the economic co-benefits to be realized necessarily depend upon the ability of farmers to adapt to the new hazards associated with climate change induced hazards and risks. Hence reliance on issuance of agr-meteorological advisories cannot ensure that the project will deliver its expected economic benefits. A high-risk low dividend unless risk averting tangible adaptation results are obtained against hazards that affect livelihoods.

### The opportunity to access FARM resources for the "graduation" from poverty has an underlying assumption that, upon receipt of soft measures such as awareness and capacity-building, farmers will be able to take up the hard measures. These are expected under the institutional platforms such as LAMS and they will be able to implement CRA on their own initiative. The project is not designed to provide direct support for the establishment of land management opportunities in the localities. There are climate driven phenomena such as floods, droughts, and cyclones that can cause damage to crops and livestock. For example, flood can wreck havoc in the agricultural scene in the northern Toliara Sub-basin, unless flood are controlled at source. The project is not designed to provide flood control of the project; however the risk around economic gains might be significantly reduced under such circumstances. An analysis involving the bigger project needs to be carried out before accounting for this.

### Environmental co-benefits

In a focus project on capacity-building, direct contribution to environmental co-benefit is difficult to come by. Following awareness and capacity building activities, farmers will be better informed about climate change but may not take necessary measures, especially when economic gain is the driving factor. Despite such uncertainties, there are some indirect benefits that can be attributed to the environmental co-benefits. The primary contributor will be organic farming practices, which gradually decrease reliance on chemical inputs and fertilizers. This will contribute to a reduction in pollution. It is hoped that international best practices and standards are followed as closely as possible. By definition, such practices are usually less harmful to environment; if not completely benign to facilitate environmental harmony.

### Improved agro-ecological connectivity and consistency is the ultimate target of activity 2.4 which aims at creating an anthropogenic landscape mosaic. The project will create a platform and build capacity to achieve this, although the project does not plan for associated activities beyond facilitating the platform on catchment and watershed management. Since there is no follow up activity being designed, the project has refrained from estimating emission gains


---
**Page 127**
---

from forest conservation and subsequent sequestration. The gender-responsive LAMS is supposed to be another platform which is being promoted by the project, which is expected to provide for a data base that will eventually help the management of landscape level agro- ecology in the country. The other two platforms are those that are limited to the creation of platforms and promotion of their use through capacity-building.

* The value chain activities will help marketing of premium products even though the biodiversity-based production systems in subsistence agriculture was limited, only proportional to national average values. However, with additional efforts on the part of the project, it is expected that the carbon footprint for export of carbon based chemical inputs and on the other, the carbon footprint for export of the premium product will be reduced significantly. In case of the premium product, if the project takes into consideration, the average product carbon footprint might escalate significantly, for which GCF might find the project about to tarnish its green image in global level.

* Gender equality is expected to be a major strength and women's roles in the value chains is likely to be the greatest strength of the project. Women are expected to play central roles in the project. The project will provide training on various aspects including CCA, responding to agro-meteorological advisories, receive information from LAMS, accessing finance using FARM opportunity to consider measures to strengthen their role in the value chain and access markets for their products. They will also be trained in organic farming, including global identification of premium products etc. All these opportunities are available to women.

* The project must be mindful regarding the fact that, women producers as potential key stakeholders in the project are also socially active individuals. Since a lot of arrangements will have to be made for their participation and engagement in the project activities, it is suggested arranging local level training and awareness building sessions need to be designed with utmost care so that their participation and engagement can be ensured and sustained over time.

* The number of beneficiaries including female beneficiaries is significant. The target area is quite large in size. Such information suggest that the project should have significant institutional capacity to manage such a large scale project. However, given the geographical there are risks that may negate the contribution to sustainable development. In view of the opportunities and constraints, the independent TAP finds the sustainable developments potential of the project as medium.

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: Medium to high

* **Gambia is an LDC:** The majority of its population is dependent on agriculture for their livelihoods. However, people's dependence on rain-fed agriculture is continuing, which is why their production capacity remains low. The poverty rate factor in terms of the population of 15.2 million (2020), with a per capita Gross Domestic Product in US$486. The country is lagging behind in many social indicators, as reflected in the human development index of 0.59, which represents a low level of human development. According to the Human Development indicator, the ND-Gain Index score is 38.7 and a ranking of 149 out of 184 countries. The country has a high vulnerability to climate change.

* **The NTB:** comprises of provinces, a few of which have higher poverty rates than the rest of the region. The region is home for 20 per cent of the country's population and one third of its agricultural land. However, the economic development in this region is poor, owing to inadequate income from agriculture-based livelihoods. In the backdrop, there has always been the flood susceptibility of the basin, owing to direct influence zone of the


---
**Page 128**
---

mighty Mekong River. It flows heavily in the upper catchment: much of it is outside the borders of Cambodia, the river overflows the valleys and floodplains. As rain is the mainstay of crop agriculture, heavy floods generally take toll on standing rice crops. In recent times, however, the frequency and intensity of flooding have increased significantly. This has been increasing, along with heat stress and resultant increase in evapo-transpiration, all of these changes in climate are expected to result in significant reduction in yield per unit area or portion.

The country and its NTSB needs international support to address climate related changeable vulnerabilities and to find ways for the farmers including female farmers to increase farm profitability. The current market conditions for agricultural products and especially organic agricultural products are of great concern. Aromatic rice produced in organic conditions finds its market, however, it is not easy for Cambodian producers to access this market due to lack of access for accessing greater international market and fair prices. The post-harvest management and subsequent marketing processes need to be improved so as to get better price than its current bulk farm gate price. The current marketing processes leading to the sale of aromatic mangos are not conducive enough to fetch premium prices, unless proper interventions are designed and implemented. This is because the market for this product is very small and requires careful planning, which is difficult for Cambodia to plan and finance without the international support.

Investment in value chain is often committed by private sector, especially if the products are nationalized or controlled by the government. The Government of Cambodia, however, Governments create enabling policy environment so that the private sector can make profit while providing public goods. In this case, the private sector has been investing heavily into value chain has been controlled by unorganized sector from neighbouring countries, who rely on informal trade and are not subject to any regulation. This is because the government is willing to impose regulatory aspects related to standards applied for organic products for export markets. In both cases, the Government of Cambodia has no control over the private sector. Assuming that the products will be made available by defying adverse climate conditions, the Government of Cambodia needs to find ways to streamline its value chains. To proceed with the daunting task in the absence of proper institutional arrangements, it is important to seek and international source to mobilize finance.

The current institutional arrangement where climate change additionality is explained and understood. Ideally, investments going into strengthening the production in the face of climate change related adversities are welcomed by GCF. In those cases, the needs of the recipients are given high priority.

The value chain related institutional mechanisms are proposed to be strengthened by the project. The project will also seek to strengthen the capacity of the Ministry of Agriculture and strengthen capacity of allied national institutions. However, the exit strategy has little practical relevance towards restoring newly built capacities, especially modeling related capacities, beyond the implementation period of the project. This is because the current institutional human resource deployment structures do not generally guarantee retention of modeling capacities. The latter is also true for other capacities that are being developed under this project. It will be considered to ensure sustainability of the system well beyond the implementation timeline of the project. However, it should be noted that there may be a need to continue to pay for retention costs for services (unless open access codes are being used), O&M costs for equipment and other related costs. The latter is also true in case of capacity development where there will be continued financial mobilization once the project's finance is exhausted. The institutional improvement approach is also dependent on such arrangements, in addition to build human capacities through training.

The needs of the recipients appear high to medium.
