10. The Royal Government of Cambodia is keen on implementing a project such as PEARL in 2013, when Cambodia launched its climate change strategic plan 2014-2023. The concerns and priorities involving climate change is embedded in the national strategic development plan of Cambodia (NSDP) for the period 2016–2025. The project aligns with the NSDP’s sectoral action plans, its mandate-specific climate change action plans (2014-2018) in the light of NSD. The project is aligned with the national development strategy and the country’s poverty reduction strategy and its linkages with national documents in relation to climate change, which includes the following: the National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (NCCSAP), the NDCs, and the consultative process etc. The project is also considered as a priority project under Cambodia's GCF Country Programme. While the objectives concerning safeguarding agriculture in NSDP through adaptation measures are clear, the lack of an integrated approach to value chain development and value chain with minimal support for adaptation makes the understanding rather confusing. The climate change component of the project is not well defined and it is difficult to identify whether financial profit by generating premium market.

11. FAO is a specialized body under the charter of United Nations, designated for its role in promoting food security and nutrition, and improving food safety. FAO is committed to meeting the 2030 agenda for achieving sustainable development and in this process, it focuses on sustainable agriculture and rural development. The country office of FAO in Cambodia has been operating since 1979. It has extensive experience in working with the government and other stakeholders including ministries including the ministry of agriculture, forestry and fisheries (MAFF) as well as ministry of environment and natural resources management (MENR). The two main implementing entities are the Royal Government of Cambodia is the other EE, working through the MAFF and the MoF as the two co-Executing Entities (Co-EEs).

12. The Ministry of Agriculture will drive the agriculture agenda within the project. Although the value chains for different products are being managed primarily by the private sector, the MAFF has a mandate to ensure that the agricultural sector is sustainable and contributes to value chain activities. There are no distinct features found in the analysis of capabilities of the particular Co-EE (i) in role in boosting agricultural production, including its extension services and research and development (R&D), (ii) in providing an enabling environment for frameworks for value chain, the regulatory functions of which the ministry has loopholes, which is why the optimal performance of the project cannot be achieved. However, the majority of the activities of PEARL project are targeted to address the weaknesses of the Co-EEs and not the capabilities.

13. The MoF is a fully new ministry compared to other ministries with specific legal mandate. The DCC has been assigned with the overall responsibilities to deliver one of the key responsibilities under the project: the development and delivery of climate information services. The MoF is also responsible for providing support to FAO in realizing its responsibility regarding the advisors. The country has necessary knowledge base and capacity to provide technical support to the advisor, including weather forecasting such as weather forecasting. However, it does not have a proven capacity to provide technical support to MoF on model driven forecasts with adequate lead time. In any case, FAO is responsible for providing technical support.

# Efficiency and effectiveness

## Scale: Low to medium

The project is proposed to be implemented in six years with a total budget of USD 430,213,516, of which USD 36,233,981.00 is requested from GCF as grant. Considering the Cambodia’s current GCF allocation rate (GCF/total funding) and the proposed co-financing amount of USD 6,618,724,500 with a co-financing ratio with respect to GCF support is 0.93.

The independent TAP finds the major focus of the project is on building capacities of various kinds. With an exception of building capacities in value chain premium products, private sector engagement and market access, the other areas are not well defined. The Royal Government of Cambodia is required to finance in aspects related to poverty reduction and socio-economic development. This is a significant amount of co-financing for a grant project such as this, which is why GCF financing is requested with full concessionality. Given the GCF financing is concessional, it is expected that the co-financing will be provided by the Royal Government.

An economic and financial analysis (EFA) is attached with the funding proposal. It is assumed that 60 per cent of the target beneficiaries will be able to adopt climate-resilient and high value agricultural practices. The estimated total cost of the project, including direct support and economic benefit of such beneficiaries is listed at farm gate level. The analysis considers a 20-year period for the project (15 years for implementation and 5 years for monitoring [20 years]) is estimated at USD 83 million. This indicates that the project is viable in financial terms. The financial internal rate of return of the project is estimated at 387 per cent, while the overall economic net present value is positive.

The robustness in the financing is tested in the EFA. If the adoption rate increases the beneficiaries will be able to generate more income and thus the benefits from the project can diminish. The financial internal rate of return estimated against assumptions of 20 per cent increase in implementation costs and separately, 20 per cent reduction in benefits give strong signal that the project is robust.

The independent TAP recognizes the ambition on the proposed financing program under FCPF/AGM. It is also recognized that the project has a good potential for delivering exposure and sensitivity to climate change induced hazards and risks. However, the proposal lacks the in-depth assessment of agri-commodity market from both the formal and informal sources, from the perspective of both supply and demand. The proposed co-financing amount has not presented an informative baseline on issues regarding sustainable finance and potential challenges such as transaction costs, liquidity, credit risk, counterparty risk, transparency, accessibility, affordability, and acceptability remain unanswered. Proposing a solution in the form of a financial instrument is premature without addressing these issues. The evidence is weak. Without these, the effectiveness of this innovation involving FARM in the agri-finance space remains to be seen, as the pure financial market assessment may not correctly address the sustainable finance issues, challenges, barriers, and opportunities that will contribute to the


---
**Page 131**
---

(partial adaptation impact on the proposed interventions under Outcome 2 (i), the paradigm shift, because without finance it is not sustainable, (ii) needs of the recipients. The FARM model, for example, is not clear if this is addressing or creating another unintended consequence in the finance sector – potential crowding out of the financial services most vulnerable farmers.

### One of the potential impacts of the project based on significant provisions to create greater value chain infrastructure, where the financial requirement might not be covered with the maximum allowable provision of FARM. Since value chain infrastructure in Cambodia is still at a very early stage and there are no existing market linkages for premium products, unless larger scale value chain facilities are in place, value chain efforts considered at small-scale level will be difficult to sustain. The project should consider that the financing window caters the financing needs on the demand side, not addressing the potential needs of the target group. By creating a more enabling environment and putting in place value chain infrastructure at all levels might reduce the effectiveness of value chain development.

### The key question is, can this project catalyse private sector financing following the project’s completion? If the project does not address the underlying constraints in relation to product marketing outside farm gate coupled with lack of financing in in-country development of value chain infrastructure, the existing value chains operating at a much lower cost than the target group will continue to dominate. This will further reduce the farmers’ income values in proper value chain development. Farmer’s know-how on agro-meteorological advisories and other advisory services is not well developed as they are still focusing on institutional development through capacity-building and developing platforms (systems). The project should consider that the existing service providers are weak. These institutions need to be strengthened so that their roles in governance are integrated with all the stakeholders involved, including the private sector. This will ensure that they do not provide any answer regarding unauthorized cross border trade, involvements of middle-men, lack of cash from investments, and so on.

### The project should also address farmers to Outcome 1 (mainly agro-meteorological services and other advisory services) and farmers under Outcome 2 (e.g., premium products). This will help in reducing the existing income disparities as well as wider income disparities between the least and most vulnerable farmers. The AE, its financial and economic analysis did not clearly highlight the NPV differences from the two farmer groups.

### An important consideration is that the project should address the environmental externalities by promoting organic farming, especially involving rice and cashew. However, the present (2015) market conditions do not support this approach as the current market cannot be achieved without increasing carbon footprints of such premium products. Export of mite products generally brings home additional income; however it may pay an environmental cost. A possible solution is to consider a mixed model that can achieve the desired outcome at GCF.

### However, capacities for the agro-meteorological advisories is a highly technical issue and cannot be approached without following international best practices. The project starts with upgradation of its computational arrangements. However, this must be matched with enhancements in terms of human resources (e.g., training of new staff, recruitment of trained human resources) and computational software and algorithms (which needs to be updated on a regular basis). This will ensure that the project can achieve the desired impact and fulfill outcome at GCF.

*During the interview, the independent TAP further clarified this issue. All in all*, as per our analysis, advisory services provided to the other farmers include agro-met, clear head, landscape agro-meteorological information, etc., which are not very technical. However, for the project group, the advisory services will be more technical and specific. The project includes the communication of benefits of improved practices, risk management advice during growing season, and general improvement in agricultural extension capacity. These will add value to the other farmer groups.*

**Page 132**

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The overall merit of the proposed project towards advancing GCF’s scarce resources is low. The scores of (a) impact potential; (b) paradigm shift potential and (c) efficiency and effectiveness are not high enough, yet. The independent TAP had no policy guideline to object such a proposal, but it recommended that the Board should endorse this project with conditions.

The independent TAP has little hesitation to inform the Board that the project ideas described in paragraphs #7 through to #10 lack on account of climate understanding and contextualization; for example, paragraphs #8 and #9 lack an analysis on agro-financing and agro-insurance market baseline assessment, and paragraphs #16, #17, #31, #32, #42, and #56 on establishing explicit connections between the project activities and climate change. The TAP also noted that the project exposure and sensitivity to climate-induced hazards and risks could be promoted and the mobilization of additional resources for this project could be more effectively responsive to climate change (rather than placing primary focus on value chain) as well as creating better response options for non-participating farmers; and paragraphs #16, #32, and #56 on strengthening eligibility criteria for selecting farmers and farmer organizations.

7a. In order to minimize the risks, the independent TAP further recommends that any approval of this funding proposal by the Board is made subject to the following conditions precedent to the second disbursement to the AE under the funded activity agreement ("TAA").


* Delivery to the Fund by the AE, in a form and substance satisfactory to the Fund, of a final report on the climate change vulnerability assessment (CCVA) for each of the products that the climate rationale, including trend analyses, on (a) heat stress due to increasing number of hot days; (b) water scarcity and droughts affecting the availability of irrigation water for rice and vegetables in the NTSB; (c) floods in the NTSB; (d) droughts using climate epochs in the NTSB. Such a report should also include an assessment of the potential impacts on yield and production volume need to be ensured for each of the major premium products (vegetables and mangoes). Such a report shall be based on application of validated climate models, including official hydro-meteorological data repositories as well as projection data based on application of validated climate models.


* The delivery by the AE, at an in-depth agri-financial and insurance market baseline assessment including consideration of potential challenges from both the supply side (e.g., lack of access to credit) and demand side (e.g., lack of access to fund).


The delivery to the Fund by the AE, in a form and substance satisfactory to the Fund, of a revised, single eligibility criteria applicable for beneficiaries under both Outcome 1 and (Outcome 2). The revised criteria should be based on the following: smallholder farmer organizations, producer groups, community protected areas, community forests and agricultural cooperatives. Such criteria shall also include an assessment of the potential impact on the eligible smallholder farmers targeted under Outcome 1 (as such beneficiaries are identified in the funding proposal), such that the same farmers and farmer group beneficiaries participating under Outcome 1 are also the eligible and direct beneficiaries participating under Outcome 2.*

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP199)

## Proposal name:
Public-Social-Private Partnerships for Ecologically-Sound Agriculture and Resilient Livelihood in Northern Tonle Sap Basin (PSPPARL)

### Accredited entity:
Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

### Country(ies):
Cambodia

### Project/programme size:
Small

## Impact potential
The AE would like to highlight that the project aims to support 450,000 vulnerable farmers including men, women, and other minority groups in the highly vulnerable Northern Tonle Sap Basin (NTSB) to adopt climate resilient, sustainable and high value agriculture to respond to climate change. The project targets areas where climate change is projected to impact: 25% of the target region’s predominantly agricultural population and ~3% of Cambodia’s total population. Given that the majority of the population in this area lives below the poverty line, the AE confirms that the needs of the most vulnerable in Cambodia (a Least Developed Country) are already adequately addressed.

As per Table 12 (Table 13), around 85 percent of farmers in the target project areas grow rice, predominantly rain-fed, as their main livelihood activity, while around two and five percent grow other crops and livestock respectively. Rice is a staple food for Cambodians and their major livelihood activity. The project targets a transformation from prevailing climate vulnerable, low quality and low value agriculture to climate resilient, sustainable and high value agriculture.

The project design is informed by detailed analysis of observed climate trends and projections in the target region as well as an assessment of existing and potential production systems as discussed in the Funding Proposal as well as the Feasibility Study. This analysis used the best available data and modelling approaches. Due to the characteristics of each system, the project will focus on improving water management practices under increased heat stress, precipitation variability and increased dry periods that present significant risks to farmers.

The AE would like to highlight that the climate change analysis prepared to develop this project has already been reused in two, quite peer-reviewed publications – 1) Journal of Agricultural Meteorology (2013) by A. Béhar et al; and 2) Agricultural and Forest Meteorology (2012) by A. Béhar et al.

## Paradigm shift potential
The AE would like to highlight that the project targets a transformation from climate vulnerable, low quality and low value agriculture to climate resilient, sustainable and high value agriculture in Cambodia through improved climate-agro-met services, agriculture extension services and public-social-private partnerships (PPP). These partnerships will drive in-country capacity building for climate change adaptation. The AE believes this project’s proven cost-effective and strategic investments to increase the sector’s overall climate resilience and sustainability.


---
**Page 135**
---

Based on the ACF’s extensive experience with similar initiatives in Cambodia and in other countries, the project emphasizes six mechanisms of transformation that are considered to be highly scalable and replicable within the NTSB and beyond:

1. Improved agrometeorological advisory services and their outreach capacity (Outcome 1);
2. Business and financial literacy development and climate-resilient and high-value business planning (Outcome 2);
3. Strengthening of a farmer-to-farmer knowledge acquisition mechanism (i.e., FARM) for the most vulnerable smallholder farmers and other local value chain actors with training on climate-smart agriculture using climate-resilient measures, which also promote high-value practices (Outcome 2);
4. Awareness-raising, knowledge management and demonstration of appropriate farming and value-addition techniques and technologies (Outcome 2);
5. Integrated watershed management for improved ecosystems resilience and agricultural productivity (Outcome 3);
6. An enabling environment for enhanced access to agricultural finance, PSPPs, and systematically leveraging private and public investment for climate-resilient agriculture (Outcome 4).

### Sustainable development potential

In addition to its contribution to other SDGs, as outlined by the ITAP, the project also contributes directly to reducing hunger (SDG 2) by promoting resilient and sustainable, high-value agricultural production in the target region. The majority of households in the NTSB are food insecure, with about 20-60% of households in the NTSB will experience moderate to severe food shortages due to crop loss or reduced yields on an annual basis.

### Needs of the recipient

The AE agrees with the ITAP assessment that the NTSB is comprised of some provinces that have higher poverty rates than other regions of Cambodia. In addition, the climate change impact assessment (CCIA) conducted by ACF in 2018 has shown that the number of days with ambient temperature beyond a certain threshold has been increasing, along with an increase in the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events. These changes are attributed to climate change and have significant implications in terms of crop production in the project’s target region highlighting the timeliness and potential importance of the proposed project for the target beneficiaries and Cambodia.

### Country ownership

ITAP assessment as high is acknowledged. The AE confirms that the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (MAFF) and the Ministry of Environment (MoE) have the capacity to work with ACF on this project. It is also confirmed that a functional agrometeorological advisory system will be implemented under the joint leadership of MAFF and the MoE in collaboration with the National Institute of Agricultural Research (NIAR) and the national meteorological service (NMS) in Cambodia. Furthermore, based on FAO experience in building similar systems in other countries, it is confirmed that the AE and the MoE will confirm that with project support a functional agrometeorological advisory system will be established in the target area within the project duration.


---
**Page 136**

# Efficiency and effectiveness

The project has an estimated aggregate Financial Internal Rate of Return (FIRR) of 38.7%, and an estimated average return on equity rate of Return (EURR) of $54.8k. As noted in Annex 1, these estimates were based on the assumption that 60% of the target beneficiaries successfully adopt climate-smart agricultural practices, which is a conservative estimate given the nature of the project, and that these benefits do not go beyond the farm-gate. Furthermore, investment costs related to access to finance, technologies, and knowledge needed by the smallholders to adopt climate-smart agricultural practices were not included in the cost estimates used for this analysis; premium market segments were not included into the crop models used to calculate expected financial returns. The AF notes that these assumptions are conservative and that the EURR would be further highlighted that these estimates of project benefits are conservative.

The AF considers that the feasibility of the uplifter effects on the gradual model promoted by the project is supported by the experience gained with other smallholder groups, civil society organizations and private insurance and finance providers as well as past experiences with similar tiered technical assistance and investment projects in Cambodia and globally.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

The AF acknowledges the ITAP overall assessment and welcomes the recommendation for Board approval.

The AF confirms the following conditions can be addressed prior to second disbursement:
1. Further evaluate the project’s climate change study, clearly providing an evidential base, including trend analysis on: (a) heat stress due to increasing number of days above indicative upper threshold temperatures in the NTSR; (b) floods in the NTSR;
(c) droughts using climate epochs in the NTSR; and
(d) ambient temperature beyond thresholds, indicating where cool chains need to be strengthened.
The AF is committed to work with the GCF to expand the range of technical analysis available to support assessments of climate change impact potential.

2. An in-depth agro-financial and insurance market baseline assessment.


## Goals

1. Gradually increase the number of smallholder farmers, farmer associations, farmer organizations, producer groups, community protected areas, commercial farms, and other stakeholders who are directly or indirectly supported by the project.
2. Gradually increase the number of smallholder farmers who have adopted climate-smart agricultural practices under Outcome 1 (as such beneficiaries are identified in the funding proposal), such that the same farmers and their families who are eligible for direct support under Outcome 1 are also the eligible and direct beneficiaries participating under Outcome 2.

## Introduction

The rapid growth of the Cambodian economy over the past two decades has been accompanied by significant steps towards gender equality in the labor market. The agricultural sector in Cambodia continues to play an important role in supporting economic growth, ensuring equity, securing food security, and promoting the development of the rural economy. The average monthly income per household in Cambodia came from different income sources, of which 91.36% from the primary income from which 58.4% was from wage and salary, 36.4% from self-employment (income of agriculture is included).

Tonle Sap zone has the highest share of agricultural land in 2020. A total of 1,527 thousand hectares in Tonle Sap zone, approximately 19% (385 thousand hectares) was owned by women headed households.

About 64% of the total area of agriculture land in 2019/20 has irrigation facilities. In the wet season, about 27% of agricultural land was irrigated for growing crops and plants.

Cambodian women farmers play a crucial role in the translation of the products of the agricultural sector into food and nutritional security for their households. Women are also central in wholesale and retail marketing activities. They are active participants in the supply chain as both producers and local traders, and the principal retail sellers, working in markets at local, provincial, and national levels. Women are active partners in input supply agro-business, dealing directly with clients, providing information and knowledge on the use of inputs.

Risks of climate change, mechanization, and migration are affecting the agriculture sector in Cambodia significantly, especially with regard to women's labor and contribution to gender roles and relations. The threat of climate change has increased droughts, storms, and floods and has been recognized as a key development challenge. There is evidence that women farmers are more vulnerable than men when compared to their male counterparts. This is due to a number of underlying elements of vulnerability including limited knowledge, and access to climate risk information and resources often due by lower rates of education, access to credit, land ownership and inheritance rights as well as lower levels of access to finance. Women are generally not the main recipients of extension services and have less access to information on potential effects or climate change adaptation and mitigation technologies. They also have limited access to technical assistance for climate change adaptation and mitigation support for climate change adaptation and mitigation activities. Mechanization and introduction of new technologies in agriculture has been slow, and targeted efforts are being made to train women in using various machinery and new technologies. In addition, existing norms result in different roles between men and women farmers using machinery and technologies.

In alignment with long-term vision of the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry, and Fisheries (MAFF), and National Strategy to Promote Gender Equality and Women's empowerment (known as Neave Kattarak - NR-VJ), the Gender Mainstreaming Action Group (GAG) at MAFF promotes gender equality and women’s


---
**Page 142**
---

# empowerment in the sector through its 5 years gender mainstreaming policy and strategic framework.

This policy and strategic framework have been communicated and operated horizontally and vertically across the sector.

The proposed project "Public-Social-Private Partnerships for Ecologically Sound Agriculture and Resilient Livelihood in Northern Tonle Sap Basin (PEARL)" aims to enhance the climate change resilience of smallholder farmers and local communities in the Northern Tonle Sap Basin (NTSB) by increasing their access to climate information, services, and financial resources. The project will support them on their transition to climate-resilient practices, mainly through effective public-social-private partnerships. The project plans to support establishing a gender responsive, landscape-level agroecology monitoring system to create an enabling environment for the development of gender action plan (GAP) at the sub-district level.

To address gender gaps collaboratively and effectively a gender assessment was considered as important to anticipate gaps and identify means to address those gaps. The current assessment is being undertaken to inform the drafting of the gender action plan (GAP) which will be operated across project's stakeholders at different levels in the project.


## Objectives of assessment

The gender assessment has specific objectives as listed below:

a) Identify gender gap, examine gender roles and needs in access to market opportunity, technical services, climate risk information, soil, and financial services

b) Identify gaps in the current project design and implementation for women farmers and assets

c) Identify roles of stakeholders for operation of gender action plan


## Methodology

The assessment was conducted through engagement of different stakeholders including relevant national, sub-national institutions, communities, and private sector actors in project-identified provinces. The question guides were prepared for the assessment and included as an annex 1 of this report. Different methods used for the assessment are outlined below.

### 1. Desk review—project's proposal and feasibility studies, national and sectoral policies and strategies

Relevant aspects from the existing studies, strategies and policies are being incorporated in the report of this gender assessment.


## Key informant interviews

A desktop review was undertaken with various national, sub-national institutions, private sector actors and community representatives to assess their views related to gender gaps in agriculture at sector and within specific scope of the proposed project. Total of 14 in-depth interviews with total of 30 participants (of whom 8 participants are women) were conducted with representatives of: (1). MAEF; (2). Ministry of Environment (MoE); (3). Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology (MoWRM); (4). Ministry of Women's Affairs (MoWA); (5). Agriculture & Rural Development Bank (ARDB); (6). Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO); (7 & 8). Provincial Department of Agriculture, Forestry, and Fisheries (PDFMF) at Siem Reap and Kampong Thom; (9 &


---
**Page 143**
---

10). Provincial Department of Environment (PDE) at Siem Reap and Kampong Thom; (11 & 12). Provincial Department of Women's Affairs (PDOWA) at Siem Reap and Kampong Thom; (13). Echo-farm at Siem Reap; and (14). Preah Vihear Meanchey Union of Agricultural Cooperative (PMUAC). To minimize risk of COVID-19 and travel time, the interviews were conducted virtually and face-to-face. The list of persons met is available in annex 3 of this report.

### Focus Group discussion
A total of 4 Focus Group Discussions (FGDs) were organized at different communities in Siem Reap and Kampong Thom. FGDs were organized with the same arrangements at Siem Reap and Kampong Thom. At Siem Reap, 10 (5 women) committee members and members of Community Protected area (CPA) living on top of Kulen mountain (national park), & Suyu Loe district, Siem Reap province were invited to participate in FGD. A total of 18 participants (11 women), of whom are the committee members and members of Agriculture Cooperative (AC) were invited to participate the FGD organized at Sot Nikum district, Siem Reap province. At Kampong Thom, 12 (4 women) committee members and members of CPA living in Prasat Sombo district were engaged in the FGD, and 19 (12 women) committee members and members of AC are also engaged for FGD at Santuk district. Engagement of these participants was combined between community leaders, smallholder farmers, value chain actors, and producers. The FGDs took between 60 to 90 minutes, organized at open spaces to avoid risk of COVID-19 transmission. The FGDs were moderated by national gender consultant expert and supported by an assistant. All participants were requested consent to discuss on the assigned topics as well as networking.

### Limitations
Due to COVID-19 pandemic, the FGDs in-depth interviews were agreed to be conducted in Siem Reap and Kampong Thom, and one interview was organized virtually with farmer union at Preah Vihear. COVID-19 pandemic also caused some limitations for organizing the face-to-face meetings with key national representatives.

## IV. Findings

### Country context
Cambodia grew at an average rate of 7.6% from 1994–2015 and became a lower middle-income economy in 2015. The agriculture sector contributed about a quarter to annual GDP, was the source of 86.4% (or 3.1 million) of total jobs in 2016. About 46.3% of [5 million] people of rural population still rely on agriculture for employment. Employment creation supplied by the agriculture sector grew at 2.8% driven by agricultural development boosted by rising agricultural commodity prices and cultivated land expansion*. The MAF gender mainstreaming policy and strategic framework (2016-2020) still recognizes


---
**Page 144**
---

that about 82% of the households live in rural areas. A large majority of these households engage in rice based agriculture, fishing, collection of forest products, crops, and livestock production. Agriculture remains central to Cambodian livelihoods of small form holders and women. Many of Cambodian women work on their own land or carry out unpaid agricultural work. There are 2.3 million agriculture households in Cambodia, of which 80% are male headed and 20% are female headed.

Cambodian women farmers increasingly supply local markets with traditional and high-value produce, but compared to men they have less access to inputs, credit, extension services, higher value products, lower levels of literacy (75% compared to 85% for men - figures only available for 2015), less access to training, less access to market information, and less access to productive resources. Lower financial literacy of women than men also limits their ability to take advantage of credit facilities and other financial services that are fair for women. To market their products, women farmers need timely, reliable, and accessible market information. Financial services are also essential so that women smallholder farmers can pay for inputs, improve their livelihoods, develop small business enterprises to empower themselves economically and support their family.

Migration has a strong influence on gender roles and division of labour in rural areas. It can have impacts in the level of participation which women had in agricultural value chain and agro-business activities. The key enabling factor is change is brought by migration of people from rural areas into garment factories, construction work, service industry and others. Rural households are often made up of only the elderly and children with working age adults, predominantly men, migrating outside their community.

From an institutional and applied perspective, MIP has highlighted challenges in agriculture sector. Gender equality issues and women’s empowerment have not been sufficiently addressed to ensure that women and vulnerable groups have not been sufficiently undertaken to regularly inform the implementing roadmap. Although, the master plan and action plan are prepared, insufficient resources remain a main challenge for implementation of the master plan. The master plan is also affected by climate change, conflict related and threat of climate change. Women’s representation at decision making levels remains low compared to men, especially in rural areas with this more pronounced for women of ethnic minority”. As part of efforts to address these challenges suggests strengthening partnership and coordination mechanisms at all levels.

### 2. Policy landscape

Overall, there are development policies supporting and shaping how Cambodia is moving forward in addressing gender gaps and achieving the goal of gender equality. The Cambodian government has demonstrated its commitment to gender equality by enshrining it as a constitutional right between men and women is guaranteed in the 1993 Cambodian Constitution. This commitment is also reinforced by Cambodia’s ratification of the Convention on Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1985, the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights (ICCPR), the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and other international conventions on women's rights at an internationally guaranteed standard with the commitment to address gender issues that links with environment and climate change. One of these commitments was the signing of United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in 1992, the year 1996, the Kyoto Protocol as one of the three years 2003 and the Paris Convention in December 2015 (COP21).

Gender equality is one of the important cross-cutting themes of the government’s socio-economic policy as reflected in the 2001 Land Management Law, Rectangular Strategy Phase IV (R-Phase iv) indicating


---
**Page 145**
---

for Growth, Employment, Equity, and Efficiency Building the Foundation Toward Realizing the Cambodia Vision 2030. Under the framework of its Phase II, the National Strategic Development Plan (NSDP) as well as the 35-year National Program for Sub-national Economic Development (NP-SEND) specify the need to further improve the status of women who are recognized as the backbone of Cambodian society and economy.

The Neary Rattanak (NR) is a national strategic plan developed since 1999. Its implementation is led and coordinated by the Ministry of Women's Affairs (MoWA) which aims to promote gender equality and women's empowerment in all sectors. Currently, NR Phase V (2019-2033) is being implemented by all government and non-government institutions and private sector stakeholders to accelerate and strengthen women's empowerment.

Women in agriculture is a key element in the NR-V which placed under the strategic pillar of women’s economic empowerment. The MoWA also has its specific indicator to support women farmers address the impacts of climate change.


RGC has also developed the Cambodia Climate Change Strategic Plan (ECCSP) 2014-2023 that aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and risks to climate change impacts. Ministry of Women Affairs (MoWA) is a participating ministry to the formation of the CCCSP which is being coordinated by the Ministry of Environment (MDE). The ECCSP 2014-2023 action plan has set one of its objectives to “Reduce sectoral, regional and national greenhouse gas emissions” through the implementation of the Climate Change and FAME framework has further developed a gender-sensitive target/ indicator: "By 2020, 10% of the protected areas, conservation areas, agro-ecosystems and forest ecosystems including mangroves, that have been under a climate change adaptation and mitigation plan, are managed by women-led or women-managed services, particularly to women, elders and children in local communities and indigenous ethnic minority groups". The focus of this target/ indicator is on the supply of essential services, including services related to water resources management, disaster risk reduction, food security, health care, education, nutrition or natural disasters and, in general, services related to livelihoods and the well-being of Cambodians. It emphasizes addressing specific needs of women, local communities, ethnic minorities, and other vulnerable groups.


CCCSP 2014-2023 includes strategies to incorporate women into decision-making on climate change adaptation and mitigation, and natural resources management; increase awareness and relevant capacities on gender and climate change within MoWA and its decentralized offices and other stakeholders; strengthen the capacity of women’s organizations; and deliver targeted interventions for women related to climate change adaptation and mitigation.


As a follow-up to CCCSP, a Gender and Climate Change Strategic Plan (GCCSP) 2014-2023 and a second Gender and Climate Change Action Plan (GCCAP) 2019-2023, and a master plan for gender and climate change adaptation and mitigation in the MDE are being developed. The GCCSP is being led by MoWA. GCCAP 2019-2023 keeps the same six priorities as the 1st GCCSP including: a) integrating gender into climate adaptation and mitigation plans, b) increasing women’s decision-making power at all levels, c) improving access to information on climate change for women, d) strengthening the capacity of women not a national monitoring and evaluation framework on climate change, and e) designing gender-responsive climate change adaptation and mitigation projects. By the end of 2018, a master plan for gender and climate change adaptation and mitigation is being developed by MoWA in collaboration with other sectoral ministry building for women and climate resilience and adaptation, safe water management and hygiene and gender equality.


---
**Page 146**
---

disaster risk reduction during flood and drought season. Kampong Thom province is one of the piloting project’s areas along with three other provinces**1**.

MoWA’s Gender and Climate Change Committee (GCCC) has developed the capacity of relevant institution staff at national and sub-national level on gender mainstreaming in climate change. The national guidelines on gender mainstreaming in investment for adaptation to climate change in five inter-sectors have been prioritized**2**.

Within national policy and strategic guidance, GMAG of MoA has developed and implemented its five-year (2018-2023) gender mainstreaming policy and strategic framework. The five-year plan’s three strategic objectives included: (1). to promote women’s economic empowerment through women’s access to goods and services for agricultural development and markets; (2). to strengthen capacities, resources, and commitment of the government and civil society organizations in promoting gender equality in the agriculture sector; (3). to increase women’s and men’s equal representation and participation in agriculture sector. Currently, GMAG-MAT is developing its updated gender mainstreaming policy and strategic framework (2022-2026).

### Gender gaps and roles at community

#### 3.1 Agricultural cooperatives committees and membership
Results from the gender assessment shows that women’s representation as members in AC is higher than men, approximately 70% women members. Table below shows number of AC members, and female members in Cambodia and the 4 targeted provinces of PEARL project. Table 2 shows the AC’s unions in Cambodia and the unions in four provinces of PEARL project. The data for Kampong Thom province is not available yet. However, the FGD with AC conducted at Sot Nikum district, Siem Reap province reported that the AC has 36 members/households (32% women) 5 committee members and only one male member as president. The female members are mostly involved in cooking and accountants while the male members work to help transport inputs and products inside the community.

At Sankt district, Kampong Thom province, AC has total of 148 households/members (majority is female member but data is not available), 9 committee members of whom 2 of them are female members. This AC is led men and the two women work as accountant and assistant. Once asked the reasons why women do not participate in AC, the female members said that they have no time to attend meetings because they have support and motivation from their family, especially their husband and community members. The law on AC 2013 states that women and men serving as elected representatives are accountable to the membership of the AC for the performance of their duties and rights. However, the FGD which attended the FGDs indicated that they do not feel sufficiently confident and capable to be AC leader considering the lack of capacity and are busy with daily household works and cares while men are considered more capable in terms of leadership roles. The women also mentioned that they are lacking in providing to information, technology, and market. In addition, men seemed to be considered having better knowledge for making decisions.

#### 3.2 Agricultural cooperatives business modality
Different business modalities the two assessed ACs are focusing on. At AC at Sot Nikum district, Siem Reap province is focusing on chicken raising, input supplies,


---
**Page 147**
---

inter and linking. Among 376 member/households, 150 are raising chicken. The business production is only chicken. The AC has invited contractors from both local companies (the chicken grill and Kasiya company). During the FGD, the committee members and members indicated their confidence in having good skill to raise small-scale chickens at individual household.

On average, each member household raise 100 chicks and the members can buy chicks from their AC member living and producing inside the community. All members stated that they are confident and motivated to raise chicken as it is no market concern. The buyer companies always collect hens and eggs. The AC has a total of 250 chickens, which cost 1,600,000 (equal to $4). As mentioned in above, ACs have more female members as they stay home for household works and care and small-scale agriculture activities while men more migrate for labor work. In addition, the committee members indicated that raising chicken can help them to make additional income, and at the same time, they can save some time for farming and other agricultural activities such as rice and home-garden.

Indicated in the discussion, most of members have land for production but their main constraints is the lack of water sources and irrigation support. Most farmers can only produce one crop a year and cannot diversify crops. The committee members noted that they want to extend their business to rice as they see the potential of market and land availability among farmers. They considered the technical support and training needed for higher product price will be given by various stakeholders. The AC has already conducted a feasibility study on rice production, which calculated input and production costs with support of water sources testing and found that rice production is another potential agri-business for their community. One asked about challenges of ACs and its members in facing the business operation. The committee members were quite confident and equally confident as they feel technical supports, if water sources made available. The female members indicated that they might need to work on rice production with men in term of access to technologies and machinery.

This AC shows very strong management structure and operation. Therefore, the AC committee wishes to support their members to access to financial services for extension of their productivity and improving income. The AC is trying to extend their production capacity to better supply the buyer companies. The committee also wishes to increase more members to increase their production capacity to larger scale.

AC at Santuk district, Kengtung Them province focuses on a single modality which is cashew. All FGD's participants stated that all lands for cashews have no land title or ownership certificate while their lands for rice are legal*. All farmers feel unsecured for cashew lands, but they can only continue using the land as long as they pay taxes and fees to the government. The committee members noted that support the AC. The AC has its own land and cashew nuts warehouses, of which the AC spent their own budget for buying the land and building two small warehouses, and one bigger newly built warehouse was funded by a local company. The committee members indicated that they are facing challenges in the lack of capital to start-up their business operation, to collecting/buying cashew nuts from each member/household and sell to the contracted buyer/companies. Without its financial capacity, all

### Community Protected Area

A total of 31 Community Protected Areas (CPAs) are identified in Cambodia, which are divided by area/zones. All these protected areas have a total of 309,463 hectares.

The LGD has a committee with 25 members, all women. Of the 25 members, 18 are female committee members and 7 are male committee members (equivalent to 71%). Once asked during the FGDs, both men and women in Kulen mountain CPAs (national park) stated that their CPA has more male committee members than women. However, women have a greater role of participation in forest activities compared with men living in this area who go into forest for taking forest products in equal opportunity but still more men play the leadership roles given consideration that men are more able to move across the areas, participate in other income generating activities and have less time to spend on household work.

The CPA at Prasat Sambo district, Kampong Thom province has only one female committee member out of six committee members. The CPA is reportedly more than 10km from a community, which causes increased travel time for women compared with men who can easily access the CPA. Women have less time to spend on household work and care. In addition, women and men still consider gender norm has limited women's access to information related to agriculture and opportunity in being a community leader.

The Ministry of Environment’s Proclamation 084 (Article 13) states that women are encouraged to participate in committee vote. Therefore, MOE, PDNE and FDG2 participants indicated that each CPA should encourage women’s participation in CPAs’ committees and attending the forest patrols. Due to gender norms, women have limited time for attending meetings and other activities such as cooking (domestic food preparation), childcare) as well as limited experience / capacity in engagement in decision making function and patrol activities there are lower levels of engagement and opportunities for women within the CPA system. Even though some rangers’ roles may be limited to cooling for other main rangers, Reporting of MOE and PDNE also observed the same, with gender norms still limiting opportunity and access of women.

Both CPAs at Kulen mountain and at Prasat Sambo district have their farming land, of which each member has approximately 1-2 hectares per household. The farms are mostly used for cashew and the farmers indicated that they earn an average income of USD $300-$500 per year after labor costs. As indicated, most of household members produce cashew without using fertilizer or pesticide, partially due to price of these inputs compared to cashew prices. Most of FDG2 participants indicated that price of cashew has decreased in the last 1-2 years and consequently, cashew production output decreased. Almost all members indicated that producing cashew requires less time compared to rice and other crops, but farmers need to use external labor (women and men seasonal migrants,

# Access to agrometeorological services

Climate risk information is frequently shared from provincial department through Vignan to the committees members/leaders and then shared to other members of ACs located by AC in Sot Nium district; while AC in Santuk district, and the two CPA can only access through the frequent announcement of the ministry and its provincial department (through TV, radio, booklet, etc.). In addition to this, the circulation of climate risk information is still limited. The majority of farmers are not able to integrate climate risk information into their daily agricultural production. As indicated by representatives of Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology (MoWRM), climate risk information can be technically forecasted and circulated to more specific areas, but it needs to be done through sub-national staff; especially women to provide responsive agrometeorological services to farmers.

Some members of AC and CPA are poor and vulnerable households, and most of them do not have smart phone to access for information, especially climate risk information, technology, and market price. The majority of female farmers in the two ACs and CPA are unable to get responsive climate services; they need to be "smart" using technology, machineries, and access to responsive climate information. Farmers can access to climate information and market price once they have smart phone and know how to access it.

Although, the climate risk information is often shared to members, female farmers still have lower access to this information and lack of capacity to integrate it into their adaptation and production. Female farmers are more vulnerable than male farmers in terms of accessing climate risk information every day. Since many men migrate for labor intensive works, female farmers experience a greater burden from household and care work, and agricultural production. Some farmers seemed to acknowledge that they did not have enough time to access climate risk information.

# Access to financial services

Based on the AC's internal regulation (AC act of Sot Nium district), each member/household can put more than one share in the amount of 50,000 riel or $12.5. Within this share, each member/household can access AC’s loan with a maximum amount of 200,000 riel or $50 per share. Off this amount, members can also borrow money from the committee to be used as a Micro-Finance Institution (MFI), of which they are required to deposit their collaterals. As indicated in the FGD, most AC’s members access to loan with MFI with different amounts.

As mentioned above, AC at Santuk district is facing their biggest challenge related to financial resources; they have not received any loan from MFI and later investment is cashew wine. During the FGD, the committee and members indicated they need approximately $500,000 to $700,000. AC’s committee members (all men) have tried to reach out to MFI and Agricultural and Rural Development Bank (ARDB) but have not received any response from them.

The contributing constraint factors are considered to lack of collaterals, new start-ups with a large amount of capital and business modality needs to be more convincing. The AC committee are still unclear how they can access loan from MFI.

When asked about individual members in FGDs with AC and CPA, most members have taken loans from MFI to buy their agricultural inputs, household consumable materials, schooling, and cultural

The AC at Kampong Thom district, Kampuchea province, indicated that the majority of farmers still rely on traditional practices to produce cashew nuts, but farmers can afford to buy and use fertilizers and pesticides, but only if they are able to grow cashews without any input cost or financial reason. However, the AC noted that the production capacity for cashews is only about half very low, with an average production of 2,800kg/year per hectare.

According to participants, the current production of cashew is only about half of what the AC could potentially produce. The committee indicated the need for improving their technical skills on cashew and other production lines around cashew (peeling cashews, cooking by machine, and packaging, etc.). The AC has invested in agricultural machines to meet the local demand of end user buyers for cashew products. Although these machines are not as efficient or productive as those used by private sector cashew nuts producers, they were trained by NGO partners on how to use them correctly, and men seemed to spend more time for access to market and financial support with mobility and resources.

However, some female members indicated that they need to improve their access to technology and use of machinery and quality of the cashew nuts to compete with private sector cashew nuts producers. The committee indicated that once financial resources are in place, they will be able to create more jobs for female members as well as for male members who have been trained by the AC.

As mentioned earlier in the report, the AC and their key committee members receive technical training and support from different stakeholders. One of these support is to process cashew fruits to be wine.

The ACs seemed to have different levels of access to skill and technology, based on the delivery mechanism used for training and support. The AC at Kampong Thom district appeared to be functional with an active and productive committee, the regular engagement of its members, and the provision of various support from government provincial departments, NGOs and private sector cashew nuts producers. In contrast, the AC in Kampong Chhnang district has limited technical support is always available and easy-to-access by male and female farmers at their community.

The village volunteers who are local trainer and/or focal persons are well trained and accessible at village-level. At the FGD both men and women acknowledged that, although more women are involved in agricultural activities than men, they have less access to training and support for cashew production due to gender bias and price. This was attributed to gender norms where women are socially expected to take household work and care duties and men are expected to work outside home and have better mobility inside and outside the community.

The CPA's committee and members who are mostly producing cashew tend to rely on traditional methods, using either none or a small amount of fertilizer and/or pesticide. All members living in the two assessed areas produce cashew only and sell cashew nuts to local traders or middlemen.

Respondents indicated that when training is organized in the community, women seem to be more likely to attend than men because they have less mobility outside their homes. Women also have a good knowledge and labor if faster, they have a greater mobility, and are responsible for making decisions. On the other hand, women are considered to attend training only when the man is not available. Household work and care duties are contributing factors that prevent women from attending training sessions organized by the AC. CPA and other farmer members as a disadvantage for producing agricultural products at their full capacity.

3.7 Access to market opportunities. AC of Sri Nakhon district, Siem Reap province has two contracts with buyer companies who are running their business in Sam Riep. The contracts were formed by multiple actors including farmers, NGOs, and CS’s commissioners leaders and the government. The contracts are considered as a strong market linkage between farmers/producers, value chain actors and buyers. As indicated during the FGD, price of chicken (1,600Riel/kg) is set for three months. Producers/farmers and buyers can re-negotiate prices if necessary. In case of contract breach, the AC’s committee has good capacity to coordinate and manage supply and demand side.

All members who attended the FGD stated that they are satisfied with contract condition, price and are motivated to continue and extend their production, if financial services made available for them. AC’s committee also indicated that they will not allow any member to sell their cashew nuts directly related to selling chicken as all MC members can only sell to contracted buyers. In case members are found selling their chicken to non-contracted buyers, those members will be dismissed from the AC. The reason of doing so is because the AC requests for respect contract’s condition and ensure sustainable price for their products.

Access to market opportunity for AC at Samrat district, Kampong Thom seemed to be a concern as all members are still selling their cashew nuts once a year during harvest season and to middlemen and/or local traders who buy in bulk. The reason is that most of the farmers stated that they do not want to rent farming is useless for them as they do not have financial resource to manage buying cashew nuts from each member household to sell in large scale to big buyer companies. As indicated, the big buyer companies are also interested to buy cashew nuts directly from farmers. However, this presents challenges for access to markets until financial resources to manage wholesale and retail are achieved.

Despite facilitation from PDAF and other NGOs, the process of negotiating contract farming remained difficult in Kampot province for cashew and rice. Most of participants stated that producing cashew, they can make profit only when they sell their produce directly to buyers. However, most farmers earn almost nothing earning from it. However, they remained keen to continue the production because they do not want to keep land unused or at least they can earn from it, even is little. Representatives from PDAF and AC committee stated that they are committed to supporting farmers in cashew production. Along with this, they prefer to continue pursuing contracts with company buyers. PDAF also showed its commitment to facilitating contract farming for ACs and encouraged the formation of new ACs to improve the quality and quantity of produce.

Farmers living in CPAL indicated that all of the cashew nuts they produce are sold to local traders or middlemen within their community. The price is often dictated by buyers, or which the farmers seemed to have little power to negotiate the price. At the assessed areas, farmers are not organized as entrepreneur uniformity to try increase their production or market price. As such, their cashew production result in low margin quantity, earning and income each year. While most of the farmers in the areas noted the importance of contract farming for improving their livelihoods, they were reluctant to enter into contract farming. Representatives from MDP and PDEE indicated that institutions aim at creating more jobs for farmers, while ensuring the conservation of natural resources, especially forests. However, there are several challenges including lack of financial resources, lack of market linkage and lack of technical trainings.

3.8. Control over resources and assets. The national constitution and other national laws, guarantee equality between men and women, including on control over resources and assets. National law on land


---
**Page 153**
---

management considers equality between men and women to own their land legally. Once farmers are able to access loans, both wife and husband are required to mutually agree to take the loan and if both of them are present on the loan documents. Results from GBDs showed that women and men can equally own their land and take benefits from it. In CPA areas, women and men can equally benefit from forest products. However, there are structural barriers that may hinder the capacity of women to equally access to forest resources. Women in some communities have limited access to forest resources because they collect forest products almost every day and that they can even collect more when men for whom it is near or close to their home (given example of forest on Kulen mountain, Siem Reap province). On the contrary, less women are able to collect forest products from forests in other areas such as Phnom Penh city, Kampong Cham district, Kampong Thom. As such, some women can only go inside the forest with the presence of a trusted man, considering their concern about safety and security during travel (from village to the forest) and inside the forest.

One asked about control over agriculture land and other assets; both men and women (JAC and CPA) indicated that they have equal rights to own land, agricultural equipment and household assets, considering that both of "their names are in land titles and acknowledged by authorities". However, some female participants who attended GBDs did not know the size of the land indicating that their husband or father had control about this. Some women also mentioned that men were more likely to make equal decision to select crops, buy equipment and household expenses. However, some of the women and men who attended the FGDs indicated that men have better chance to make decision as "they are more" and "more experienced". In addition, some women mentioned that in case of conflict then should be the one making the "right" decision for agricultural products, use and control over resources and other assets.

### 3.9 Sexual Exploitation and Abuse and Sexual Harassment (SEAH) and Gender Based Violence (GBV)

Within consultations it was noted that limited feedback was provided with regard to potential risks of SEAH/GBV linked to project activities. However the assessment notes that these risks exist based on broader trends within the target population and that these may be exacerbated by project activities. An assessment undertaken by the National Institute of Statistics (NIS) denotes 31% of women over the age of 15 in rural areas are victims of sexual violence. In Kampong Cham province, for example, a quarter of men believed there were conditions that justify violence against women. While change is occurring across the project population and has done since these surveys were conducted risks remain, especially with regard to young people who will change the existing dynamics within rural households and communities. These elements will include:

* Risks linked to minor labour inflows and enhanced mobility of actors – due to improved levels of productivity and enhanced value from production systems the project may result in minor influxes of labour and employment opportunities for women, particularly in urban areas. This could have a demand side effect, including the risk for trafficking of women for the purposes of sex work; or the risk of forced early marriage. Furthermore, higher wages for workers in a community can lead to an increase in transactional

19 National Institute of Statistics, Directorate General for Health and ICF International. 2015. Cambodia Demographic and Health Survey 2014. Phnom Penh, Cambodia; Rockville, Maryland: USA. National Institute of Statistics, Directorate General for Health and ICF International. Available at https://dhsprogram.com/pubs/pdf/FR371/FR371.pdf


---
**Page 154**
---

sex. The risk of incidents of sex between laborers and minors, even when it is not transactional, can also increase.

The proposed project also envisages increased agricultural activities on and off the farm, improved productivity of critical food crops and will create short term employment opportunities in the project target areas, and hence mobility of both women and men will be enhanced. In light of the cultural aspects and gender biases, higher mobility may expose women to more risks of GBV.

Risks linked to changes in household labour and income balance - Women hold primary responsibility within target areas for household chores and caregiving, which require much time and energy. They will be added responsibilities as a result of increased agricultural activities on the farm and off the farm, with less time spent in household chores and caregiving, which may well cause dissatisfaction of male members of the household and lead to violence against the women.

Women may also face opposition of their husbands and other male family members for their wish to participate in project related activities. This opposition can escalate to violence if their spouses are not involved in the project, the possibility of violence is higher. Women beneficiaries may be harassed or attacked also by men outside their households, who are not involved in the project and take out their frustrations on women.

If women's income increases, that fact may make men feel insecure and turn violent against the women. If women refuse to give up their earnings to their husbands and other male family members, that is likely to trigger violence against the women.

Risks linked to project personnel and executing agencies - While all project related personnel are engaged in training that they follow all relevant Codes of Conduct, including those on sexual exploitation and abuse, risks exist that female and male beneficiaries may be asked for sex or related favours in exchange for participation in the project or for obtaining agricultural inputs.

Risks linked to changing household dynamics and potential for child labour - Considering that many project activities will require women's participation, there is a risk of children being involved in project activities. FAO experiences show that almost no agricultural intervention is neutral in terms of child labour. The children are affected by the changes in adult responsibilities in the field area and by the changes in household dynamics. This may lead to increased vulnerability for children to be exploited or held in hazardous work.

V. Conclusion and recommendations

The assessment has noted a number of key considerations and potential recommendations based on reviews of existing literature and past project development plans as well as consultations carried out as part of the baseline survey. The following summary sets these out, with those that are included within the gender action plan and main funding proposal. It is also noted that at present many of these interventions remain broad with more specific and targeted interventions to be developed as part of project implementation. These will include strengthening of the capacity of women's groups in target areas as well as through development of key project products (e.g. commodity strategic plan development), many of these elements will be coordinated by the National Gender Specialist with Support from FAO HQ and other consultants as outlined in the funding proposal. Key element of the central approach however include:


---
**Page 155**
---

# Agricultural system specific:

Access to agrometeorological services – there are significant limitations in women’s capacity to access effective agrometeorological information due to a number of factors with main elements of their outlined below:
- Capacity - existing gender disparities (linked to lower access to education and training) as well as access to technology such as mobile phones has limited the capacity of women to fully access and understand agrometeorological information as well as other market information
- Quality – the information provided is often not relevant or useful for women farmers, particularly in terms of their scope and level of details as well as accuracy and as such are not as useful for any farmer, including women, as they are needed to be.
- Coverage – agrometeorological services currently do not cover areas in sufficient detail nor provide information tailored to different production systems with small scale systems, often women led, such as vegetable farming that have high needs for such systems often being poorly catered for.

Recommendations - in the development of the agrometeorological systems it is critical that consideration be given to meeting the different needs with this including both information production and how new that information is disseminated.

These recommendations have been considered and are included within the gender action plan and mainstreamed into the project document.


Access to information and market opportunities – as noted within the agrometeorological system points above and the content of this report, women face a number of challenges linked to gender norms and systemic gender issues. A number of these key issues are noted below along with recommendations on steps to address them and links with how these are considered within the gender action plan and main project document.

- Access to information – women often have more limited access to information due to a number of reasons that are both due to structural inequalities (e.g. lower access to education) as well as gender norms (e.g. limited roles in decision making bodies as well as lower access to technology).
- Mobility - across at minimum lower levels of mobility with women farmers being less engaged (with PA patrols) was noted as a barrier to addressing a number of gender gaps.
- Gender norms within family – it was widely noted that women continue to fall into full or many of the domestic roles and responsibilities, often taking up time which they could undertake other activities linked to agricultural development of leadership roles.
- Access to finance - access to finance was also noted as a challenge across genders with the specific gendered nature of financial services being noted. Many enterprises were “new” to the market or with which financial service providers had less experience and were less well adapted to service.

Recommendations – Addressing these challenges requires a combination of elements with the overall approach needing to target a wholistic approach to addressing gender disparities. Key elements of this include:


---
**Page 156**
---

- Improved access to training – it is critical that female headed households and other relevant women gain access to training through the project with elements of this being women focused and tailored to their needs.
- Awareness raising and training on gender issues – it is clear that there is broad action to support enhanced gender awareness across key project stakeholders to enhance understanding of the differences in roles, responsibilities, and opportunities for men and women.
- Mainstreaming of gender based approaches into key sector and business plans as well as all elements of project implementation - building on the above elements gender elements but be mainstreamed into all aspects of project implementation including access to credit, inputs and financial services products to ensure that these are able to both meet the needs of and help address disparities between genders.
- Development of specific awareness raising across the project – all project products from information and awareness raising materials to specific technical products should be gender aware to support improved access to them and that they are able to functionally support reductions in gender disparities.

Cross cutting
Effective quantification of the impacts of the project and changes in agricultural systems for women - the project is seeking to demonstrate tangible improvements in the lives of women with many women within the farming community facing additional challenges linked to gender norms, including demands linked to unpaid domestic work at household level as well as restrictions on mobility and access to information on improved agricultural techniques.

- Recommendations: Adoption of W4 approach – the W4 standard is the first women-specific standard that measures women's empowerment in a transparent and quantifiable manner, gives a monetary value to women's empowerment, and provides a framework for measuring women's empowerment. It should thus provide the dual benefit of providing both a mechanism for the project to monitor impact and a means by which beneficiaries can gain increased market access as well as direct financial support.
- The potential use this approach has been noted within Sub-activity 2.1.2.4: Explore the possibility of adopting and operationalizing W4 Standards to empower female farmers, particularly in the vegetable sector.

Violence against women (VAW), and Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) – these elements were not included as part of the project baseline data collection but remain a risk given the prevalence of violence against women in rural areas (with 33% of women reporting experiencing violence) as well as the changing power and operational dynamics that project interventions will bring [as noted in Section 3.9 of the above assessment] and as such these risks must be addressed within the project implementation.

11 National Institute of Statistics, Directorate General for Health, and ICF International, 2015. Cambodia Demographic and Health Survey 2014. Phnom Penh, Cambodia; and Rockville, Maryland, USA: National Institute of Statistics, Directorate General for Health; and ICF International. Available at https://dhsprogram.com/pubs/pdf/FR311/FR311.pdf


---
**Page 157**
---

- Recommendations – the project must ensure that there are appropriate measures in place to raise awareness and understanding of VAW and SEAR and support education to reduce its risk. This must also be done through strengthening an effective grievance and redress mechanism.
- Grievance and redress (GRMs) – detailed information on the functioning of existing GRMs was not available as part of the assessment.
- Recommendations – as noted within the ESFM the project will utilize FAO's Co national GRM that will be under implementation when project starts. This mechanism will be further strengthened and supported by the MoFA, prisons and through expertise of the National Gender Expert also cater for grievances based on VAW and SEAR.
- Sustainability – the sustainability of gender based interventions is critical with impacts needed to continue well beyond the project lifetime.
- Recommendations – the current project has sustainability built into its design using a model of public-private partnerships (PPPs) as a means to drive change, with actions to develop business plans and strategies that are then supported by improved access to finance, agrometeorological information and high value markets. These objectives are fully in line with key agricultural strategies and plans within Cambodia's National Strategic Plan for Agriculture and Rural Development (NSPARD), the Fisheries Sector 2016-2020 (CCFAP-AFF) and Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC). From a gender perspective the project also aligns with the Neary Rattana (NR) Phase V, as well as the Gender and Climate Action Plan (GCAP) 2019–2023, as well as the master plan for gender and climate change (2018-2030) that have been prepared and used as a roadmap for formulating the projects and programs by MoFA.
Based on these recommendations the following action areas were developed as part of the gender action plan with further information being provided in Annex 1 of the current document.

# Annexes

## Annex 1: Data collection tool

### Questions for farmers

1. Is agriculture your main household income/employment?
   a. Yes, why?
   b. If no, what else do you live for?

2. Are you satisfied with your agriculture production?
   a. Yes, why?
   b. If yes, why?

3. Do you think you can access to skill and technology for higher quality of product and higher prices?
   a. If yes, how can you access?
   b. If no, why?
   c. If yes, what do you need to be done for you to improve your product?

4. Do you think you can access or have good market opportunity for your product?
   a. If yes, how can you access?
   b. If no, why?

5. Do you know about climate risk information?
   a. If yes, what are you aware of or can access within your village the climate risk information?
   b. If yes, what is it? And who share it with you?
   c. If yes, how often you need it? And what is the best way to make you fully aware of it?

6. Once you want to extend your agriculture activities and productivities, do you access to credit?
   a. If yes, with whom?
   b. Do you think you can fully or easily access for it? If no, why?

7. At present, how often man and woman make equal decision to use and control over resources and/or assets?
   a. If yes, how is the decision made?
   b. If not, please give an example.

### Specific questions for value chain actors: (questions for farmers will be used here too)

1. What are you doing in value chain?
2. Tell us, how do you access to skill, market info., technology, local network, credit?
3. Do you have any constraints?
   a. If yes, what is it? Why?
   b. If no, how can you make it smoothly?
4. Do you have enough support to improve your value chain? Why and why not?
   a. If yes, who do you need? And how?
   b. If no, who supported you? And how?
